## Chapter I

The first time I saw him he was standing there near the high, narrow gate in front of the employment office. The top buttons of his Mackinaw were open and the fine rain slanted down into his face and he acted as if he didn’t mind getting his chest wet at all. Kidding the guard, he was, in a way that was sort of confidential and yet half amusement. He had a crooked smile around one side of his mouth that you couldn’t hardly tell what it meant, and quiet, black eyes that didn’t seem to notice anything special and yet took in everything. The minute I saw him I said to myself, “That guy’s never been in the inside of an automobile factory.”

There were thirty or forty fellows lined up, trying to get in, but he wasn’t standing in line with the others.

The guard filled the whole narrow gate with his shoulders. In his hand he had a piece of black hose that he kept slapping against the side of his leg.

“You guys will have to get in one line,” he said, “or they won’t none of you fellows get in.”

Then I saw what had happened. There were two lines: one of colored fellows and one of whites and neither line would give up its place to the other. It made a fine chance for an argument in a place where arguments are plenty easy to start.

I went up to the guard.

“They hiring anybody?” I said.

“Yesterday we put on a few. Got a paper?”

“No.”

“Had a paper I might let you in. All these fellows ain’t got no papers either.”

I knew what he meant. It was a paper showing that you had bought a new car. It had to be a Holt car, of course. And if it showed that you had bought the car from Mr. Holt’s cousin, who was a Holt dealer, so much the better. That way it sort of evens things up. You buy the car and Mr. Holt gives you a job. It’s like Mr. Holt says—“What is good for the manufacturer is good for the worker.” But if you can’t make the down payment, the system don’t work.

Across the road was a big parking field full of cars. Ninety acres of cars. They were all Holts. And if you came to work driving some other kind of car, the parking men were liable to not let you in. You had to park on the other side of the railroad tracks where they charged you ten cents a day. It was a long ways to walk. You almost had to take the street car back to the main gate.

I talked some more with the guard.

“If you was to let me in,” I said, “they would give me a job. I hear they are starting work on the new model. I’ve got a good record in there. I’ve put in five years in motor assembly. I’m a helper. Ain’t a job on the line I can’t handle.”

“How’s come,” he said, “you ain’t got a badge?”

“I turned it in to get the deposit,” I said. “I got down pretty low. Five bucks is big dough when you’re down to doughnuts and coffee.”

It was no good. He told me to line up with the others. But I didn’t; I went over to where the big fellow stood. He spoke to me.

“Russ’s my name,” he said. “What’s yours?”

“Mine’s Bennie,” I said.

“I heard you tell him,” he said, “that you’ve worked here before. You ought to know the ropes.”

“Sure,” I said. “Only—there’s nothing to it. Main thing is to get in. You’ve got a good chance of being hired if you can get through the gate. They don’t ask many questions. Give you a physical examination. They want to see if you’re healthy. A good workman ought to be healthy. You look healthy.”

“I’m healthy, all right, but I’ve got no trade. I don’t know what the inside of an automobile factory looks like.”

“You don’t have to have a trade,” I said. “If a man’s got a trade he’s liable to have a lot of wrong ideas. They’ve got their own ways for doing things in there. If a man’s got a trade, he’s liable to think his own ways are the best.”

“Maybe they are—for him.”

“Maybe,” I said. “And then again—maybe not. It depends on how you look at it. Getting the job done—that’s what counts.”

He made a motion and we walked a few steps away from the gate so the guard would not hear us.

“Listen to me,” he said. “The main thing is to get in, ain’t it? I’ve been hanging around here five hours. When I got here this morning there were already six colored fellows standing in line and that big mouser told me to line up behind ’em. ‘Listen, buddy,’ I said to him, ‘I’m not used to lining up behind niggers.’ So I started a line of my own. Some Arkansawyers come along and I had ’em fall in behind me and then some more fellows came and I stepped out. Now there’s two lines—one white and one niggers.”

“Yeh,” I said, “what about it? If a call comes for a bunch to go in there’s liable to be an argument.”

“That’s it,” he said. “That’s the way I figured. And when the argument starts—the big fellow will get scrambled up in the fight and we walk in.”

“That fellow’s husky,” I said. “He’s an ex-prize fighter. When he starts swinging that piece of hose somebody’ll get hurt plenty.”

“Don’t worry. When I walk in, you follow.”

“Sounds easy,” I said.

“It is easy,” he said. “He’s going to let in a fellow first from one line and then from the other. You know what’ll happen, don’t you?”

“What?”

“Somebody’s liable to stick out his foot and trip up a nigger. Somebody’ll be standing there—maybe me—right next to the gate, looking innocent. That’ll start it. He’ll begin swinging that piece of hose and then somebody’ll reach over and plant a haymaker right on the side of his jaw. That’s when you follow me in.”

I don’t like that way of getting a job but it’s like Mr. Holt says in his book—“There is something sacred about a man’s job.” And I didn’t have any.

It was noon and the guard and the fellows standing in line began eating their lunches. Russ and I didn’t have anything to eat so we walked down the line a half mile to where there was a lunch cart near the main gate. Walking along he told me a few things about himself. He’d come down from upper Wisconsin and all his life he’d trapped and worked in the lumber camps. And now he’d come down to Detroit to work in Mr. Holt’s factory because he’d heard the wages were good and his idea was to save money and go into business and the business he wanted to go into was clamming. Clamming, he said, was the most independent life that any man could get into. And there was a chance to make money because the shells were worth fifty dollars a ton and you sold the meats to the farmers to feed to their pigs. And then there was always a chance of finding a pearl. But the thing that appealed to him was that you were your own boss. If you wanted to go to work at four o’clock in the morning before the fog lifted off the water, you could. Or, if you felt like laying around in camp all day and taking it easy, there was no one to tell you you couldn’t. He’d done a little of everything. In the lumber woods he had run a donkey engine, where, he said, he’d got to be pretty slick with the levers, One summer he’d been an engineer on a steam thresher. But a clammer is what he wanted to be and he’d come down to Detroit to earn money to buy an outboard motor, a boat, and an old automobile to haul his stuff around in. He’d never worked in a factory and when I said something about “the line” he didn’t know what I meant.

I tried to give him a little advice.

“When you get in there,” I said, “they’ll give you a job. Mostly, you take what you get. If it’s production, it’s doing the same thing over and over all day, like putting in a screw or a gasket. The stuff you work on moves past you on a track, or a belt. You’ve got so many seconds. There’s lots of different lines,” I said, “and they’re all fast, but some are faster than others. There’s ‘blue mass’—that’s final assembly—and the motor line, for instance. They’re fast. I worked on the motor assembly line. I was a helper. I helped to break in new men and stood in for fellows when they wanted to go to the toilets. A helper has got to be an all-around good man. But there’re other jobs.”

“What are they?”

“Well, they’s sweepers.”

“Yeh, sweepers. Fellows that go around and sweep up the floors.”

“I don’t wanna sweep floors. I can’t see myself going around with a broom and a dustpan picking up other fellows’ dirt.”

“It pays the same money,” I said. “It’s not line work and the bosses don’t push you. You saw those fellows lined up. About half the white fellows are farmers. The other half are auto workers. You can tell the auto workers. No overcoats, most of ’em; patent leather shoes with cracks in ’em, and no rubbers. They’ve been in there before. About half of ’em will try to get on as sweepers. If they can’t make it the first time, they’ll go out, line up and try it again. Some of them won’t have a job on the line. Some of ’em can’t take it. They get nervous and first thing you know, they’re all tied up in a knot. I’ve seen it happen. When the bell rings there’ll be a kind of dumb look on their faces and they’ll start walking off in the wrong direction. It’ll be a minute or two before they’ll come to.”

He laughed.

“Listen, partner,” he said, “you don’t have to worry about me. I want a job that’ll give me a chance to show what I’m worth. In the woods—I’ve always been able to hold up my end along with the rest of ’em.”

“That’s me, too,” I said. “I’m trying to work my way up. Some day I’m gonna be a straw boss in motor assembly. But right now, I’d take any job they got for me.”

We got us something to eat—a bottle of milk each and a paper carton of beans dished out with a scoop, and while eating we kidded the girl. I had seen her before. I had tried to make a date with her once but never got to first base. She was good-looking, in a cute sort of way. Lots of black hair and snappy eyes. A boy’s cap made her look smart and sassy. She made fun of Russ’ boots and the way he had his pants tucked into them and when we turned in our milk bottles and were ready to leave, she said:

“All alone in the big city?”

“How did you guess it?” he asked.

“Oh-—I been around some,” she said. “Been places and seen things and stuff.”

“And stuff, mostly, I guess. Where I come from,” he said, “kids like you are still taking things outa bottles.”

“They do here, too,” she said, “if you get what I mean.”

“Tough, eh?”

“No—just dynamic. That’s our slogan, you know. You’ll see it if you keep your eyes open. ‘Dynamic Deroit.’”

“What’s it mean,” he asked, “dynamic?”

“Oh-—I don’t know. I guess maybe it means we go places and do things.”

She gave him a card and he looked at it. On it there was the name and address of a taxi-dance place over a store on Woodward Avenue.

“Come up and see me sometime, big boy,” she said. “I’ll show you how to do the Big Apple. Ask for Rita.”

He bent the card between his thumb and forefinger and snapped it out into the street.

“I don’t like to climb stairs,” he said. It was easy to see he could be a heller with women if he wanted to.

We started back but before we got there the rumpus began and we made it back to the gate in just about nothing flat. The whites and niggers were piling into each other. Half the fellows were down, the others on top. It was just a heap with arms and legs sticking out—pounding and kicking. But the guard was not in it. He had his back to the gate and reached out with his hose whenever he got a chance to get in a crack. This big fellow—Russ—was itching to get in a poke at the guard.

Then a colored fellow crawled out of the heap and got a clout with the hose that made him let out a bellow. He made a dive for the guard, wrapped his arms around the guard’s legs, and they were down in the heap. A farmer with a bloody nose broke loose from the pile, ran to the emergency alarm on the curb and turned in a riot call.

“Come on—” said Russ; “now or never. They’ll be here in a minute.”

We ducked through the gate, ran through the narrow lane between the steel fences, and stepped into the office. There were thirty or forty fellows in the bull pen, sitting on benches. The clerks were still out to lunch. We took seats and in a minute we heard the sirens. Some of the fellows wondered what the excitement was all about and tried to look out of the high windows.

After ten minutes, or so, the clerks began drifting back into the office. One of them went into his cage and came out with a piece of paper.

“Any of you fellows here ever run a manipulator?” he asked.

Not a man answered.

“What’s a manipulator?” Russ whispered.

“A kind of derrick,” I said. “Used for handling hunks of hot steel.”

“Let’s grab it,” he said.

“Wait a minute—” I warned him. “You don’t know what you’re getting into.”

“If it’s a machine,” he said, “I can run it.”

The clerk looked the men over. He noticed Russ.

“Anybody here,” he said, “that’s had experience running electric cranes?”

“That manipulator job—” said Russ, “I’ll take it. I’d like this man here to swamp for me.”

“Ever run one?” the clerk asked.

“No, but I’ve run donkey engines and loaders. I’m a good learner. It won’t take me long to get what I don’t know.”

The fellow scratched his head for a minute.

“All right,” he said. “We’ll give you a try. Where’s your helper?”

“Right here.” And he pointed to me.

I had a job. It all happened so quick I didn’t know whether to be glad or not. A colored fellow sitting next to me said they were hiring men for the foundry. Work there ain’t what it’s cracked up to be. I decided to stick with the big fellow. If I didn’t like it I could put in later for a transfer.

We went up and filled out our cards. Then we sat down again and waited for a bunch of other fellows that were going up to the plant hospital for examinations.

“What’s the matter?” said Russ. “You look disgusted. You think I stuck my foot into something, don’t you?”

“Not your foot,” I said. “Your neck. You got any sort of idea the kind of job you grabbed off for us?”

“No. You?”

“No. All I know—I’ve seen it a couple of times on my way through the drop forge. They use it handling big steel. It’s so complicated they have to get a factory man on the job every time it breaks down.”

“Swell,” he said. “We’re going to have fun. We’re going to learn something.”

“If we don’t get fired.”

“We didn’t pay anything to get the job. Why not try something that’s interesting? Those line jobs don’t sound good to me. If they fire us—all we’ve lost is a job that didn’t cost us anything.”

“But that outfit’s complicated,” I said. “More gears and levers than you ever saw. Got an iron claw that picks up ten-ton billets of steel and sticks em into the furnaces. Pulls ’em out when they get hot and holds ’em under steam hammers as high as a two-story building. I’d be scared to climb up on it. Suppose the thing got out of control. Then what?”

“They give a man a chance to learn, don’t they? They don’t expect him to get up there and run it the first day, do they?”

“Oh, sure. They let you learn. Even if you could run it, they wouldn’t let you, the first day. They’ve got their own way of doing things.”

“Q-keh,” he said. “That’s all that matters. I don’t know yet what kind of outfit it is. All I know is, if it’s meant to be run, we’ll run it. All a man needs on any job is brains and a fair chance to learn. We’ve got the brains. If we didn’t . . . we’d still be out there in the street.”

“Or in the cooler,” I said.

I had to admire his nerve. He had a lot of respect for himself—more than most of the fellows I know that have worked in the Holt plant a long time.

## Chapter II

Early the next morning I was in the drop forge, near the time clock, waiting for Russ. The night shift was still on and the heavy production machinery was running full blast. Rows of furnaces were shooting out flames and smoke around the door; hammers and presses were pounding out front axles, gear blanks and such. The floor of the building covered five or six acres and the ground trembled and shook.

Over in a far corner of the building the top of the manipulator loomed up through the smoke like a battleship’s turret, the big boom, or arm, sticking out like a ship’s gun. There everything was still dark and silent. No production there—no working on small parts for the car—no night shift. Our work would be during the hours of daylight with big machines getting out big dies used in forming fenders and body panels, forging out pieces of steel too heavy to handle by hand. I knew that much already.

Russ came in and we went up to the machine. It was as big as a locomotive, and higher—mounted on a carriage that ran over steel rails, wide apart, laid in the concrete floor. On the carriage were other rails that ran crosswise and the superstructure ran on these so that the machine could run in four directions. From the upper part of the machine a big arm, or boom, projected that turned with the superstructure. The arm could be raised or lowered, shortened or made longer, turned or pivoted in any direction and on the end there was a claw with two curved iron fingers big enough to go around a bass drum. Steel ladders ran up the sides of the machine and from where we stood we could see the tops of two rows of brass control levers. I think the machine weighed sixty tons.

“That’s her,” I said. “Still want to run it?”

“Oh, sure,” he said. “She’s a honey. I think we’re gonna get along swell. There’s room here, room to work.”

At the end of the long floor were piles of cold steel, all sizes and shapes. Along one side were the furnaces, some of them under slow heat, grumbling and purring. On the other side were the heavy presses and hammers reaching up into the smoky heights of the building. Over-head, forty-fifty feet above the floor, a traveling bridge crane rumbled back and forth, carrying bundles of steel in cable slings to the production machines. The operator stuck his head out of the steel cage as he rode past and looked us over.

The name of the day superintendent was Riley and we went up to his desk and the place swarmed with timekeeps, foremen, and clerks, and everybody was talking loud to make himself heard above the noise of the machines, waving papers and getting tangled up in telephone cords like they always do in Mr. Holt’s factory between shifts. A timekeep gave us our cards and our badges and told us where to punch in and then let out a yell for Johnson, our straw boss. Johnson came up and he was short and the skin on his face was like leather. He had no eyebrows and he looked at us a minute with smoke-colored eyes and said:

“Gotcha cards?”

“Yeh.”

“Gotcha badges?”

“Yeh.”

“Follow me.”

The three of us walked over to the floor where the machine stood and here Johnson had a little desk on high legs, but no stool. A lot of blue prints were stuck in a wall cabinet and he pulled out one and began looking at it and the skin on his forehead wrinkled as though it hurt him to think. Russ had started walking off towards the machine when Johnson raised up and said:

“Hey! Stick around here, will you?”

He studied the blue prints again and I motioned to Russ to take it easy and wait and we stood there watching the men drifting in. And then the bell rang and a couple of fellows started oiling the machines; the steam was turned on in the hammers and the big pistons began warming up—sliding up and down in long easy strokes, not quite touching the anvils below. Some little colored lights over the furnaces changed from green to orange and then to red; the flames back-fired a couple of times and then settled down to a steady roar.

Johnson raised up again.

“I don’t know if I tol-ya,” he said, “but I don’t put up with no smoking; no sitting down and no standing with hands in your pockets. It looks bad for fellas that’s on production; it’s against the rules, and besides—I don’t stand for it.”

“Well—you’ll notice,” said Russ, “we didn’t bring any cigars and armchairs.”

Johnson’s eyes snapped open.

“And anodda t’ing,” he said; “I don’t stand for no loafing in toilets. Between heats—when there’s no steel to work—I expect you fellas to keep your machine clean.”

He called for one of the fellows working on the machine to come over. The man wasn’t dressed like a Holt worker. He had on a good suit with a pair of coveralls over it and he had a temporary badge.

“Barney, here,” said Johnson, “is from the factory. He’ll break you fellas in. Ride around with him,” he said to Russ. “Keep your eyes open and don’t be afraid to ask questions. I don’t expect you’ll try to handle no jobs the first coupla weeks.”

“And this man,” he said, pointing to me, “will take Hank’s place. Introjuice ’em togedda and let Hank show him the work.”

“Q-keh,” said Barney. “And what’s the schedule?”

“Load five and six. I’ll mark some steel pretty soon. An ’en we’ll pull number three and work it up under number one hammer.”

The three of us went to the machine. I got introduced to Hank.

“If I was you,” said Hank, “I’d go to the tool crib and get me some rags. We’ll swipe off some of this grease.”

I could see that he wasn’t a smart nigger like some that’s been in the factory a long time, drawing big wages and working alongside of whites.

After a while Barney finished his oiling, threw in the master switch on the wall, and then he and Russ climbed up on the rig and they rolled down the wide tracks to the end of the bay. In a minute or two they came back, electric horn blowing, and in the big claw sticking out ahead of the rig, there was a billet of cold steel that weighed maybe four-five tons. They stopped in front of number five furnace; Barney maneuvered into position; aimed for the door and gave Hank a signal. Hank cut off the air; pressed a button and the heavy door of the furnace slid up towards the ceiling. Barney’s hands fairly played a tune on the levers. The long arm reached in with its load—the claw opened and the steel was laid on the furnace floor. The arm retracted and as Hank pressed another button that closed the door, the flames curled and twisted around the steel like snakes getting ready to swallow a gopher.

“Always cut off the air befo’ opening the furnace,” said Hank. “If you was to forget—the flames might shoot out and singe the hair off yo buddy. I sho don’t like this job. Half the time I’m trying to remember if I forgot to do something I should—the rest of the time I’m worried les’ I did something I shouldn’t. I’ll be mighty glad when you gets this job learnt and I can go back to the foundry. There I knock sand out of molds. Don’t have to wrack and ruin my brains about nothin’.”

There wasn’t much to his job. Opening and closing the doors; watching to see that the track was kept clear; cutting out the switch in emergencies, and a few things like that.

Barney and Russ loaded the furnaces and then they rolled up to number one and got ready to do some forging. I had an idea what it was like, having watched the machines work before. It was one of those jobs like tapping a blast furnace, or discharging a coke oven, that was worth watching; worth stopping a minute to see, even though the bell had rung and you were in a hurry to get home. The biggest steam hammer in the factory was there—a pair of shears that had a bite of a thousand tons. What they did to the big billets was liable to give you an idea that steel is about as tough and hard as new cheese, which is all wrong, even when it’s been in the fire a long time and is up to white heat.

Barney pulled the hot billet of steel out of the furnace, got a firm hold, and swung the boom around in front of the hammer. There were experts on the job, now. Johnson, who directed the work and made all signals; the hammer man, who controlled the force of the giant blows; and Barney, who ran the manipulator. His job was to get the steel up on the anvil; to turn it and tip it—to hold it in position while the hammer got in its work.

Johnson signalled to get the steel up on the anvil. The hammer got into motion. Not striking, yet—but getting ready. Warming up for the signal. That up-and-down motion of the hammer between the smooth slides, that oily flow of movement, gave you an idea of what steam could do: of the power that was held in reserve behind the long piston, of the kind of blows that would come when Johnson gave the signal and the hammer man came down hard on the throttle.

The hammer stopped, poised to strike, and the job was all Barney’s. The white hot billet had to be just so on the anvil—had to be just right for the hammer. A slight turn—a little to the right—a little back, maybe. Slight movements, these were, that had to be done quickly. No time to fool around, no time to waste, because the steel was all the time losing heat. Five tons of hot steel is not like a piece of cold stuff that you can come up to and sit on; it’s something alive, something vicious, waiting for a chance to strike you, if you lose control.

Barney was clever, all right. He knew the job, knew the machine, knew the signals. And the last was not the least part of the business. Johnson made them all with his right thumb; mysterious little jerks and twitches—jabs over his shoulder—past his ear—downwards. The brass levers snapped back and forth, switches flashed, circuit breakers popped out and slammed back into place, rheostats smoked. I wondered what Russ was thinking about standing up there next to Barney. Plenty—I guessed.

The steel was battered and pounded; was dropped to the floor; turned over. The iron claw grabbed a new hold and slammed it back on the anvil for more. The hammer crashed down in full strokes. Fire sprayed out in a circular shower and rained to the floor. The ground shook. Not light shakes—heavy and deep—transmitted through a foundation of solid concrete, sixty feet down.

They were unable to finish the work in one heat and the steel was put back into the furnace. Afterwards, Russ and Barney came down for a drink. Their shirts were soaked.

“How’s she going?” I asked Russ.

“Fine and dandy,” he said.

“Be a long two weeks for you,” I said. “Riding around up there, doing nothing.”

“Don’t worry,” he said. “In a week I’ll be runnin’ the thing. See if I won’t. Only thing hard to learn is the signals.”

“Johnson’s?”

“Yeh.”

“What’s hard about them?”

“They’re not plain. You’d think it was some kind of secret code. That he didn’t want the man on the rig to catch on.”

“Why don’t you tell him?”

“No use. Can’t educate him none. He ain’t the type. He’s the kind that never lets you forget he’s the big boss.”

The bell rang and we stopped for lunch, and while eating, Russ asked Barney a lot of questions about the job and the rig. About back-lash and over-loads; dynamic brakes and magnetic clutches. Barney answered the questions, but not with any great show of enthusiasm. He was a traveling mechanic for the company that made the machine and he’d been around quite a bit and he was full of talk about the towns he’d been in. But mostly, he was interested in women and when Russ asked a question, he answered short and then swung the talk around to girls he’d met in Pittsburgh, Fort Worth, or Birmingham. His company paid him twelve dollars a day and all traveling expenses and that was argument enough to convince him that he could afford to be independent.

He was telling about a girl in Cincinnati who never warmed up right satisfactory unless she was riding around on a steamboat with a band playing, when Russ interrupted again.

“If one of the rheostats burned out,” he asked, “how would you go about to fix it?”

“Now listen,” said Barney, “it’s lunch time. It’s time off. They’re not paying you now for asking questions. Let’s wait till the bell rings.”

“I know, but if you don’t mind —”

“All you have to learn is how to punch those levers. If the rheostat breaks down—they’ll get the electricians to fix it. If you get drunk and bust up the rig—like the other fellow did—they’ll send for me.” .

“I savvy. But just the same, I want to know.”

“All right, then. You’ve got two weeks to learn. You don’t need two weeks. In two days I could turn the job over to you—if I wanted to. You’re the kind that learns quick. But what’s the use? Make the thing look easy, and they’ll figure anybody can run it. Then how you gonna hold ’em up for a raise? Tell me that.”

“I hadn’t thought about that.”

“Hadn’t thought about it?”

“Not yet.”

“Well, now’s the time to be thinking about it. Look at the job you’ll be handling! Look at the responsibility! Ruin one of these blocks and it’s five hundred bucks. Wreck the machine, and it’s thousands. Kill a guy—and you’re washed up. They’ve taken the seat off the rig so you can’t sit. They won’t let you smoke a cigarette to steady your nerves. And what do you get? Five bucks. Five lousy bucks. They’s sweepers here in this outfit gets more’n that.”

“The time to think about all those things,” said Russ, “fs later. Right now I’m trying to get the job learnt.”

The bell rang and we went back to work.

## Chapter III

At the end of the week Russ took over the controls. Barney rode around on the machine a couple of days doing nothing; hung around on the floor a couple more, and then left for Cleveland. By the end of the third week Russ was pretty good—good enough to coast along on the job and take it easy, if he’d wanted to. But that wasn’t his idea. He kept learning—kept digging into the machine to see what made the wheels go around—kept trying to work up more speed—more speed, all the time. When he didn’t get a job done and it had to go back in the fire he took it as a personal grief and felt bad about it the whole day.

We’d moved into the same boarding house and one night, up in the room while fiddling around with his guitar but not really playing, he got an idea.

“Listen,” he said, “a pianist doesn’t stop to look for the keys when he’s playing. What’s to hinder a man learning to run that rig the same way? Learning to work the levers without looking at them? There’s only a dozen.”

“What’s your idea?” I said. “Take a little nap now and then?”

“No. Keep my eyes on the steel. On Johnson. Wouldn’t have to stop to look for the controls, for positions.”

“Could be done, I guess. Only thing is —”

“Sure it could be done. Dog-gone —”

“Only thing is there’s a slight difference between playing a piano and running that rig. When the pianist hits the wrong key there’s a sour note but no one gets hurt. You hit the wrong control—you may accidentally drop a hunk of steel on somebody’s head.”

What I said didn’t discourage him none. The idea stuck. He hauled a couple of two-by-fours up in the room and rigged up a contraption with two rows of sticks the size and shape of the levers. They were spaced accurately and like the controls on the rig could be moved back and forth into various positions. Blindfolded, he practiced night after night. That was all right with me, except that I lost some sleep. But when he started practicing on the rig, I watched my step and gave him plenty of clearance. It was hard enough to figure why a smooth hunk of steel the size of a cow didn’t slip out of the claw under normal conditions. With him looking off into space and paying no attention to what lever he struck, I gave it up. However, no one got really killed and the building wasn’t wrecked and things went along as before, except that not so many jobs were going back into the fire. But still there were some and these continued to bother him something fierce.

“Where we’re losing motion,” he said to me one night, walking back and forth between the door and his bed, “is in the re-heats. Think of the time we’d be saving if we could finish every job in one heat—if we never had to put a single piece back in the fire. Besides, we’d be running more stuff through the furnaces.”

“Sure,” I said, “anybody can see that. But what’s holding the jobs up? Who’s handling the stuff?”

There was no harm in kidding him a little. He was good and he knew it. He was so good that if Barney had come back he would have made him look like an amateur.

“Here’s how it could be done.”

“What done?”

“Jobs speeded up,” he said.

“How?”

“Not wait for signals. Know all about a job before starting. Go over the blue prints with Johnson. Even now—lotsa times—I know before he signals, what he wants me to do. But I can’t move, can’t move a lever till he gives me the signal, till he twitches that thumb. And all the time we’re losing the heat.”

“That’s true. But don’t forget—there’s Johnson.”

“Yeh,” he said, slumping down on the bed, “and there’s Johnson’s idea of Johnson.”

“You’ve got it figured out,” I said. “That ought to show you the idea won’t work.”

“Just the same, I’m going to try it. Put it up to him easy, be diplomatic.”

And he did, a couple of days later. But he wasn’t diplomatic—not diplomatic enough. And it got Johnson pretty hostile, which I knew that it would.

“What’s the matta?” he said. “Don’t you like the way I’m handlin’ my job? You want me to trade you?”

“That’s not it,” said Russ. “But if I had a look at those blue prints before we started a job, I’d know what was coming. Know how to work with you better. Get the idea of what you were trying to forge out. Give you better cooperation.”

“Oh—” said Johnson, “that’s it. You wanna get your hands on those blue prints. You wanna make it look to Riley like you was runnin’ the floor. Why doncha see Riley? Tell ’im you don’t need me no more. Tell ’im you’ll run the whole shebang by yourself.”

“Listen, Johnson. I’m not after your job. Don’t get sore. I’m only trying to speed up the work.”

“Well I’m tellin’ ya somethin’: I’m gettin’ paid for looking at those blue prints. See? I can do all the looking that has to be done. And when I can’t do it—when my job gets so big I can’t handle it—I’ll let you know. I’ll ask ya to please help me out. See?”

For the rest of the afternoon Russ was madder than seven boiled owls. He took it out on the ingots. There was a close connection between Russ—the way he was feeling, and the way the rig worked. I could always tell by the way the machine worked, how he felt. When the machine dragged, I knew Russ was tired. When it moved sharp and quick, I knew he was full of pep. That afternoon there was a snap to the rig. Nothing gentle and easy about the way it acted. The big claw bit into the ingots like it was going to bite them in two, swung them across the floor like a bulldog shaking a cat in a wide alley. Blue flashes played over the switchbox; things moved with a bang and I kept myself plenty well in the clear.

That night, up in the room, he was still feeling meaner than a wet traffic cop.

“With a guy like that Swede over us,” he said, “we’re stuck, Bennie. All he thinks about is his own measly job. When I’m up there on that rig—feel all that power at the tips of my fingers—all that static in the brass levers—I get to thinking. I get to feeling how easy it would be to reach out there and nip off his head. Or, if you should accidentally leave a furnace door open some time, how easy it would be to pick him up and stick him head first into the fire before he could let out a pip.”

“What are you crabbing about,” I said. “You’re drawing your wages.”

“A man’s got a right to get something out of a job besides wages,” he said.

“Besides wages? What else would he get? A Congressional medal?”

“He’s got a right to get some satisfaction.”

“Satisfaction?”

“Yes—satisfaction. A feeling that he’s worth something. That his work counts. That what he’s trying to do amounts to something.”

“That’s a new one,” I said. “You didn’t pick up that idea in no auto factory. Forget about Johnson. You don’t have to pal around with him, you and him don’t have to be buddies. What do you care if you don’t get to be general manager? You’re going clamming, ain’t you?”

“In a year. Maybe two years if I don’t get a raise. I’ll need three hundred bucks. Takes time to lay up that much.”

“Well, put in your time and be satisfied with drawing your wages—same as the others.”

“I don’t work that way,” he said. “If it was two days instead of two years, I still wouldn’t—unless I had to. It’s no fun. It’ll be no fun either for the fellow that has to dig up my wages if all the time I have to buck Johnson.”

He sat there on the edge of the bed for a while, thinking, and then he said:

“Bennie, why don’t you make up your mind to pull out and go clamming with me? We’ll be partners.” Just like that, out of a clear sky.

“Cripes—” I said. “Russ, I don’t know nothing about clams. I’ve always worked in Detroit—in Mr. Holt’s factory. I don’t know nothing else.”

“You don’t have to,” he said. “I’ll show you. I’ll explain everything to you that you have to know. You could work around camp—cut the wood and fix up the grub. I’d go out on the river and drag bottom. With the both of us saving our money, we could pull out of this dump in six or eight months. We’d go fifty-fifty. Make as much in one summer as we could here in two years.”

“Maybe. You’re not sure. It’s a gamble.”

“So’s this. So’s everything. Suppose we get laid off tomorrow? Suppose Mr. Holt figures he’s made enough automobiles for a while and shuts down? Besides—look at the fun we’d be having. Out in the open, lots of fresh game and fish. No cards to punch, no straw bosses playing Napoleon over us. We’d work hard and have fun at the same time.”

“It wouldn’t be like having a steady job.”

“How do you know? You’ve never had one. You get laid off every time they change models and then you don’t have any job. And all the time you’ve got to spend money. Clamming you practically don’t need money. You trade clam meats to the farmers for grub. Cut your own wood, dip your water out of a spring. Here even the water you drink has to be paid for; the air you breathe if you’re sitting in a movie and they’re running it through a strainer to take out the street dirt and smoke.

“What bothers me,” I said, “is the nights. There’d be nothing to do—way out in the sticks. No movies, no women—no nothing.”

“You’d forget about the movies,” he said. “You’d find you didn’t need ’em. You’d get along without newspapers, too, and you’d find that all the newspaper reading that you’ve done has left you just where you started. After supper we’d make a fire and talk about things. The frogs would be singing. Maybe there’d be a big moon coming up over the river, and, way off somewhere, there’d be 2 whippoorwill or an owl. Maybe a farmer would come down and bring us a jug of buttermilk and some Red Astrakan apples and we’d sit around the fire a while a-talking and telling stories. There’d be the smell of new clover hay and cornflowers in the air and by’n’by the fire would get low and go out and you’d see the fireflies making little trails of light over the grass where it’s high, and the mist forming along the edge of the water, and way off somewhere—t’hell ’n gone over the river—you’d hear a cowbell. That kind of life does something to you, Bennie. You get so, pretty soon, you lose all your bad habits. You forget about women; you don’t think about booze; and after a couple of months you get so that you can’t stand the stink of a cigarette. You get out an old pipe that’s caked up like a cement mixer and you fill ’er up and light her with a coal that you scrape up from the fire and you enjoy the smell of good honest tobacco without dope in it.”

“Dog-gone, Russ,” I said, “you sound like a poet. Blamed if I don’t think I’ll try it. I’ve always thought I’d like to do something like that.”

“Why don’t you?”

“Will you learn me how to play the guitar?”

“Sure,” he said. “It’s a go. From now on—we’re partners.”

We talked about clamming some more, and, then, pretty soon it got late and we turned in. And then I got to thinking, laying there under the blankets, I got to thinking maybe I was letting myself into something. What did I know about clamming? Suppose they didn’t bite? Suppose the government should put down an embargo—or a closed season—or whatever it is they do when they’re feeling mean about something? Then what? I had a good job in Mr. Holt’s factory. Times had been bad in Detroit; weren’t so good yet. And if they got bad again, I knew my way around—knew where I could get a hand-out in restaurants. Knew where I could pick up things in alleys behind stores. There are ways of getting by in a town like Detroit if you know how. But out in the country with nothing but cows and mosquitoes, and not knowing how to get along with either, I’d be lost. It was all right to sit there by the fire listening to whippoorwills and drinking buttermilk, but if it’s raining black cats a yard long, I’d rather have a drink of straight rye and be listening to the click of billiard balls in Jim’s Place. It was all right, too, to say that I’d lose my bad habits—that I’d forget all about women. But suppose I didn’t? Suppose sometime in a weak moment I’d want to tear off to 418314 North Hastings Street and couldn’t? Then what? Yeh—then what? Then what? Then what? I said to myself.

The next morning I didn’t have the nerve to tell Russ that I’d changed my mind. I thought I’d let it ride for a while and not tell him. I thought he might change his mind, too, if he got along all right in the factory, got a raise after while and didn’t stir up any more arguments with Johnson.

## Chapter IV

It wasn’t long before the white-collars up front began taking notice of what was going on on the floor. You’d see one of them hurrying along down the aisle as though he was late to a circus, and then he’d see Russ up there on the rig handling steel as though it was cordwood, and he’d slow down, come to a stop near a pillar and clean forget what he’d been in such a bloody hurry about. The way Russ got to handling that rig when he had a tough job up in the anvil was something to watch through the flying red sparks and the smoke. And then, one day, they changed the routing of the gangs of tourists that came through the factory and had them all bother us. It gave a man a headache to try and keep them off the track where they were all hell-bent on standing. There were all sorts and sizes of tourists. Flocks of schoolteachers; Rotary fellows, with little red flags and yellow buttons in their coats; Holstein breeders and members of the United Brotherhood of Bartenders. While I tried to herd them off the track and back into the aisle again, the guide would go into a spiel that sounded good but was all wrong, as a matter of fact, and Russ would shoot up and down as though it was just my hard luck and not his if he flattened out a couple of Masons or Elks on the tracks. One thing was sure—it wouldn’t be the Company’s, because every man, woman and child had to sign cards saying that it was their own fault, if, while going through the factory, they lost a leg or an arm or got slightly killed.

If it happened that Russ was handling a piece of steel under the big hammer—they saw something. It gave them something to take home and remember and tell the brother Masons about when they got back to Sioux Center or Oshkosh.

One time there was a lady in the crowd who had a pair of glasses on the end of a stick and carried a dog. It was a small dog, hardly big enough to support more than one or two fleas; white as a snowflake, and bangs down over the front of its eyes. At the very first crack of the hammer he let out a yip, jumped to the ground and scampered under the manipulator. The lady threw a high-toned conniption fit. We were stymied—couldn’t turn a wheel.

“Oh, my Da-die—my Dadie!” she screamed. “He’ll get hurt—he’ll get killed! Somebody save him! Oh! Please! Won’t somebody save him!”

Russ swung down the ladder in one flying leap.

“What’s the matter?” he said. “Somebody get hurt?”

“No—no—o—o—” she moaned in a husky voice. “My dog—my Dadie! He’s under there. Can’t you see? Can’t you see—e—e ——”

“My gosh,” he said. “That’s serious. What’s he doing under there? Call him out, can’t you?”

She stooped down and called. Begged him to come out, pleaded. But he had his mind made up to stay where he was.

“He won’t come,” she said. “He won’t mind me. Poor dear thing, he’s scared. He’s scared to death. He’s been sulky all morning. Didn’t like the noise, didn’t like the smell of the dirty old factory. He’ll think I’ve been mean; he’ll never forget it.”

Half the crowd were on their knees calling. The other half were getting primed with suggestions. A man in gray spats suggested getting a big dog to haul the little dog out. A schoolteacherish looking lady said that animal magnetism would do the trick and went about from one to the other asking if there was anyone in the crowd that was “dog-minded.” Johnson came up, sputtering, full of original ideas.

“Start the rig up,” he said to Russ. “Scrape a couple of gears; throw a scare into ’im. He’ll come out a-fluting.”

“Don’t you dare!” she exploded. “I’ll have you reported; I’ll have you fined. He’s too scared, now. That’s what’s the matter, that’s why he won’t come.”

Russ had the good sense to back out and leave the field clear for Johnson.

“Lady,” said Johnson, “that dog a yours is holdin’ us up. It’s costin’ us money.”

“Can I help it? It’s your men, your noise, that frightened him. Please—tell the others to stop calling. It merely confuses—makes him worse.”

“Maybe,” said Johnson, “if you had somethin’ to eat for ’im. Maybe one of the boys has got a sandwich —”

“That’s ridiculous. He never eats, except at mealtimes. It’s doctor’s orders.”

“But, lady, we gotta do somethin’. We got a job on the iron. It’s gettin’ cold.”

She tried calling again. And such a line of ga-ga the hard-boiled scalers never listened to before in their lives. It was “bad mans” this and “‘b’es ee’s itty heart” that and “Mommie’s sweet Dadie doggy. Won’t um tum out. Oh pease. Won’t um tum out.” It was the stickiest line of baby-talk you ever heard. She did her best. You half expected the nuts and washers to back off and come rolling out. But Dadie didn’t.

“It’s no use,” she moaned. “He’s too nervous. He’s lost confidence in me—in his own Mommie. He won’t come until he changes his mood—until he loves me again.”

“How long you tink it will take?” asked Johnson.

“Oh, I don’t know. He hardly looked at me—then. Shut off those terrible machines back there that keep hammering, keep pounding so! They’re making him nervous. I know.”

“Can’t do it, lady. They’re on production. Have to shut down the whole factory.”

“Then take this thing apart. Get your men here. How long will it take?”

“Can’t do that either. We gotta use it. We gotta get going. Listen, lady. If we start it up—make a little noise maybe—not much —”

“NO-NO! It would frighten him. Make him worse —”

“I t’ink he’d come out a ki-yi-ing, if you ask me. If he didn’t I’d see the company got you anodda white pup. It’ll cost a couple hundred if we can’t get to work.”

“A couple of hundred! What’s that? Would you kill my precious for that? You’re brutal! You’re not fit to live in the same world with animals.”

She left Johnson cold and turned to the guide.

In the meantime, Russ had dragged up an air hose and was giving directions to one of the scalers. The scaler stuck the hose under the rig, on the far side; Russ stood on the other.

“If Doctor Anson were here,” she said to the guide, “he’d know a way out. He has such a way with my Dadie—such understanding. Where’s the nearest telephone—long distance? I’ve got to talk to him. New York.”

The scaler had his thumb on the valve. Russ gave the signal. There was a mighty blast as a hundred and fifty pounds of air were cut loose: an upheaval of dirt, cinders, coal dust, and iron scales rose from the floor, and one or two of us who didn’t have our eyes shut saw a greasy white streak about the size and shape of a dirty dishrag skid over the floor.

When the dust settled, there was Russ holding the pup, trying to keep the dog from licking the grime off his face.

“My Dadie!” she cried. “My darling! The man saved you! My snookie—um—u—m—m—ums—.” They clinched.

The steel was cold. We had to stick it back in the fire. And when Russ picked it off the anvil and rolled down the track with the rig, Joe, the sweeper, raised an eyebrow and wrinkled his nose and looked through an imaginary pair of glasses at the end of a stick down at the floor. The pup had left a mess for him to clean up.

## Chapter V

You would have thought what with all the admiration he got from the crowds, and the guides laying it on plenty thick that he would have started swelling up like a poisoned pup. But such wasn’t the case. He gave most of the credit to the rig and began getting fussy about it, calling it pet names like “Betsy” and “Old Ironsides,” and even others more sentimental, I expect, when I wasn’t near. Night after night, after work, we’d go down to the factory with a bottle of polish and shine up the brasses, he doing most of the work; me listening to his odd line of chatter.

“I tell you, Bennie,” he’d say, “it gives a man a great feeling to get up there when the power is on and get his hands on those levers and feel the static go tingling up and down your fingers and arms. It’s the same as though you had two hundred horsepower added to your own strength. To be able to handle power like that and make it mind you—make it do your will, with the delicate touch of a lever—I tell you it makes a bigger man outa you.”

“It sure does,” I said, “unless you was to get too much of that static and it sat you down on your seater.”

He got so he made all his own adjustments, and after setting up a brake or a clutch, he’d throw on the juice and see how it worked. I’d set a milk bottle full of water down on the floor and he’d try to pick it up and empty it without breaking the glass. We fooled around so much in the factory at night the night watch got suspicious and we got orders to stay out.

One time Herman, who ran the overhead crane and chewed tobacco, spit down from his cage and hit the claw on the machine. Russ was carrying a piece of cold steel. He stopped the rig, lowered the boom, and got Herman’s shirt from the cabinet and swiped off the rig.

That night, at the clock, they had some words. Not much—but sharp. It left bad feelings in both.

A day or two later Herman started being careless with his loads—swinging in close, as he went over the rig. Russ got up on his ear.

“Tell your man up there,” he said to Herman’s helper, “if he does that again I’ll reach up there and pull him down to the floor.”

“Take it easy,” I said. “No use getting him sore at you. There’s some big jobs coming up. You’re going to need his help.”

What we called a big job was anything over ten tons, which was the limit capacity of the rig. Anything over, Russ couldn’t handle alone and Herman helped. A sling was wrapped around the steel and Herman let down his hook and tied onto it, easing some of the strain off the rig. Cooperation between the two had to be smooth; it was no job for a couple of soreheads to work on together.

The bell rang and Herman came down for lunch. Russ went up to him and made himself plain.

“Listen here,” he said, “throw another load of steel over my head—and you and I are going to tangle. One of us is going to the hospital. Understand?”

Herman never opened his mouth. It had some effect on him. For a few days he kept his loads in the clear, and then he began swinging them in close again. Not close enough so that Russ could get down to business and do something about it, but still close enough to make him uncomfortable. He spoke to Johnson about it.

“TI t’ot you tol him,” said Johnson. “He tol me you was givin’ him orders what to do. He don’t need two bosses.”

“I did tell him,” said Russ. “Now I’m telling you. If he drops something—hits me—don’t try to tell ’em up there it was an accident. See? Because Bennie, here, will be there to prove what I said. What I’m telling you now.”

“What’s a matta—” said Johnson, “you getting nervous or somethin’? For eight years he’s been slinging steel ova guys hets. He’s neva hit nobody yet.”

“That’s a great argument, Johnson.”

“Well—it’s an argument, ain’t it?”

“The kind you’d put up. But you’ll have to think of another. You’ll have to talk to Herman, get him lined up. Or I’ll get him lined up without talking.”

They had no love for each other, them two. Johnson would have fired him in a minute if he thought that he could have got away with it. In the old days straw bosses fired men right and left, but Mr. Holt had put a stop to it. Besides, Russ had made himself pretty valuable and Johnson knew it, and he knew that some of the white-collars up front knew it, too. And it was no secret to Russ.

That night, after supper, Russ went out, and when he came back he had an outboard motor. It was a great bargain, he said. Only been run just a little. Two cylinders. A hundred and ten bucks is what he had to give for it and he paid forty dollars down.

He lugged it up into the room and began taking it apart. In a short time he had it spread all over the floor. He hadn’t talked to me about clamming for a month and I was beginning to think that he had got the idea out of his system. Now he was all hepped up on it again.

“Now, all we need,” he said, “is a boat. A boat and some sort of old jaloppie to get around in. And I’ve been thinkin’—if we had a trailer, we wouldn’t need a tent.”

“Love of Mike,” I said, “let’s not buy no more stuff till we get ready to start.”

I was thinking of the fifty-five bucks—my share—I’d have to shell out for the outboard.

“What’s the matter,” he said, “you gettin’ cold feet?”

“Why no, Russ, I’m not gettin’ cold feet. Only thing is—we’ve got us a couple good jobs now. Things are going along nice; times are hard yet and there are a lot of fellows around on the streets looking for work. If they give you a raise, you might change your mind.”

“Not me,” he said. “Never.”

“I’ve seen it happen—lotsa times. A fellow wants to earn a little money; wants to get himself set up in some little business or something; wants to buy a little garage, maybe, and doesn’t have quite enough cash. Goes in and gets himself a job on the line. He’s only gonna work just a coupla months—half a year, maybe. Then he’s gonna pull out and go in for himself. What happens? He gets a raise and forgets all about the garage. I’ve seen it happen. Not once—lotsa times. He likes it after he’s been there a while.”

“That’s where you’re wrong,” he said. “He doesn’t like it. He gets married, maybe, or his wife gets a kid. He’s afraid they’ll starve if he misses a pay check—afraid the loan sharks will get his car—afraid he might not make a go of the garage. He loses his nerve and fear keeps his nose to the grindstone. It’s bad, but he gets used to it. Everybody else is in the same fix and pretty soon he gets to thinking, ‘there’s nothing funny about me—nothing unusual. It’s just life. Life as she’s lived.’ And he’s right. It is life—in Detroit.”

“But we can’t pull out till warm weather.”

“That’s right. We’ll put in the winter and the spring in the factory. Save our money. About June we’ll start. In June the black bass will be biting.”

“Yeh?”

“And I know a place where there’re mushrooms the shape of strawberries and as big as your fist.”

“But the nights. What are we gonna do in the long nights?”

“At night we’ll sit around the fire and light up our pipes. I know some stories I learned in the woods—about a fellow by the name of Paul Bunyan. Couldn’t tell them here; here they’d sound like a pack of plain lies. The surroundings have to be right. There has to be the smell of wood smoke in the air; the sound of crickets; or way off—a coyote. A bucket of spring water at your elbow. I’d show you how to pick out some tunes on the guitar and after you’d learned to play we’d get in the boat and drift out onto the river and we’d have us some music. The air would be still and the water smooth as a table and sky soft as blue velvet. It wouldn’t be grand opera, maybe, but it would be fun. And being on the water, it’d sound twice as good as it really was. How’d you like that?”

“Dog-gone it, Russ, when you get to talking like that —”

“Makes you feel like you’re rarin’ to go, don’t it? I know, Bennie. I’m the same way when the air is warm and damp and soft in the spring and the geese are flying. Sometimes, in the morning, when the air is like that I can hardly make myself go back to the factory.”

It was midnight and he had the motor together again and he wrapped it up in a piece of burlap and shoved it under the bed. We turned in. “Oh, well,” I said to myself, “itll be six months till summer and a lot of things can happen in six months to make him change his mind. And if he doesn’t, and Mr. Holt should decide to change models about that time and lay everybody off, it would be fun to get out in the sticks, for a while, lay around on the grass, go sputtering up and down a nice river, and every morning have frog-legs for breakfast. At the end of a couple of weeks I’d sell my interest in the outboard and go back to town.”

## Chapter VI

When we got off the street car there was a mob at number two gate.

“Come on, Russ,” I said; “let’s get over there. Looks like a fight!”

I ran across the wide street. Whether Russ ran or not, I don’t know. Maybe he got held up by the traffic. All I know is that I got over there, pushed my way through the crowd, and got into a fight so quick that I don’t know yet how it happened. There was the lunch cart, and it was Rita’s and she stood on a box behind the cart, and a little runt of a wop was shaking his fist and calling her names in bad sounding Italian. She called him a liar in plain English, and one or two other things besides, and he came for her in the face of a barrage of ripe bananas and candy bars, and jumped up on the edge of the cart like a monkey. I grabbed him. Right away some big guy in the crowd yelled out, “Back up! Make room—they’re gonna fight. The little guy’s gonna clean up on the Eye-talyan!”

“Dee-sa guy? Dee-sa guy gonna fight me? Me—Tony?” said the Italian. “I keel ——”

Before I could argue the point somebody gave me a push and the Italian lit into me. I squared off and landed one alongside his head. He backed off and came at me—drove his head into the pit of my stomach. Before I got straightened up he was at me again. This time he missed. I swung, but he wasn’t there. He was like a rooster; he didn’t know how to fight. He jumped in and out and kept dodging around like a wild man. We clinched and I slugged him two on the nose, but not hard—not hard enough. I needed more room—room to swing—to get in some good licks with steam behind them. We pulled away and he landed a kick on my shin that hurt—I mean really hurt. He landed a wallop on the side of my jaw. I was getting the worst of it by a lot. I was taking more than I was handing out. One hit me square in the eye and the fellows let out a roar. That got me mad and I came for him, fanning the breeze with my fists. He came in again like a goat. I swung and just scraped his ear. Again we clinched—milling around and around—trying to get at each other. I remembered that kick; watched his foot; but now he was clawing at me, digging his nails into my neck. Trying, for all I knew, to bite off my ear. Teeth clinched, he hissed like a leaky air hose.

He tried to kick me again and I tripped him up and we went down together, kicking and biting and scratching. I was on top but he was too slick. I couldn’t hold him. Then he was on top, pounding my face and my shoulders. I flipped over, got my face down. He had me but I had my face to the ground.

In that split part of a second, when I flipped around, I saw the legs of the fellows—millions of legs, all around me—legs like a forest; and I wondered if Russ was there. I remember thinking—if he’s there, why doesn’t he do something? Why doesn’t he help me? Why doesn’t he pull this wop off? And then I thought—what if he’s not there? And I got a sick feeling in the pit of my stomach. Oh Lordy, I thought, what if he’s not there to help me out of this mess.

The wop was still on me, trying to get at my face. But I hardly noticed the blows—I was numb, but my brain was still clear. And then, in a flash, my whole life came up before me; things that Mr. Holt is against, that he writes about in his book: fooling around with women—labor unions—gambling with wages—drinking and smoking cigarettes in toilets. I thought of his book and the golden text popped into my head: “Work hard and behave yourself.” And here I was in a fight—and getting the worst of it—might even get killed. “Oh, Mr. Holt—” I dug the words out of myself— “Please get me out of this mess. Amen.”

Some one reached over and took the wop by the seat of the pants and lifted him up as though he had been a sack of potatoes. It was Russ. I got up. Russ had the wop by the collar.

“What’s the matter,” he said to the wop, “can’t you fight fair?”

“I no wanna fight; hee’s a wanna fight me. I wanna fight joost mine own—a familee—joost me an’ she—joosta leetle fight. Me, I’m too old makka fight eferybod-dee.”

“It look to me,” said Russ, “as though you were just going to pull a knife.”

“Non—non —”

“Let’s see ’f you got one.”

He reached into the wop’s pocket and pulled out a jack-knife.

“There you are,” said Russ, snapping the blade open. “A regular toad-stabber of a thing. What do you carry a thing like that for? Look at the size of that blade.”

A couple of cops bulled their way through.

“What’s going on here?” they asked.

“This little Dago, here,” said Russ, “got into a scrap. I took his knife.”

“Did he pull it?”

The little wop jabbered a streak.

“I tell-a you I no wanna fight. Dees-a guy wanna fight. Me—I’m too old. Joost-a mine own familee —

“Shut up,” said the cop.

The girl pushed her way through.

“He’s my father,” she said. “He tried to slap me. But it doesn’t matter. Please let him go.”

That reminded the wop of the first argument, and he got riled again and began edging off towards the girl.

“She’s-a no gudde,” he said. “She’s-a alla time crazy for man. De whole night she’s texi-dencing—texi-dencing—texi-dencing. I say—no; she say yes. She go joosta same I say notting. She’s-a no gudde I tella you. She’s —-”

“Aw shut up,” said the cops. “What we want to know—” they asked Russ, “did he pull this knife. If he did he goes in.”

“Don’t ask me,” said Russ. “Ask her. Looks like this was just a friendly little family affair. I’m out of it.”

They looked at her.

“No—he didn’t,” she said. “He didn’t even have it open. Some smart guy—standing here in the crowd took it out of his pocket.”

“All right—” said the cops. “Scatter. All you guys scatter. Get outa here!”

Nearly all the fellows beat it. Russ and I stood there a minute.

The old man’s hat lay on the ground. The girl picked it up and put it back on his head. She brushed the dirt from his clothes; took the knife from the cops and put it back in his pocket.

“Musn’t lose your temper like that, Pa,” she said to him. “It’s bad. Besides, if the H.P.’s see you—you’ll lose your job.” The H.P.’s were the Holt Police.

She took a box lunch from the cart and gave it to him. “Better hurry,” she said, “you’ll be late.”

Russ and I walked off together.

“Looks like you jumped into something with both feet,” he said to me. “What was the idea?”

“How did I know it was a family affair? It looked like he was going to climb over the cart, pull out her hair.”

“And you were gonna rescue the poor maiden and live happily ever after ——”

“Where was you?” I asked him. “Why didn’t you do something when you saw he was beating me up?”

“You quit,” he said. “You didn’t try. You could have handled him—easy. If that gang had been a bunch of lumber-jacks you would have got the pants razzed off of you.”

He was always dragging the lumber-jacks into his arguments.

“The wop had me pinned. He was pounding the tar outa me.”

“So I noticed. Why didn’t you pound the tar outa him? You stuck your nose into it. It was your fight.”

It was late.

“You can’t go in,” he said. “Your eye’s a mess. Better go home and put a piece of raw beef on it.”

He went in and I turned to cross the wide street. A black river of men, the graveyard shift, streamed out of the factory and across the wide, overhead pass. Below, the street was all clear. A Holt truck with a string of soup carts came rattling down the wide concrete; stopped, hooked the girl’s cart on behind, and went on. On the other side of the fence the factory was running full blast. Locomotives ran back and forth over acres of tracks; a steel car full of white hot coals from the coke ovens ran into the quenching tower and as the cold water let go, a white cloud rose up into the gray Michigan sky. The Cooper-Hewitts were burning and the factory looked like a monster with a million green eyes. A piece of paper from somebody’s lunch blew down the street in a little flurry of coal dust and cinders and caught against the steel fence.

The girl stood with her back braced against the cold wind off the lake, waiting for a bus. She had on brown slacks and a black coat and a boy’s cap and the wind blew her bobbed hair past her eyes. I thought of some lines I’d once heard a long-haired fellow with a banjo recite to the crowd one night near the gates:

When the last moonshiner buys his radio,  
And the last wild rabbit of a girl,  
Is civilized by a mail-order dress —

That’s all I remembered.

“It’s you,” she said when I came up. “Where are you going with that black eye?”

“Home,” I said.

Whenever I get around a good-looking woman I get to acting as if I was feeling sad about something. I can’t help it. With Russ it’s just the opposite. He can always think of a wise-crack to make and he acts as though every dame owed him a compliment for his good looks.

“You should have stayed out of it,” she said.

“How did I know it was just a friendly little squabble?” I said. “I thought he was going to tear you apart. You don’t look sorry exactly.”

“What do you expect me to do?” she said. “Hold your head in my lap and bathe your brow?”

“I’ll bet you enjoyed seeing him get the best of me.”

“Well, there’s the Rettinelli reputation, you know. Us Rettinellis are all fighters, quick-tempered. And after all, he’s my father, and you didn’t put up much of a fight. What did you expect me to do—cheer for you?”

“I didn’t start in to lick him,” I said. “He was mad —”

“And you were scared. I’ve got a kid brother at home I’ll bet could lick you.”

“I’m not a fighter,” I said.

“I don’t think so, either, and I’m sorry you got into it. I really am, because you didn’t have it coming. Where’s your boy friend?” she asked.

“In there.”

“Where does he work?”

“In the drop forge.”

“I’ll bet he’s a straw boss, or something. I’ll bet he draws down big wages?”

“I get the same as he does. We work together. What do you care where he works, what he gets? What’s he got that I haven’t?”

“Put it the other way around,” she said. “It wouldn’t take so long to answer.” -

“What have I got, then, that he hasn’t?”

“Well—a black eye. And it’s getting worse. Better go home and do something about it.”

“I can tell you one thing,” he said. “In case you don’t remember what he did with your card —”

“What?”

“He’s not interested in skirts.”

“That’s what you think. I’ll bet you’re spoofing me—about where he works.”

“I’m not. He works in the drop forge department. He runs a big machine there; handles big pieces of red-hot steel. I’m his helper.”

“One of these days I’m going through the place—find out for myself. See if you’re telling the truth.”

“You’d have a sweet chance,” I said, “getting past the guards.”

“Not like this,” she said, meaning her slacks. “I’d put on a dress, wear a hat. I’d get a ticket, make believe I’m a visitor from out of town. I’d go through with the crowd.”

“You wouldn’t have to do that,” I said. “Not a good-looker like you. You could tell ’em you were a movie star—Greta Garbo. They’d give you a special guide—maybe two, let you go through in style.”

Her bus slowed up to the curb.

“Maybe I will,” she said, swinging on like a kid. “Tell your boy friend to be on the look.”

“She’s-a all time craz-zy for man,” her Old Man had said. He had her figured out to a T—I could see that plain as day.

## Chapter VII

A couple of days later Johnson lined out a job for us that looked tough right from the start. It was a piece of steel half as big as a Holt car and marked “experimental.” It was a die job and was the lower block of a set of dies that together would weigh thirty tons. They were to be used for stamping out from a single piece of metal the whole top and a part of the sides of the two-door car body.

Getting it into the furnace was a job for both rigs. Easing it off the floor Herman helped and took most of the strain; Russ steadied. The eight steel lines coming down from the drums on Herman’s rig were as stiff as iron rods with the tension. Herman had most of the load; Russ had the responsibility. Herman could raise and lower and move the load in four different directions; Russ had control over every motion that a man has in his shoulder, elbow and wrist. It was nice team-work. Smooth and steady the load was raised from the floor and moved up the bay to number one furnace. And then came the ticklish job of getting the steel through the door without wrecking the furnace. Herman got into position—cut off his power, and went dead. “All yours,” Johnson signalled to Russ. He eased her in, his hands stroking the levers. Herman still had a dead-gravity load. Russ pushed her gently, easing her in; there wasn’t one inch to spare. The front end of the steel touched the floor of the furnace. Herman dropped off and Russ gave her a clean push through the door. He pulled out.

It was nice work, but the hard part—the work under the hammer—was still to come. Johnson spoke to Russ. “Betta check your rig over, right afta dinner. I want everything to go extra smooth—see? Once I sta’t flat-tennin’ her out you’ll never get her back through that door. We gotta do it all in one heat and there’s plenty of forge work on that baby before she sta’ts freezing.”

Anything that wasn’t red hot to Johnson was cold. A piece of steel that didn’t lick the grease off the floor wasn’t even warm to the touch.

“I never let a job down yet,” said Russ. “Guess I won’t this one. Better talk to Herman.”

“Herman’s doing all right, ain’t he?”

“So far,” said Russ.

All the forenoon the steel soaked in the furnace. It took up a lot of heat even before starting to turn red, and after that a lot more.

We had lunch and then Russ and I worked on the machine. I swiped while he oiled and checked over camshafts and gears. His big, gentle hands slid over the journals; the way he felt of the bearings was like a caress. “We’ll sling her up there,” he said. “We’ll twist her tail any old way the Swede says. We’ll make her behave.”

I didn’t say anything. He acted as if he was talking to me, but I knew he wasn’t. Other things he said I couldn’t hear, but I knew what they were—a lot of sweet jabberings that a man usually don’t say to nobody except his sweetheart or wife.

About two-thirty Johnson said he was ready. We took the hooks off Herman’s crane and rigged on a sling. The big hammer was cut in on the high pressure steam and began warming up. Johnson cleared space for action and gave Russ right-of-way on the floor. Russ put on his green goggles, sloshed water on the front of his shirt, and climbed up on the rig. We were ready to start.

A cold piece of steel is one thing; a hot piece of steel is something else. Cold steel is like a wild animal that is dead; hot steel is like one that is alive and dangerous—there is energy in it. Energy to make the breath stick in your throat—to scorch the inside of your lungs if you breathe through your mouth—to lick the grease and chips off the floor and to send you to the hospital if you get close. Yet you have to work close or you lose control.

Russ dragged the steel halfway out of the furnace and clasped on. We wrapped the sling around and even with long irons the job was too hot to be fun. Herman picked up his share of the load and they swung her across the floor to the hammer. A minute later they had her up on the iron and the anvil took Herman’s load. Russ had it all.

Johnson’s signals were fast: “Left turn—a little—lower the front end a hair—a bit more—too much—o-keh—hold her.”

The hammer rapped out ten or fifteen blows. Fire sprayed; the ground trembled and an empty milk bottle that someone had left on a switch-box crashed to the floor. One end, the upper side, was beveled off, fairly smooth.

A new position. “Shove her up,” Johnson signalled. Herman picked up the load for a minute; Russ pushed her over; once again there were signals. Russ’s eyes were on Johnson; his hands on the levers was a sight that was beautiful to see. His shirt was steaming and the red light from the steel was on his face.

Once more the hammer—the upper right side was beveled. Once more a quick change; the hammer again—the left side was finished. And then Johnson signalled: “Pull her off—reverse end positions.”

“Judast priest,” I said, “that’s gonna make the job tough.”

A string of visitors came in and lined up to watch. The guide started his spiel and began telling the crowd all about steel, the number of kinds used in making a car, the cost of the rig. Every week it went up a thousand dollars or more.

We lowered off to the floor, switched ends and shifted Herman’s rigging while Johnson snapped some measurements with long-handled calipers and the scalers cleaned off the anvil.

“Pick her up!” Johnson signalled. And it was then Herman started his monkey business. He lit into his load with a snap. Russ lost his bite and was helpless.

“What’s a madda!” Johnson bellered. “Can’t you fellas come up togedda? Take it easy!”

Herman couldn’t have heard what he said. Russ kept his mouth shut and worked out a new hold. The job was harder for him, now; he had the end that was beveled. There were no corners—no rough surfaces to bite into.

“Easy now,” Johnson signaled. “Bring her up —”

This time they got her off the floor—were starting to raise her—when Herman jerked. Once more Russ lost his hold. He was beginning to get sore. He motioned for Johnson to come up but Johnson ignored him.

“Get it up there!” he signalled. “Stop horsin’ around—she’s gettin’ cold!” That time they both jerked. She slipped clean out of Herman’s sling and rolled to the floor.

Johnson was burnt up. Russ slid down the ladder and went up to him.

“Pull him off,” he said. “Give me the floor. I can’t work with that fellow—he’s trying to queer me.”

“You’re crazy,” said Johnson. “You can’t handle ’er.”

“I’ll handle her,” said Russ. “Get him out-a here. I’ll shorten my boom—get leverage.”

“And burn yourself up.”

“What-a you care? We’re losing our heat. She’s getting cold.”

“O-keh,” said Johnson. “She’s yours—you asked for it.”

He waved Herman off the floor.

Russ passed me.

“Keep everybody off the track behind me,” he snapped. “I’m gonna move—shoot in and grab—back out for air. Throw me a spray when I come past.” He swung up.

There was action without waiting for signals from Johnson. Russ shortened his boom a full quarter. The steel lay on its side. He bit into it—slammed it over—backed away—came from another angle and turned it again. He was after a good hold and got it. He shortened the boom another foot. He could have spit on the steel.

“What’s ’e tryin’ to do,” said Johnson, “get ’er up in his lap?”

Russ began raising the load. Slowly, it came up from the floor. There was no singing of gears—no easy purring of motors. The gears growled; the motors groaned with resentment. Circuit breakers popped out with the sound of explosions and were sent back with a flash. Switches snapped and gave off little corkscrews of blue smoke. The machine responded against a gigantic resistance but you felt that it was not the electrical current that turned the wheels and raised the steel—it was the strength, the speed, and the expert control of those powerful hands on the levers.

He got it up on the anvil and the fancy work started. Johnson stepped in again and for once his signals were clear. There was no sling to worry about now—no clumsy helper. Russ crouched low behind the controls. The heat must have been something fierce, and when the heavy blows fell he was in the red rain of hot iron.

The guide stood in line with his gang—forgot his spiel and looked on. The scalers, better able to appreciate what they saw, stopped work and rubbered as hard as the others. Three times he got the steel in position; three times the hammer battered and pounded. Then he balanced the steel on the anvil, unclamped, and shot back for a breath of cool air.

The paint on the forward end of the machine was beginning to blister. The oil on the open gears smoked and the steel ladder was hot to the touch. I handed him a clean pair of goggles and one of the scalers threw up some gloves.

“It won’t get you nowhere,” I hollered, “if you burn yourself up.”

“Don’t worry,” he said. “This time we’ll finish her off.”

He rolled in again. Johnson signalled. There was a high spot near the center to work down.

This time it was even more difficult to hold. It was beveled all the way around. He got it, but the first light blow of the hammer loosened it up. It was a struggle again to get the right hold and all the time the steel was up there on the anvil. He turned and tilted and once or twice it threatened to get away and slide to the floor. But he got it, and the hammer did its last work.

“O-keh—” Johnson signaled. “All done.”

The crowd broke out and applauded. Some high school kids in the bunch let out a football yell.

Russ backed away and climbed down. He was wiping his face with a towel when Rita, the girl, stepped out from the crowd and came up. This time she was dressed like a lady. No slacks, no cap pulled down over one ear, but street clothes like the others. You couldn’t have told her from one of the girls in the high school bunch except that she had them all beat for looks.

“Gee—” she said, “you were swell.”

“Did you like it?” he asked.

“I sure did,” she said. “The way you handled those levers—made the machine do what you wanted-. If you hadn’t won I would have felt terrible. It was like watching a fight. I got a thrill.”

“I thought you would,” he kidded. “I put in a few extra frills for your special benefit—just to make it ood.”

“Really? Did you know I was there?”

“Oh, sure. I could tell by the static.”

She gave him a questioning look. The fellows began noticing that Russ was talking to a good-looking dame. She wore a straw hat that sat on the back of her head and when she looked up at him it framed her face like a picture. Not many of the fellows there realized that she was the same girl who dressed in boy’s overalls or slacks and sold box-lunches outside the gate. The guide had started and the crowd straggled out loosely behind.

“It’s against the rules for visitors to kid us workers during hours,” said Russ. “If you wanna talk to us you’ll have to get a permit from Old Sourpuss, the boss.”

“How’s that?”

“Safety engineers don’t allow it—been too many accidents.”

“Why’s that? Tell me about it.”

“Well, a gal in a sun-suit came through one time and started talking to Bennie, here, and he near lost an eye. He came pretty near getting compensation out of it.”

“I should think he would have,” she said. “I should think it would be compensation enough just to have gotten a smile, if she was pretty.”

“Oh, she was pretty,” said Russ. “But good looks and nice shapes don’t mean nothing to us. When we talk about compensations we mean dough—real dough, and nothing else but.”

“How about the workers kidding the visitors?” she asked.

“Oh, that’s all right—after hours.”

“Well,” she said, “after hours I’m still working in that taxi-dance place.”

“And after hours,” he said, “I still don’t like to climb stairs.”

The guide was a block down the aisle and she hurried along after the others.

“No soap, eh?” I said.

“No soap,” he answered. “When I’m clamming I like to catch my own clams. I don’t like to have ’em come up outa the water and start climbing in over the sides of the boat.”

## Chapter VIII
