One Saturday night, a week or two later, Russ and I were walking up Woodward Avenue, coming home from a movie. We stopped in a beer joint and took on a few beers and a little farther on up the street we came to a place where there was dancing upstairs. It was a taxi-dance place and there were pictures of girls in low-necked dresses behind a big window and a sign that read:

One Hundred Beautiful Girls  
A Dime a Dance.

It was one of those places where the house furnished the girls and the girls and the house each get half of your dough.

“Let’s see what it’s like,” said Russ. “I feel like dancing after those beers.”

“Let’s not,” I said. “Let’s get to bed. All they’re after up there is your dough. It’s a racket.’ ”

“It’s only ten cents a dance.”

“Dances only last two minutes. That’s three bucks an hour—figure it out for yourself.”

“Well—let’s see what the women are like.”

“What do you think they’re like?” I asked.

“All virgins,” he said with a laugh.

He had his mind made up and we climbed the stairs. We checked our hats and coats and he laid out a buck for a string of ten tickets. I didn’t buy any.

“What’s the idea?” he said.

“Go ahead and dance,” I said. “I’ll sit and watch. I can think of other ways for getting rid of my money.”

We walked out to the edge of the floor and looked over the lay-out. Thirty or forty couples were dancing; a bunch of fellows sat near the wall on chairs and sofas and about twenty girls stood along a white line on the floor waiting for partners. Some of them shook their hips in time with the music; some swayed and shimmied and a couple of them gave us a “I’ll-give-you-a-good-time-honey” look. Russ picked out a tall blond and they . waltzed out onto the floor.

It was warm in the building; the pink lights were low so that you couldn’t see the wrinkles on some of the old biddies that were no longer spring chickens and the whole place had a smell like a gents’ barber shop in the colored district. The players were dressed like waiters; the girls wore evening gowns that came to the floor, while the men who’d come to dance were in street clothes.

While sitting there, who should I see but Rita, dancing with a dark-skinned little fellow about twice her own age. She saw me and came in off the floor, sort of trailing the little gent behind her like an old Holt car with the rear axle gone. She introduced us and his name was Louie and I could see in a minute that he wasn’t interested in anything very much except her. She asked about Russ in an indirect sort of way, wanting to know whether I’d come up alone, and she was still chewing the rag—marking time—when Russ also came in off the floor; parked the blond, and came over. He got down to business at once and asked her to dance.

“I’m verr sor-r-rie,” said Louie. “Is tree more tickets I got. Dees lady she is dance with me.”

“I’m asking the lady,” said Russ. “I’m up here on her invitation. I think I’ll dance with her now.”

The musicians were playing. Russ took her hand.

“I’m verr sor-rie—” said Louie, “dees lady is mine. Is tree more ticket —”

Russ ignored him. “Come on, baby—” he said to Rita, “Jet’s you and me dance. Never mind him.”

She jerked her hand out of his and stood rigid.

“I’m sorry,” she said, “you’ll have to wait your turn.”

“It’s my turn now,” he said. “He’s had his.”

“He asked me first. As long as his tickets hold out ——”

“Holy Mackinaw,” he said. “I’ve got tickets, if that’s what you want.”

The wop took her arm. He tried to lead her away. Russ held her. Put his arm around her waist.

“Come on,” he said. “Cut out the foolishness—. dance!”

She leaned back and slapped him a hard one alongside the face. Her eyes blazed like a cat’s.

“Just because you’ve blowed yourself for a few tickets,” she said, “don’t get the idea you own me. I’m a taxi-dancer but that doesn’t mean I’m your slave. You can wait for me or go home. A little rest might do you good—after climbing those stairs.” She went off with Louie.

“The little hell-cat,” said Russ. “I could break a box-car in two.”

“Now, if you know your women,” I said, “you’ll get your hat and coat and we’ll get outa here. Hang around here and wait until she gets good and ready to give you a dance—you’re a sap. Let’s beat it.”

“Beat it nothing,” he said. “I’m onto their racket, now. I’m going to get her away from that wop and when I do—she’s gonna dance with me till they turn the lights out. I’m gonna get me a string of tickets a yard long.” And he did. He got five dollars worth.

And then he waited until this guy Louie had danced out his three tickets and he grabbed her before the wop could go to the window and plank down his money for more.

I watched them dance for a while. Russ was no slouch—he moved easily and carried himself well on the floor—but she was better. She was streamlined in a dress of black stuff like velvet that came low on her back and shoulders and was tight at the hips and made her look like a mermaid. She wore long earrings that sparkled like diamonds; small slippers the color of silver, and a sparkling thing on the side of her hair that shimmered like the top-notch on the head of a peacock.

At first they were sort of stand-offish and cold and danced like a couple of high school kids under the eyes of their mothers. After the third or fourth dance they had made up and began kidding and talking and she showed him some new steps and after that she danced hot-cha and close. It was easy to see that she knew all the tricks that women practice and men fall for on the dance floor.

Poor Russ! I wouldn’t have minded having had her myself for the night but Russ was the kind of fellow that was liable to take a dance like this serious. With a taxi-dancer it’s one fellow tonight and another tomorrow—all they’re after is a man’s dough. But Russ had a kind of old-fashioned idea about women and any young girl, to him, was a starry-eyed virgin who reminded him of his old maid sister who was a county teacher and used to scold kids for writing four-letter words on the rear wall of the school.

There was an intermission and Russ and the girl came in off the floor and he bought a couple of cold drinks at the counter. The Italian went up to them and I guess he wanted the girl but Russ waved a fistful of tickets in the wop’s face and sent him away. The music started again and they began dancing and it wasn’t long after this guy Louie came up to me—bellyaching.

“Beeg fella—’e’s you fran,” he said.

“Yeh.”

“You tell-a ’eem—leave-a my gurl lone.”

“She works here—she’s anybody’s girl. That’s the way I figure it.”

“She’s-a my gurl I tell-a you. She’s-a not like dose oddas. She’s E-tali-yan. Me—I’m E-tali-yan, too. You tell-a ’eem—that beeg-a fella—leave my gurl lone.”

“Aw, climb a tree,” I said. “I can’t help you’re both wops.” With that I walked away. But he kept following me, kept hanging around, kept making me nervous. He was serious.

“Betta you tell ’eem,” he said. “She’s-a my gurl; he’s-a you fran. You tell ’eem.”

“Lay off me,” I said. “Quit following me around. If there’s anything you want told—tell it yourself.”

“Non—non,” he said. “Eez more betta you tell, ’E’s you fran. Me—I mek-a ’eem mad. ’Ee’s mek-a fight me.”

I went to the opposite end of the room where they served the drinks; ordered a ginger ale and sat down at a small table. Before I’d finished, there he was again—right at me.

“I like-a,” he said, “tell-a you sumpt’ing. ’Ee’s you fran, eez not so? She’s-a my gurl. Las’ night I take-a she home. Las’ night ——”

“Love of Mike,” I said, “lay off of me. What do I care if you took her home? What do I care if you jump in the river? Lay off—can’t you?”

I was fed up on him. I was fed up on a lot of things. I got my coat and hat and got out of the place as fast as I could.

Outside, it was snowing—fine, hard snow that filled the air like a dust storm. Traffic on Woodward Avenue had thinned out and was fast so that it was a wicked job getting across the wide street. Every car raked up a little blizzard of white, powdery dust as it passed. Half ; the street lights were turned off for the night, and in the dark of the alleys behind the stores, men with flashlights, and coat-collars turned up, were raiding the cans. Some carried sticks with which to beat off the rats that had got so bold during the hard times that they stood up on their hind legs and fought for choice bits of garbage. In a vacant lot filled with left-over Christmas trees some bums had a fire and a place to sleep and were arguing with a couple of cops who wanted to take the bums in.

“We’re working,” said one of the bums. “You can’t take us in. We got all dees trees to sell yet.”

They had a rough sign up that read:

Bargains in Christmas Trees  
Big Sale.

“What are you tryin’ to give us?” said one of the cops. “Christmas was two weeks ago. You can’t sell these trees.”

“Oh, yeah? We’re runnin’ a sale on ’em. See? We got de price so low dey can’t help but sell. I’m tellin’ ya, officer, yer losin’ money, if yer don’t take one home for de kids.”

“Aw-—cut it out. You fellows just squatted here. You’re just trying to get away with something—that’s what you’re doing. You know it.”

“Naw-—honest to gosh, officers. We’re doin’ business. Look at dis fine tree. Ain’t it a beauty? Was a dollar; I’ll letcher have it for a dime—cash and carry. I’ll even put it in de car for you.”

They were still arguing when I left. I couldn’t stand there all night, waiting to see how it would end up.

For no reason that I can remember I walked past the library, a white building set well back from the street, surrounded by high elms. “Trees in captivity,” Russ had called them once, when he had first come to Detroit. He used to walk out in the evening to see them when there was a fog or a mist, or a fine rain was falling, because then the branches made a soft pattern in the gray light from the high lamps.

I bought a Sunday paper and went up to the room and lay on the bed, trying to read, waiting for Russ to come in. In the magazine section of the paper there was a piece about a song called: “Oh Gloomy Sunday.” The police in some country in Europe were trying to suppress it. The first lines went:

Oh gloomy Sunday—I waited, my dear—  
My arms full of flowers—for you to appear.  
With prayers on my lips, my heart full of dreams;  
I waited alone and in vain it now seems.

There was no heat in the room and it was cold and I threw the paper down to the floor and lay a long time thinking about Russ and the girl and the little Italian and the job that I had in the factory. Sometimes a big city like Detroit is a terrible place and it comes to you, all of a sudden, that the only thing that stands between you and the cold and an empty belly is your little, thin hold on a job that you’ve got no control over and that’s liable to be somebody else’s any morning when you come to the factory; look for your card in the rack and find it’s not there. Nobody has to tell you that it’s up on the superintendent’s desk—punched out for good. Times when I get to feeling like that I pull the blankets up over my head and get to trembling all over. Times like that, even though I’m a-scared of the city, I want to get down into it even closer. I want to become a part of the noise and the speed and the racket so that I can’t think—can’t remember.

When I woke up in the morning Russ was not there. His bed was made up the way the landlady had left it Saturday. He hadn’t slept in it all night.

## Chapter IX

It was late Sunday afternoon when he got back to the room, and because I made a crack he didn’t like about the girl, he blew up and for a month we were like strangers. Not mad at each other, exactly, but at the same time, not friendly. We worked together and talked to each other when we had to, but he went his way and I went mine and neither of us ever so much as mentioned the girl to the other. At night, after work, he’d take a bath and change his clothes and leave the house without telling me where he was going; I wouldn’t ask—I wouldn’t have to. And then when he had gone the room would seem lonely and empty and I wouldn’t know what to do with myself to kill time. I’d go out and walk up and down Hastings street and talk with the niggers; maybe I’d shoot a game of pool at Jim’s Place or go to a movie. Doing those things with him would have been fun but doing them alone—I was bored stiff most of the time. Every time I thought about the girl and how she’s roped him in I got sore.

Sometimes when the weather was too dirty to go out I stayed up in the room and read Mr. Holt’s book. For the first time I began making a real study of it—going through it systematic—learning whole paragraphs by heart. I made neat copies of some of the verses and stuck them up on the walls on my side of the room. One of Mr. Holt’s favorite mottos I pasted onto the edge of the mirror. It read:

Machinery—the new Messiah.

The next morning when I was pulling on my shirt and Russ was combing his hair he saw it there and let out a snort that fairly raised the dust from the floor.

“If you have to stick that sort of thing up here in the room,” he said, “put it where I don’t have to look at it. It’s bad enough up there on the wall without having it here where I have to see it right under my nose every time I use the mirror to comb my hair.”

It had a good effect. It broke down the tension between us and started him talking.

He’d got into the habit of taking the street car to the factory while I’d made it a practice to walk. That morning, after we’d had breakfast, he walked to the factory with me.

“Bennie,” he said, after we’d got a little way from the house, “I’ve got to marry the girl.”

“Already?” I said.

“Already.”

“Well,” I said, “there’re ways of fixing those things. It’s easy enough to get the name of some doc —”

“It’s not what you think,” he said. “It’s not that.”

“Then what is it?” I asked. “Has the Old Man got a shotgun?”

“No. It’s just that I had her figured out wrong. I thought she was a tough little taxi-dancer—ran around with all sorts of men. That dancing dress she had on made her look grown-up and able to take care of herself. And she wasn’t. She’s only seventeen—just a kid. And she was innocent.”

“A hard-boiled virgin, eh, Russ? And you took ad —”

“Be careful,” he said. “I’m talking to you as a friend. She’s not hard-boiled. That’s only put on to stand off the tough guys that she danced with, that bought lunches.”

“She was after you from the start. That first day—she started angling for you.”

“I was after her, too, when I found out that she wasn’t what I first thought she was.”

“That makes it unanimous,” I said.

“It does.”

“Well—it’s your funeral,” I said. “Not mine. Does she still work in that taxi-dance place?”

“I asked her to quit.”

“And she did?”

“Sure.”

“And you’ll give up the idea of clamming?” I said. “Settle down in the shop?”

“No. She’s going with me,” he said. “We’ve talked it over. She thinks the idea is swell but says we oughta get us a trailer to live in.”

“Just wait,” I said. “After the honeymoon is over she’ll forget about the birds and the flowers—she’ll want to get back to the bright lights of Woodward Avenue. She’s like me—brought up among the sights and smells of the back streets of Detroit.”

“You’ve got her wrong, Bennie,” he said. “She’s the real McCoy. And another thing, Bennie—the proposition that I made you about going fifty-fifty still holds. I want you to go.”

“No sale.”

“But I want you to—she does.”

“Count me out,” I said. “I’m not in the mood—even for talking about it.”

We walked along for a block or so, neither one of us saying nothing. Then he spoke again.

“I wouldn’t be surprised,” he said, “if I got into an argument this morning with Johnson.”

“You mean,” I said, “you’re gonna ask for a raise?”

“That’s it. Exactly.”

“When?”

“When we get to the factory.”

“But listen—” I said. “There’s a heavy schedule lined u ”

“So much the better.”

“You’ll get them mad.”

“Let ’em get mad—just so I get the raise. There’s a lot of work ahead of us this morning. I’ll get the raise or walk out. And what’s more—I’ll try to work it so you get one, too.”

“Forget about me,” I said. “All I’m worrying about now, is hanging onto my job. It’s the middle of winter. Jobs are scarcer than hen’s teeth and there’s a big gang outside that narrow gate waiting to step into my place.”

“Don’t worry,” he said. “The time’s right—a lot of work ahead; not another man in the factory can step up on that rig and run it. If Johnson tries it—it’s gonna be just too bad. I’ve got it fixed so he’ll get into plenty of trouble. If I go out, don’t you try it. The static off one of those levers will raise the hat off your head.”

We walked into the factory together; punched our cards and the bell rang and Johnson came up with a fistful of rush orders.

“Right avay first,” he said, “we’ll do some loadin’. Get number one ready ——”

“Before I start,” said Russ, calm and lazy-like, “I’d like a raise.”

“Got no time to talk about raises, now,” said Johnson. “We’re busier than a cat doing a chob on a tin roof.”

“I got time,” said Russ. “Lotsa time. I’ve been here more than six months—still getting five bucks. I want six-eighty. I came here because I heard Mr. Holt was paying high wages. Now I’m gonna find out if it’s so.”

“What’s-a matta,” said Johnson, “tryin’ to go on a strike? Trying to hol’ up the work?”

“I’m not trying to hold up the work,” said Russ. “I’m just trying to make it plain that I’d like a raise. That I’d like to get six-eighty instead of five bucks.”

“Then you’d betta get up on your rig. I’ll talk to Riley when the time comes and see what ’e says. In a couple-a weeks I’ll letcha know.”

“You don’t get the point, Johnson. I want the raise now—not a couple of weeks from today. The idea is that I get the raise or I don’t —”

“Listen—” said Johnson, “don’t say it! Right now raises ain’t going true. I could put in for you—but I’m tellin’n ya—it wouldn’t be no good. We’re layin’ off fellas now. We’re lettin’ ’em go every day, ’stead of handin’ out raises. Times is harder’n ’ell yet and fellas on production is only workin’ tree-four days a week. You’re working five. What you bellyachin’ about?”

“I’ve thought about all those things,” said Russ. “Do I get the raise—or don’t I? If not—I’m ready to turn in my badge.”

“Holy smokes,” said Johnson, “I can’t getcha no raise—the way yer’re going at it! You can’t bulldoze the Holt Motor Company! There’s regular ways for doing dose t’ings. I gotta put in for you and wait to see what dey say. Maybe it goes true—maybe it don’t. See? You ain’t big enough to lay down the law to the Holt Motor Company and get away with it. See?”

“Yeh, I see. But I am big enough to tell the Holt Motor Company to stick their job up if I have to work for five bucks a day. I’m just trying to make it plain that I don’t pull a lever on that rig until I get six-eighty.”

Johnson was in a tight spot. Three furnaces were full of ripe steel. There were a whole mess of orders ahead. And not another man in the shop—not Johnson himself—had brains enough to climb up on the manipulator and run it. Yet he didn’t want to give the raise under pressure.

“Listen,” said Johnson, “let’s you’n me cut out this arguin’ togedda. If I had my way I’d get you a raise—it’s no money out of my pocket. If I had it to say I’d get you a raise every week. But you can see how it is. You can see how it would look to Riley if I got you a raise and not de oddas. He’d t’ink we was buddies. You know what ’e’ll say to me—doncha? ’E’ll say ‘what’s-a matta, Johnson, you and that fella buddies togedda? You roomin’ togedda, or sumpt’ing?’ See? An’ ’e’ll say, “Whyn’t you get anodda fella for that job, Johnson? Whyn’t you fire that long-legged, no-account sojer—get rid of ’im?’ Tha’s what ’e’ll tell me. And you know what I’ll hafta do, doncha? I’m gonna hafta fire you to make ’im t’ink we ain’t chums; that it ain’t sumpt’ing between us. That’s what I’ll hafta do—see?”

“Don’t make me laugh,” said Russ. “If I don’t get the raise you won’t have to fire me. I’ll save you the trouble. And as for me being the only one—that reminds me. I want a raise for Bennie. He’s been here as long as I have.”

“Holy smokes, man! What’s the matta with you? You talk crazy. You want everybody get fired? I can’t do nothin’ for you. See Riley.”

“O-keh,” said Russ, walking away. “But let me tell you—I’m not afraid to speak up. And if I tell Riley things you’d rather not have me say—don’t say I went over your head.”

“Wait,” said Johnson, “I’ll get ’im. You stay here. We’ll have it out. If he wants to give you the raise—it’s none of my business. But I’ll hafta tell ’im you ain’t been co-operatin’.” Johnson left to get Riley.

“Well,” I said, picking up the rags I’d been using to swipe with and putting them into the can, “might just as well start getting our tools together. In about ten minutes we’ll be drawing our last pay.”

“Leave it to me. If I go it doesn’t mean you’ll get fired.”

“For the love of Mike,” I said, “go easy. You can’t get hard-boiled with Riley.”

“I don’t want to get hard-boiled with anybody. I’m just trying to make it plain that I get a raise or I don’t work. I’ve got ’em over a barrel and I’m going to crack down on ’em. If they don’t come across it’s gonna cost them plenty of dough and they know it.”

Johnson came back with Riley.

“What’s the argument?” said Riley.

“No argument,” said Russ. “I want a raise. I’ve been here more than six months. I know my job; I work hard; I don’t back down when it comes to facing hot iron. During the last three months not a job has gone back to the fire for re-heat. Working hard; putting myself into it—not watching the clock. I figure I’ve helped the Company make a good deal of money. I figure I’m worth ten dollars a day. I’m not asking ten—I’m asking six-eighty.”

“What about your helper?” asked Riley. “Six dollars o-keh?”

“You bet it is, sir,” I said. “Six dollars is fine, sir. I would appreciate it.” It was the first time I’d ever talked to Riley.

“Better let them have it,” he said to Johnson. “They’re in line for it.” It was simple—like that.

A couple of weeks later, walking home from the factory, Russ told me the news. He’d got married. And not only that, but he and Rita had rented a house and on the way home nothing would do but what we had to get off the street car, walk three or four blocks, and have a look at it. Rita was there with her kid brother, painting some second-hand chairs, and when we came in she ran up to Russ with the brush in her hand and got yellow paint all over the back of his coat.

The house wasn’t much. Not very big and there was a bulge in the floor so that two legs of the stove had to be propped up on one side to make it stand level, but there were electric lights and running water. There were only three rooms. Two bedrooms, one on each end, and a large room in between that would have to take the place of a dining room, living room, kitchen, and everything else. In this room there was a table, some half-painted chairs, a place to wash dishes and an old couch covered with bright new cloth. Rita was full of excitement. They were both happy as kids.

“Wait till I get the curtains up,” she said. “You won’t know the place. No house is a home without curtains—and yet if a man was doing it, that’s the last thing he’d think about.”

“Sure,” said Russ, “a man would want to look out of the windows. Look at the view, Bennie.”

I looked out of the back window and there behind the house, less than a stone’s throw from the back door, was a small marsh with a few cat-tails; quantities of old automobile bodies; broken bathtubs; rusty boilers and broken pieces of concrete. On the opposite side there was a row of tar-paper shacks and in between there was such a mess of rubbish and junk that you could hardly see the mud and black water. In the broken-down fence there was an old willow with broken branches and scarred trunk that leaned towards the house as though trying to get away from the sights and the smells of the swamp.

“In the spring,” said Russ, “we’ll be able to shoot mud-hens from the back door.”

“And every night,” said Rita, “I’ll have a bouquet of pussywillows on the table. The old tree looks dead—but it’s not. There’s life in it, Nito tells me.” Nito was the kid brother who was supposed to be such a fighter.

“And did you notice?” she asked Russ. “There’s a lilac bush near the front gate.”

“I didn’t,” said Russ. “In fact, I hadn’t noticed the gate. Unless —”

She laughed.

“I mean,” she said, “where the gate would be if there was a gate.”

“You mean if the kids hadn’t taken the gate off the hinges and used it for a raft. I think I saw it stranded out there in the swamp. Oh well—the shack is only costing us twelve-fifty a month.” They were gay about everything.

And all the time they were talking and planning I was thinking how gloomy it would be with no one to talk to at night—no one to argue with. And on Saturday nights, when I’d want to go out, I’d have to go all by myself. And not sharing the room with anyone else Mrs. Prouse, the landlady, would raise the rent on me, or else get in somebody I wouldn’t like. Somebody, like as not, on the night shift, who’d come in and go out at the most ungodly hours, or sit up maybe half the night with the lights blazing just when I’d be trying to catch up on sleep.

Russ told her about us getting the raise and she gave him a hug and a kiss that would have made a bachelor like me happy for life.

“Now we’ll be able to get the rug that I wanted,” she said. “And the lamp-shade for Bennie.”

“Hey! Hey!” he said. “You’re spilling the beans.”

“May as well tell,” I said. “What is it?”

“Shall we?” she asked.

“Might as well,” said Russ. “What’s the diff?”

“Well,” she said, “we’ve got a room fixed up for you. We thought maybe we could get you to move in with us. Be our star boarder.”

“I couldn’t think of it,” I said. “You’ll be honeymooning the first couple of years. I’d be always under your feet. Be getting in the way every time you’d want to look at the moon or see the sun set.”

“Yeh,” said Russ. “That would be bad. Those sunsets over that row of old shacks must be pretty swell. But kidding aside, Bennie—we mean it. Two reasons we’d like to have you stay here. One, it’d be lonesome without you, and another—we need your board money to help run the house on. However, we’d only charge you six a week instead of the seven you’re paying old lady Prouse. Seven’s too much. It’s like Johnson says, “Times is harder’n ’ell yet.’”

“He hasn’t seen the room yet,” said Rita. “How do you know he’ll like it?”

“I don’t,” said Russ. “If I knew he liked it I wouldn’t be wasting all this salesmanship.”

Rita opened a door and I saw the room. It was pretty small but had a window and a small heating stove. There were some yellow drapes in front of the window, a bed and a chair, and in one corner, behind a curtain that slid on a wire, a place to hang clothes. Near the bed was a waste-paper basket with some blue ribbon.

“It has the feminine touch,” said Russ. “How do you like it?”

“Swell.”

“It’s all yours—on one condition.”

“What’s that?”

“That you stick a Holt motto over every one of those cracks in the wall.”

“That’s easy,” I said. “I’ve got enough to cover the walls. When do I move in?”

“Saturday.”

“And what about the outboard?” I asked. “Shall we turn it in on a new baby carriage?”

“Not by a long shot,” he said. “The outboard comes here. It’s all we’ve got. That and about ten dollars worth of used furniture.”

“We’ve got to keep it,” she said. “I’ll want to say to Junior, when he gets big, ‘Junior—all your father and I had when we got married, was an outboard.’ ”

I said, “Still got your mind set on clamming, eh Russ?”

“Why not? Getting married makes it all the more logical. Now we’ve got someone to do the cooking and keep house for us.”

“We’ve talked it all over,” said Rita. “He told me all about it—even before we were married. We’re all going to sit around the fire after dark while you play the guitar.” She danced across the floor, singing a tune —

She shall have music,  
Wherever she goes,  
Rings on her fingers,  
And bells on her toes.

“From the dance hall,” she said.

The kid had started a fire in the old stove. We stood around it.

“Listen,” said Russ. “Where we going to stow the outboard?”

“Is it big?” she asked.

“About as big as a suitcase—a little longer, perhaps.”

“There’s a shed in the back,” she said. “There’s some wood in it. We could put it in there.”

“Too risky,” he said. “Some kid would kipe it. We’ll have to stick it under the bed. Can’t take no chances.”

“Ts it smelly?”

“Not bad.”

“But the dust. How will I be able to sweep under the bed?”

“Sweep around it,” he said. “I’ll pull it out once in awhile—help you.”

It was beginning to get dark. Mrs. Prouse would be getting supper.

“I’ll be going, now,” I said. “Be seeing you later.”

“Tell the Old Girl,” said Russ, “that I’m eating out. I’ll be in after while.”

“Nito,” said Rita, “you run along, too. Tell Ma I’ll be home after supper.”

When I left they were standing on the rickety porch with their arms around each other. They looked and acted mighty affectionate, but they were still talking about where to put the outboard. She said something about getting a lock for the shed; he still insisted on putting it under the bed. Well, all I’ve got to say is that if I ever get married—which I wouldn’t be surprised that I would, now that I’m making six bucks a day—I’ll be blamed if I’ll have any smelly gas motors under the bed until the honeymoon is old stuff. Over at the house the thing stunk up the whole room.

## Chapter X

Living together in the little old house we got along fine. Once in a while the three of us would take in a movie, or Russ and I would shoot a few games of pool, but for the most part we worked hard, stayed home, and sat around the fire and talked shop. There was no tearing around—no taxi-dancing and no hanging around beer joints at night. Sometimes after supper we’d sit around the table and play cards and maybe Russ or I would tell Rita about something that had happened in the factory that day. Sometimes, before washing the dishes, even, she’d go over and sit on his lap and start fooling around with his shirt collar or rub his whiskers with the back of her hand. I could see how it was.

“Well—” I’d say, getting up and stretching my arms, “I guess I’ll go into my room. I’ve gotta write a fellow a letter tonight about a dog.”

Sometimes I’d get on my hat and go out to Jim’s Place and talk to the fellows awhile, or maybe just hang around there and do nothing. Getting home the house would be dark and the dirty dishes still on the table. I’d go to bed and then maybe two-three o’clock in the morning I’d hear them kidding and laughing out there in the room like a couple of kids. They’d fry some eggs, or cook some spaghetti maybe; wash the dishes and then turn around and go back to bed. If they were with each other they were happy and nothing else mattered. She ran the house and knew how to hang onto the pennies, while he talked big about saving money, but if he had a couple of bucks in his pocket he figured they didn’t count.

All through the winter Russ was steady and regular but when the spring came he began talking about clamming. And then in May he told me that Rita was in a family way and that they were expecting a kid in November. That should have made him serious about his job and his work, but it didn’t.

One morning, on the way to the factory, the windows on the street car were up and he kept sniffing the air like a hungry pup when there’s a soup bone a-cooking. The wind was soft and it came in off the lake and there’d been a warm rain in the night and all of a sudden he punched the button and got up.

“Tell ’em I won’t be there today,” he said, and got off.

On a job like his, to lay off, unless you’re sick, is pretty darn serious. The safety rules say “Willful absence from work will not be tolerated.”

That morning there was work to be done and Johnson was madder than a wet owl and he called up the front and they sent out a car with two H.P.’s to investigate. They found him. Not at the house, sick in bed; but in the marsh, sitting on a log whittling a stick and watching a couple of blackbirds building a nest.

That night in my room, where he’d sometimes come to sit after supper, I said:

“Russ, the trouble with you is you’re too independent. You spit where you see a no-spitting sign; walk on the grass where it tells you to keep off; rub when you’re told to blot and blot when it says rub. You’re so contrary that if Mr. Holt wanted you to vote for his cousin for mayor, you’d just as likely as not vote for Tom Mooney. There’s too much of the old devil in you. Unless you turn over a new leaf and learn to take orders—some day you’ll find yourself out of a job.”

“I was thinking this morning,” he said, ignoring what I was trying to tell him, “that it will soon be summer again. By the first of July we’ll have three hundred dollars saved up between the two of us. What say we start about then?”

“I’ve been thinking it over,” I said. “Clamming’s pretty uncertain.”

“Uncertain?” he said. “What’s more uncertain than working in Mr. Holt’s factory? When he gets up for breakfast tomorrow he’s liable to get a couple of eggs that are too soft, get on a grouch, and shut the whole place down for six months. Then where would we be? We’d be nowhere. With sixty-seventy thousand men on the streets looking for jobs we’d be so far on the outside we couldn’t even get a look in through the gate. Clamming is less of a gamble. Besides—it’s more fun.”

“There’s other things in life,” I said, “besides having fun.”

“What are they?”

“Well, to have a house of your own—go out riding on Sunday in a nice car—have a vegetable garden —”

“Have to be a vegetable garden? No flowers?”

“Well, a vegetable garden’s more practical. Can’t eat flowers, you know.”

“Right from the book, ain’t it, Bennie? Listen, Bennie,” he said, “how often have I told you to throw that book in the fire? That book will ruin you if you take - it serious. It’s full of ideas that won’t work for you—won’t work for anybody unless they’re billionaire auto manufacturers out to get the last ounce of flesh from every man in the shop. Why, Bennie, ideas like that are positively dangerous, once they get loose. They’ll be the ruination of us working men if we don’t put them down.”

“Put them down? Hear you talk—you’d think Mr. Holt was a communist or something.”

“He’s worse. Take that motto there on the wall—‘WORK HARD AND BEHAVE YOURSELF.’ It’s all right if you own the factory. But if you’re a working man—don’t behave yourself ever, unless you want to be a machine tender all of your life. Take your own case. You work hard, behave yourself, save a little money, and get laid off when the machine catches up. When you’re dead broke you get on again—work hard—save a little money again, and get laid off again when the machine does. And you always get laid off because the machine goes to beat the band and always catching up.”

“But I always get on again.”

“And you always get laid off again. In the end you’re always just where you started. You don’t have as much fun as the niggers. They’re always broke, too, but at least they have fun as long as the pay checks are coming in and the finance company lets them tear around in new Holts.”

“It’s the yearly models,” I said. “They have to shut down.”

“Yearly models,” he said. “Yearly models—me eye. They shut down because they want to throw us out of the shop. The whole country would be better off if they didn’t have yearly models. They would, themselves. That yearly model stuff gives ’em an excuse to flood the whole country with cars and then close up and kick the men out. If they didn’t have some excuse the government would mighty soon do something about it. They ought to, anyhow. It’s ruining the country. You’ve been in the factory a long time. How often have you seen Mr. Holt in the shop?”

“Twice.”

“Twice. What was he doing? Working? Answer me that.”

“Well—no,” I said. “Not exactly. Once he came through with a fellow from the movies. Another time with an English Duke or something. Mr. Holt’s too big to work in the factory. He’s above that. But even though he’s not there—even though you don’t see him—you feel his spirit. Anyone that’s worked in the shop a long time will tell you it’s as if he was watching you—as if he was standing there looking over your shoulder. All the old fellows feel that way—all the fellows that’s trying to work up.”

“Work up to what?”

“Straw bosses. I feel that way myself. I put an oil can up on the shelf and I try to set it there the way Mr. Holt would. When I stick a bunch of rags in the waste bucket, I try to do it the way Mr. Holt would do it himself.”

“And what does it get you?”

“Maybe I’ll get to be a straw boss some day.”

“And if you do, when the shut-down comes—you’ll get laid off with the others.”

“Maybe. But I’ll have a good record. I’ll get on again. The trouble with you is, Russ, you’ve never learned to take orders. What you need is a spell on the assembly line where some other fellow throws in the switch and you do the work and the electric motors set the pace for you—tell you when to start—when to stop—and when to speed up. Electric motors you can’t argue with and can’t shut off when you want to go to the toilet, and a straw boss that’s not in the habit of talking very much but takes you for a walk and lets you have a long look at the string of men at the employment gate when you get behind in your work and hold up the line.”

“That reminds me,” he said, “of an old song we used to sing in the woods:

I shave my whiskers like a man,  
Use a larder’s pail, or an old tin can  
To lather up my grizzled pan,  
Then give me an axe, with a blade that’s true,  
A double-bitted axe, that’s bright and new,  
And a hack and a whack, and then I’m through.

“I’ve shaved with a double-bitted axe, Bennie. Give me a double-bitted axe and I can hold up my end any time—with any man—on any line. If you think it’s easy to shave with an axe—try it some time.”

Sometimes when we’d get to arguing that way his talk didn’t hardly make sense.

## Chapter XI

A couple of weeks later, on account of his hot-headed temper, Russ got himself into a mess. Blame it all, I had to lie to beat the band to get him clear.

Herman, the fellow who ran the overhead crane, chewed tobacco.

You’d see him up there near the ceiling coasting along in his iron cage with his head sticking out, a cud in his cheek the size of a duck egg, and every once in a while sending a long, thin squirt down to the floor. He hardly ever hit anybody and almost never hit Russ’ rig, but just the same Russ was worried. At night, after work, he’d go around the machine, looking it over.

“What’s that?” he’d say. “Looks mighty suspicious to me.”

“Aw, get out,” I’d say. “It’s a little iron rust or some water.”

From day to day he worked himself up about it something fierce and nothing I said made any difference. And then one day he sent in a long complaint to the safety department, and the next day, after lunch, two safety engineers came to the floor. They got Johnson and called Russ down from the rig.

“What’s this,” they said, “about you being liable to kill somebody on account of the overhead man chewing tobacco?” They had Russ’ letter.

“Well,” said Russ, “I work on this rig. The man’s up there over my head. You can see what the situation is.”

There was Herman, his head sticking out of the cage a foot, a cud in the side of his face, looking down and wondering for all he was worth what the deuce the pow-wow down below was all about. In a minute or so he spit, in a sort of meditative way, and a brown stream of tobacco juice sailed through the air, flattened out and broke up in a dozen long smears.

“It’s just luck,” said Russ, “I wasn’t there that time. Suppose I had been and a swab of that hit me in the eye. And suppose Johnson here, was standing there under my rig. I’m handling a big lug of steel—I can’t see for a minute—lose control—hit the wrong lever. I drop the load and kill Johnson. It would cost the Company some dough, wouldn’t it?”

Johnson laughed a thin little laugh.

“He’s neva hit you yet, has he?”

“No,” said Russ. “And I’ve never killed you yet, either, have I?”

“No —”

“Well, there’s always the first time.”

The safety men looked serious.

“We oughta do something,” said one. “What would you suggest?” he asked Johnson.

“Tell ’im to be a little more careful,” said Johnson. “I’ll see this fella don’t kill me.”

Johnson, himself, chewed tobacco. In fact, he was practically never without a chew and even as he talked he jerked his head around every once in a while on the sly and spit to one side. But not being up in the air fifty feet he wasn’t conspicuous about it like Herman.

“There’s a chance there for an accident all right,” said one of the men. “It’s our business to catch things like that before somebody gets hurt.”

“Somebody’s liable to get hurt,” said Russ. “And if they do, you’ll have to admit I gave warning.”

“What would you suggest?” said the other.

“Suggest? I wouldn’t suggest. I’d tell him. I’d tell him to stop chewing.”

“We can’t pick on him.”

“Why not? You’d pick on me if I smoked. If I smoked my card would be pulled in a minute. Smoking’s no worse than chewing.”

“Depends on how you look at it.”

“Well,” said Russ, “if I had a job in the safety department I’d look at it the way Mr. Holt looks at it. Mr. Holt hates tobacco. He’d put a stop to it while we’re standing here arguing.”

The safety men left and went to see Riley. They were pretty serious about something that didn’t amount to a darn.

“Of course,” I said to Russ, “it wasn’t yourself you were worried about.”

“I suppose not,” he said. “I could put on a raincoat and wear goggles, but that wouldn’t protect Old Betsy.”

“What’s the diff? I’m the swipe. You’re making a big stir about nothing.”

“Not exactly,” he said. “Maybe I’m a little sentimental but I don’t want anybody spitting tobacco juice on Old Betsy nohow at no time.”

A couple of days later a general order came through from the safety department. There was to be no more tobacco chewing by crane operators while on duty. Herman was sore as a boil. He knew who was to blame. So did forty-fifty other crane men after Herman made the rounds.

A little thing like chewing tobacco don’t mean much to you and me, but to a crane man, sitting up there in his cage the long day, with nothing to do except pulling the same little levers and thinking what he’d do to the hooker if he got the chance and never being able to do it, it’s different. For him, it breaks the monotony and makes up for not having anyone to talk to and not being allowed to cuss out at the men on the floor. It’s something to smooth out the little irritations that come up every day. Besides, it gives him a chance to express his feelings when he gets mad at the hooker and gets all steamed up inside like a boiler. Give a crane man a sizeable chew and he can express himself one hundred percent in the way he lets fly at the hooker or a dumb loading man down on the floor.

As I said, Herman was sorer than a mashed thumb. And he took it out on Russ by starting to swing in his loads. All that afternoon he kept swinging closer, and once, when Johnson wasn’t on the floor, he pulled one straight over the rig. Russ got up on his ear, and that night, after Herman climbed down from the crane, Russ warned him again.

“I’m telling you once more,” he said. “Keep your loads in the clear. Swing another load over my head and I’ll reach up there and pull you and your cage down to the floor so quick you won’t know what’s happened.”

“Boosh-wah,” said Herman. “Tell it to the safety committee, why doncha?”

I had a chance to talk to Herman at the tool crib.

“Better take it serious,” I said. “When he gets his hands on them levers—he’ll do anything—he ain’t human.”

“That’s all right,” he said. “Me—I’m hot-headed, too. If he wants to start something—I’m for it.”

The next day it happened. It was about three in the afternoon. Johnson was up at the desk talking to Riley. Russ had a piece of cold stuff and was getting in position to load number three. I had the air off and was raising the door when Herman came down the bay with a stack of front axle blanks, three-four tons of them. The load came down the bay in a straight line for Russ. I figured Herman was going to throw a little scare into Russ—figured he’d come up close and then swing his load to the other side of the bay. But he didn’t. He came straight on—the load swinging a little at the end of the long cables. He was close—getting closer—without cutting speed. Thirty feet—twenty—coming straight over. But no—Russ dropped his load to the floor and shot backwards. He stopped; raised his boom like a snake getting ready to strike and stood his ground. Herman came straight into it. Russ shot out his boom, the iron claw open for business, smashed into Herman’s load, and the front axle blanks clattered and rained to the floor. The racket was something terrific. Iron chips flew in every direction; dust rose thick from the floor; scalers and hammer men scattered. I ducked for a pillar.

The fight was on. Herman rolled back and pulled his hooks up—got into position. Now they were empty—ready to hit. Russ drew in his boom like a rattler getting ready to strike. The big claw snapped open—aimed at the cage—came at Herman. But Herman struck first—dropped his hooks like a swinging plumet in a long curve. It was a good shot. He got a grappling hold on the undercarriage of the rig. He drew tight. A couple of pulls and he’d turn the rig over. But Russ was too quick. He slashed his boom around against the steel cables and wiped himself clear. Snarling steel rope filled the air. In a wild swing Herman’s hooks struck the top of a furnace. Bricks rained to the floor—dust was thick enough to walk on. Somewhere a steam line let go. Noise was terrific.

Herman shot back and rolled in his line. Came in again, maneuvering for a direct overhead drop, trying to keep Russ on the defensive. But Russ went at him. He shot out his boom—full length—straight from the cage. Herman kicked back—not a second too soon. Another two feet and Russ would have had him down on the floor. Herman dropped the hooks; Russ struck as they fell and sent them spinning, and they crashed against the big hammer. But they were open and on the rebound they hit the undercarriage of the rig—closed up and held. Herman gave her the juice—all he had. His lines tightened. Once more Russ tried to slash himself clear—struck out. It was no good. The steel lines tightened still more. Russ swung his boom—raised and lowered—trying to cut through. Fire leaped from the cables as steel sheared on steel. No use. The rig—one side—began to raise from the floor. He’d go over! To save himself he aimed his boom for the crane—shot out to full length—clamped onto the bridge. It held. Herman was powerless to move him. The two machines were deadlocked to a standstill.

It was then I thought about throwing out the master switches like I’m supposed to do in emergencies. Running through the white fog of dust from the wrecked furnace I nearly knocked over Johnson.

“What’s the matta?” he hollered, eyes sticking out a foot. I kept a-going.

Both switches were up there on the wall pillar and I cut Herman out first, and when I did, Russ settled back to the floor.

And then I got busy. Fellows were running all around, looking up, still not sure whether the building would come down or not. Scalers with brick dust on their clothes began showing up on the floor looking scared.

I went up to a couple.

“Listen,” I said. “You fellows. I don’t care who you’re for. But if one gets fired—they both go. We gotta make them think it was an accident. I’ll do the talking.”

And that’s how it come about that I had to do some powerful lying. Blame it all—I don’t like to tell such tall lies. Mr. Holt is against lying. But what could I do? There was Johnson and he had the two men down on the floor, and there was Riley and a whole bunch of others and they wanted to know everything. And after I got started, there was no way of changing my story—no place to stop; and I got in deeper and deeper; and I told them that the two machines had just accidentally come together, and that in trying to untangle they had got scrambled up something awful. Russ was still fighting mad, but looking at him you might have thought that he was just scared, and I was afraid that he would lay into Herman right there while I was talking, though a fist-fight, I guess, would have been pretty tame after the way they had been going at it. And there were the two machines, still standing there with death grips on each other, in what you might call a compromising position—axle blanks and broken brick scattered all over the floor and smoke pouring from the hole in the furnace. If Johnson or Riley had had a lick of sense in their heads they wouldn’t have had to ask any question, but what it came down to, I guess, is what I told Russ. They just couldn’t imagine that there would ever be two fellows so dumb and hot-headed that they would try to run off a fight with a bridge crane and a manipulator.

The pow-wow ended up with me getting it in the neck from Johnson.

“When you saw what was happening,” he said, “why’n-cha do sumpt’ing? Why’n-cha t’row out the switch right away, ’stead of standin’ there with your hands in your pockets?”

## Chapter XII

In May, times were still bad in Detroit, and things didn’t pick up like everybody said in the winter. Factories that were running four days a week cut to three; and those that were running three, cut to two; and some closed down altogether under the excuse of changing models or taking inventories. When business is bad, taking inventory can sure keep an automobile factory closed down a long time. Gangs of men loafed around the street corners and in the barber shops, and rats started coming out again in broad daylight looking for garbage, not waiting for dark and the stiff competition the men out of work gave them. You could pick up a good-looking dame on the streets just for buying her supper.

Mr. Holt made a statement in the newspapers. “We’ll take it easy,” he said, “and get ready.”

“That’s an idea for us,” I said to Russ, one night after supper. “We’ll just go along easy and steady and hang onto our jobs.”

“You’re all wet,” he said. “When things look bad, take the bull by the tail. Don’t wait until he gets his horns into you. I’m just waiting to catch Johnson with a heavy schedule. I figure we’ve got another raise coming.”

And the very next day things happened. All that day we’d been working along same as usual and there was nothing to show that anything was different, or that we wouldn’t be on the same job a long time, but after the bell Johnson called Russ and me over and gave us a couple of slips.

“Take these,” he said, “and go up to the transfer department and see if ya can get yaself a couple new jobs. I can’t use-ya no more.”

“What’s the matter?” said Russ.

“It’s nottin’ the matta,” said Johnson. “It’s just what I’m tellin’ ya—I can’t use-ya no more.”

“Why not?”

“Cause I can’t. We got a new way figured out for making dies. We ain’t gonna forge ’em no more.”

Johnson was fooling around with some blue-prints—filing some away—throwing some in the can.

“Who’s gonna run the rig?” asked Russ.

“Nobody.”

“‘What’s gonna happen to it?”

“They’ll feed it to the open-heart’s. Melt it up and make some new Holts. In a couple days you’ll see it standin’ out there in the bone-yard. The big hammer goes too. No more poundin’ and bangin’. It ain’t civilized to make dies like that.”

“Ain’t civilized? How are you gonna make dies if you don’t forge them?”

“We’re gonna type ’em.”

“Type them?”

“That’s what I said.”

“It sounds crazy.”

“Might be it sounds crazy to you.”

Johnson went on with his work.

“But listen, Johnson—in typing you use cast ingots.”

“What of it? You don’t have to pound and bang around and play circus in front of a bunch of visitors with a piece of steel to make it tough. It’s fun for you, maybe, but it’s old fashioned. It ain’t civilized, I tell you.”

“But how are you going to make dies without forging? How are you gonna make ’em tough and hard—to stand up?”

“What’s gotta be harder’n a camshaft for a Holt car?”

“I don’t know.”

“And what’s gotta be tougher’n a crankshaft?”

“I don’t know. Why?”

“Well—I’m just askin’ to see if ya know. Why’n-cha keep on your toes? Know what’s going on in the shop? We ain’t forging camshafts no more. Neither crankshafts. We’re castin’ ’em. Now what d’ya tink about that? The first ones came t’rue two mont’s ago. I could-a tol’ you then—your job here was blowed up.”

“I still think it sounds crazy.”

“And I still t’ink it don’t make no difference what you t’ink.”

“But what’s to hinder them breaking—wearing out?”

“If I tell you—you’ll keep you mout’ shut?”

“Sure.”

“Well, a little copper in steel makes it tough. A little carbon makes it hard. But how much to put in—that’s sumpt’ing I wouldn’t dast to tell.”

“But why junk the rig?”

“It’s absolete, I tol’ ya. Odda companies use old fashion tools. We can’t afford to.”

“Then why not sell it?”

“Because the Holt Motor Company ain’t in the second-hand business.”

Johnson put on his coat.

“Betta get going,” he said. “They’ll be closin’ up.” Then he left.

I had to wash up and when I got back to the floor Russ was fooling around with the rig. He had a piece of rag in his hand—too small to do any good—and pretended to be swiping.

“Don’t wait for me,” he said. “I’ll be along later.”

But I didn’t go and while waiting for him I went over some old clothes in my locker, throwing some away, saving others; and while doing this, I watched him, and what he was doing was sort of saying farewell to the rig. His big hands curved over the brasses, his fingers touching and feeling—the tips of his fingers sensing the feel of the metal that was still warm from the heat of the day’s work. Along the smooth shafts his hands slid, gentle and yet strong in their motions, firm and gentle, flowing along the warm steel. Continually in motion, they were—always exploring, except when they rested a moment cupped over a journal, or hiding the soft bronze of a bearing. Always in motion, over the rounded shells of the motors—along the easy curves of gear-housings—on bare iron, along naked steel; always exploring—always caressing and feeling and seeming to draw something from the warm steel. He climbed to the controls to the brass controls, and let his hands flow over the levers—levers that his hands knew so well; touching each one—sliding his hands along the smooth metal—feeling them—understanding them with the tips of his fingers. And then down again, reaching a hand intimately into a nest of warm gears, leaning the while against the base of the machine, one hand exploring and touching, the other hand pressed against the bare, unpainted steel.

“Come on,” I said. “Let’s beat it. They’ll be closed up before we get there if we don’t get a move on.”

He came out of it then, and we left, and it was a long way to walk—past the rolling mills, the hospital, the final assembly, and the foundry. The night shift was on and the Cooper-Hewitts were burning, throwing a grey-lavender light that made a man’s face look like a corpse. A ship lay at the side of the dock, unloading. The spot lights turned on her deck, and the Hullett unloader, like a giant praying mantis, unbuckled and bent and reached down deep into the hold and lifted out trailing buckets of ore. Straining locomotives shifted cars of red, molten metal that lit up the sky, and high overhead, conveyors carried endless streams of rough parts and castings, finished parts and new motors, still wet with paint, from one part of the plant to another. From the river a low fog crept in and a cloud of steam from the quenching tower dimmed the high lights and spread like a blanket over the big plant.

At the foundry we stopped in a minute and spoke to one of the bosses and asked if it was true that crankshafts for the new motors were being cast. He said they were and pointed out a section of the building given over to that special work. Here the men were all Negroes and the night shift was on and they were pouring. It was line work—a continuous process, going night and day. The molds were set endwise on L-type conveyors, each one receiving a charge of the hot metal as it moved past the furnace. There was no secret about it, the man said; the metal used being a high carbon, copper, silchrome steel; carbon, 1.5%; copper, 1.75%; chromium, .5%; and manganese, .7%. It worked out so well, the man said, that they were able to make each crankshaft fifteen pounds lighter than when they were forged, and the machine operations were cut down from sixty to fifty.

After we left the building Russ said, “We’re not the only ones. A lot of good mechanics got laid off or transferred when that bunch of cotton-pickers went to work on that line.”

“And to think of it,” I said. “They’re doing a better job of it. If they can turn out crankshafts like that I’m ready to believe anything Johnson tells me about making dies. Engineers are sure smart.”

“Smarter than we are,” said Russ.

In the transfer department they were getting ready to close up for the night. A clerk took our slips and looked at them.

“Don’t you fellows know,” he said, “we close at five?”

“How long does it take?” I asked. He didn’t answer. It was still not a quarter to five.

He went to the files; looked at a couple of cards, and came back.

“What have you got for us?” I said.

“Motor assembly,” he answered, starting to fill out some cards. “You’ll have to turn in your badges. Get new ones tomorrow.”

“But listen,” I said. “This fellow here’s a mechanic. He can run anything that’s got wheels. Ain’t you got something more in his line?”

He stopped writing and kept looking at the point of his pencil with a painful expression.

“Listen, pal,” he said, “I ain’t got time to stand here arguing with you all night. We close at five. You don’t have to take it—only make up your mind.”

“Aw, what’s the use arguing with the fellow,” said Russ. “If he’s got something else and won’t give it to us—what’s the diff?”

The clerk finished making out the cards and we left. It was later than usual when we went out the gate. The day men had gone and there was a thin crowd around a medicine peddler, dressed like an Indian chief, trying to sell some kind of dope he said was a sure cure for oil dermatitis, a skin disease that was common among the men who worked on the automatics. We walked. Russ was quiet and gloomy and didn’t say very much and I guess I rambled on just to make talk.

“After all,” I said, “we didn’t get put on ‘blue mass’—that’s something. On motor assembly a good man can take it. After all, a man’s got to get out the work. If he didn’t, Mr. Holt couldn’t pay the wages he does. Others are only paying three-fifty—some even less.”

Motor assembly was only working four days a week. There was talk of cutting to three. And there, we’d get cut back to five dollars a day, but even that’s better than three-fifty.

“And then another thing,” I said, “in motor assembly I know the bosses. I worked there before. Maybe they’ll put me on again as helper. That’s a job that puts you right next to the bosses. It’s not the same as being a helper in the drop forge. You break in new men; stand in for fellows that want to go to the toilets. But you can’t tell. Can’t tell, either, what they’ll give you. Maybe you’ll get something pretty soft—like splitting cotter pins. All you do is stand there with a pair of pliers and split the keys open—no responsibility, no worry, no nothing. Maybe it’ll be just putting on a gasket as the motors go by. It’s a cinch it won’t be nothing hard like the job you had in the drop forge where you had to be on your toes—getting it in the neck when things went wrong—thinking all the time—figuring out all the time what to do next. There’s no jobs there—not like that—not in motor assembly. I know.”

All the time he walked along as though he didn’t hear me.

“If you get to work on a machine,” I said, “it’ll be an automatic, working on cylinder blocks, boring cylinders, cutting valve seats, or drilling push-rod holes—something like that. All you do is put the stuff in, press a lever, take the stuff out, and put it back on the conveyor. The machine checks its own work—fastens the piece in—won’t start if you get it in wrong; stops if anything goes hay-wire. The machine you had in the drop forge didn’t have any brains—you had to do all the thinking. There it’s different. There the machines have more brains than some of the fellows that run ’em.”

Still I didn’t get a word out of him.

“Of course,” I said, “there’s some things in motor assembly you won’t like, but you’ll get used to ’em. The standing in line: you stand in line at the tool crib—to punch in—to punch out; when you go to the toilet you have to stand in line for your turn. There’s fifteen hundred men to the acre. In the drop forge we had the whole place to ourselves. In motor assembly you’ll get your own little square to stand on—you have to watch your elbows. Machines all around you: fifty, maybe hundred doing the same work, making the same noise, making the same number of revolutions a second. When there’s so many—when they all run at the same speed it sort of gives you the wobblies till you get used to it. After a-while the vibrations get inside of you—under your skin. First you don’t like it—you fight it. I did. But after a while you get so you time yourself with the noise and go along with it the same as the others. It’s not bad if you know how to take it—let yourself go along with it. Don’t try to run things.”

Still he didn’t say a word.

The fog came in off the lake like a rolling breaker, and yet when we reached the house it wasn’t quite dark. Light enough, it was, to see the outboard laying there in the wet air on the rickety front porch. Rita opened the door and the first thing she said was:

“I’m sick and tired of having that thing under the bed. I hate the smell of it. I wish you’d get it out of here. I wish you’d sell it. I could sure use the money to get things I need for the house.”

He took the motor into the house and shoved it under the bed and covered it carefully. Then we sat down to eat.

There was a bouquet of twigs from the old willow tree on the table. There were no flowers, no leaves on them—nothing but a handful of sticks.

We were having liver for supper and Russ speared a slab with his fork and put in on his plate and began cutting it into neat squares.

“When that motor goes out of here,” he said, “I go. Savvy? ”

She got up and went into the bedroom; slammed the door and flopped down on the bed, crying. We could hear her carrying on something fierce.

## Chapter XIII

It was like I told Russ the next morning while we were waiting for the timekeep to give us our badges and make out our cards:

“A fellow ain’t really worked in an automobile factory until he’s been on the line—knows what it’s like to hold up his end on production.”

“Looks to me,” he said, “like any man could hold up his end along with these fellows. They’re not the same breed we had in the drop forge department. Kids, mostly, and those that ain’t kids are pot-bellied. The only real men here are the niggers. What would a man do with a bunch like this in the woods? Pick up chips—that’s all he could use ’em for.”

“That’s all right,” I said. “You’ll have more respect for them after you’ve watched their duet for awhile. You’ll find out that they can get out the work. They’s fellows here, that when they step out for relief, four men like you couldn’t hold down their jobs on the line.”

It was good to get back where things were humming; where I knew the fellows and where I didn’t have to worry about somebody dropping a piece of steel the size of a house on my head. The graveyard shift had punched out and the day shift was on and the noise made you feel as though you wanted to get going at something. In one end of the building were the automatics, machining cylinder blocks—boring and drilling, cutting and honing and milling. In the other end, a quarter of a mile long, was the assembly line—a moving conveyor system—hundreds of little four-wheeled buggies, moving slowly along, pulled by an endless chain driven by electric motors over a track eighteen inches above the floor. Starting out, each buggy carried a cylinder block—a single piece of cast iron; and as it moved along each man did his work, adding this part and that, until at the end it was a complete motor—ready to run. At right angles to the assembly line were the over-head conveyors, bringing in materials and parts where and when needed. These were monorail conveyors, mostly, attached to the ceiling—little streams of pistons, little streams of valves, starters, transmissions, cylinder heads and gaskets—streams that came into the main assembly line—the big river.

The timekeep took us in tow. Past busy machinery, through groups of men, over air hoses and cables, under fast-moving belts and shafts.

Near the middle of the line we met Norman. I knew Norman—he was my old straw boss.

“I got a couple of men for you,” said the timekeep. “One’s worked here before.”

Norman looked at me—saw who I was.

“Well, well,” he said, “if it ain’t Bennie. Let’s see, you was a helper—before. Guess I’ll put you doing same thing again. Follow me, both of you.” Norman was always busier than a dog with seven kinds of fleas. I walked close up.

“This other fellow—” said Norman, “he your buddy?”

“Not especially,” I said. “We been working together, that’s all.”

“Guess I’ll put him next to Jerry,” he said, “starting nuts on pin bearings. Con rods—you know the job, doncha?”

“Sure.”

“He ought-a pick it up in a couple weeks. You stay—till he gets on.”

“You bet.”

“How’s this fellow, Jerry?” I said. “He used to have nutty ideas. Used to think he was Mr. Holt’s son. Still funny that way—is he?”

“Worse than ever,” said Norm. “He’s got an idea now that he’s gonna commit suicide. Better tell this new fellow not to pay him no mind.”

We got to the place on the line. A couple of helpers were doing the job and we took their places, me on one side and Russ on the other, next to Jerry.

“Aw right,” said Norm, “guess you know what to do.” And then he left with the other helpers. As soon as his back was turned the fellows started razzing me, making cat calls and hollering “See who’s here! Look what the cat’s drug in!” And then, all together, they let out a howl like a bunch of hungry coyotes sitting back on their haunches. I didn’t pay any attention. You don’t have much time for foolishness like that when a job is new to your fingers. And the line was traveling—not fast, but still pretty good—maybe three hundred motors an hour.

The job was one of those that look easy when you stand and watch somebody else do it. Russ could see what to do. I didn’t have to tell him a word. The motors were bottom side up. Jerry put on the caps over the bolts; we started the nuts—eight to each motor. Didn’t turn them down—just started them on. The nuts were there right in front of us in steel trays. We didn’t have to bend down, didn’t have to reach if we stayed in our station.

“You get four,” I said to Russ, “and I’ll get four on each motor.”

“What am I supposed to do the rest of the time?” he said.

“You’ll find out,” I said. “I won’t have to tell you. The line’s not traveling fast yet. In about a half-hour it’ll start speeding up.”

It was one of those tricky jobs where the only thing that makes it hard is the speed. If you had twice as much time, or only half as many motors to do, there’d be nothing to it. A job like that—when you try to learn it you don’t grit your teeth and try to get a stranglehold on it. You go at it easy—sort of play around with it for a couple of days and let the helper do most of the work. You try to cut down your movements—figure out the best way to stand and limber up the muscles of your fingers. I’ve seen fellows get all tied up in a knot just because they tried too hard to lick a job in the start. But I didn’t tell Russ. I thought I’d let him find out for himself what it was like to hold down a job on the line.

On the left of Russ was Jerry, the guy with the nutty ideas, who put the caps on the bearings. He and I had never got very chummy together. On the right was Thomas Jefferson, or Jeff, who ran on the nuts that we started, with a high frequency electric wrench. Jeff was a nigger. Any garage mechanic watching Jeff run down those nuts would have got an inferiority complex and laid down his tools in disgust. Jeff had been on the same job eight years. With his big hands on the handles he’d pull the trigger. Eight jabs with the spinning socket—eight smoking nuts—tight and in place. Visitors coming along the steel bridge over our heads never gave him a look—he made the job look so ridiculously simple and easy. But the fellows who knew their stuff on the line often watched him, and us helpers, who had to step into his place when he took relief, had the nervous jitters every time he took a long drink.

Some of the fellows on the line used to think I was a stool, but I’m not. Of course, when you’re a helper, you’ve got responsibilities. You get paid for more than just the work you get out. And, of course, I’ve got ears, and if there’s red talk around, or talk about unions, I get to hear it and I get to talk with the bosses.

“We might make a straw boss out-a you sometime,” they used to tell me, “if we thought you wasn’t holding back on us—if we thought you was telling us stuff—telling us who the trouble-makers are on the line.”

And then there’s rumors that have to be put down, and these always start on the line where the fellows work close together, elbows touching. Rumors travel fast and someone has to plug in and cut them off before they get too far down the line; spread through the factory and get out on the streets. Out on the streets anybody will believe anything about the Holt factory if the fellow who tells it has a Holt badge on his coat and looks serious. And about half the rumors are that Mr. Holt is going broke.

They start like this: Some fellow near the head of the line mentions that he has read in the papers that Mr. Holt is only going to clear ten million this year. The next fellow cuts it to nine. By the time it gets down the line a hundred yards Mr. Holt is only just about breaking even. Another hundred yards and he’s losing ten-fifteen million, and by the time it reaches the end of the line he’s dead broke and the money lenders of Wall Street are dividing his coat-tails.

And then about every two-three months a rumor gets started that Mr. Holt is going to shut down, and that rumor, once it gets started, is bad. It makes everybody stop spending money. The fellows stop making payments on Holt cars, stall the landlords on rent, and let their houses go back to the mortgage companies. The tradesmen and small storekeepers all over town pull in their belts—lay off the help and sit tight. Anybody can see what would happen if us helpers didn’t try to stop rumors like that.

The line got into its swing. The fellows stopped joking and talking. There was no whistling or singing or horseplay—no time for nothing but work. It was good to be back in the noise and the racket; it was like getting back to city streets after being in the country a long time. It made you feel good; it made you feel like you were a part of the factory. On the line there was the rata-tat-tat-tat of pneumatic hammers, the sharp pft-pft-pft and snarl of air hoses, the whine of electric drills and the hum of power wrenches and screw drivers. But above it all rose the beat and the peculiar vibrating hum of the high-speed automatics. The vibrations from these filled the whole place and got into your blood and your nerves. It made you breathe faster, work faster; if you wanted to go slow you couldn’t, and if your work didn’t keep you busy you jiggled around on one foot, or made some extra motions with your hands or your arms just to keep in time with the noise. Just to be there—to be making your share of the noise—it made you feel good. It made you feel as though you were a part of something pretty darn big and important.

Russ wasn’t satisfied with trying to get four or five nuts on each motor. He’d made up his mind he was going to get all of them right off the bat. He stood there with his feet wide apart, jaws set, and went at his work as though he personally had to lick the tar out of every motor that came towards him down the long line—as though he wanted to hold them back—keep them from coming at him—tear them apart with his hands. Not being used to the work, his fingers were clumsy. He’d drop a nut, start picking it up, get mad at himself, and then drop another. By that time the motor he was on would be in Jeff’s station—another one coming—no nuts for Jeff to run down, and I’d have to jump in and work to beat the band to catch up.

After lunch things went the same. I didn’t tell him anything. Sometimes when a fellow has ideas of his own it’s better to let him find out for himself they’re no good. Tomorrow, I figured, he’d be in the right mood to listen. He’d be ready to take some advice and I’d get him straightened out. When the bell rang, and we were standing in line to punch out, I said: “How do you like it—so far?”

He let out a snort.

“That’s no job I got there,” he said. “It’s a pain in the neck.”

“Tonight,” I said, “you’ll be putting on nuts in your sleep.”

“Tomorrow,” he said, “I’ll have that job learnt. I’ll either get all those nuts—or I’ll eat my shirt.”

“Well,” I said, “you’d better wear a shirt that goes down easy.”

He didn’t have the right attitude, yet.

## Chapter XIV

Russ,” I said, the next morning, after the line had started up and was still going slow, giving the men a chance to warm up, “you’ll get yourself all tied up in a knot on this job. Kind of take it easy for awhile. Don’t try to learn it all the first day. Not even the first week.”

“Learn it all,” he said. “What’s there to learn? Putting these bloody nuts on—that’s all. Stand back! Give me room here to work!”

“Listen, now,” I said. “I’ve put in more years than you have days on this line. You’re not the first man I’ve helped to break in. Let me tell you a few things. Show you how to use a little science on this job.”

“Science—me eye. All I need here is a weak back and a weak mind.”

“But to make it easy for you, I’ll show you. I’ll give you a few pointers.”

“All right,” he said. “What are they?”

I stepped in beside him.

“In the first place,” I said, “don’t waste time with a nut that won’t start—that won’t take a hold of the threads the first time. Maybe it’s got a burr, maybe the threads are not right. Don’t fool around with it. Shoot it into the scrap box.

“In the second place—don’t pick up a nut that you drop. It ain’t worth the time it takes to stoop down and get it. And in the third place—don’t waste any motions. Make every move count and go through the same motions exactly the same way each time.”

I showed him.

“You grab a bunch of nuts with your left hand,” I said. “Get nine—no more and no less. At first you can’t do it. I can’t myself. But after a while, you’ll get expert; you’ll hit it right every time. That gives you enough for one motor and leaves one for the scrap.

“And then to start them,” I said, continuing to show him, “you take your right hand—get one nut between your thumb and forefinger. Pick it up so you’re holding it right—so you won’t have to change it. Put it on the end of the bolt, give it one twist to start it—not two—just one spin with your thumb and forefinger. At first you can’t do it—you’re clumsy. Your fingers are all thumbs and a yard long. But you’ll get it. It’ll come to you after a while and when it does, you’ll have the job learnt. You’ll be the best little nut-starter there is.”

“I’ve got it learnt,” he said. “All I need now is some room to work and less noise. Clear out—will you?”

Him talking about noise!

“Noise,” I said, “you’re used to noise, ain’t you? Seems to me we had some noise in the drop forge. Seems to me you helped make some.”

“It’s not the same,” he said. “You could think.”

“You’ll get used to it,” I said. “After a while you’ll get so you’ll like it. You’ll get so if you didn’t hear the noise it would be like dancing without music. Let yourself get into the swing of it. Don’t fight it. And remember—if this job’s too much of a strain, let me know. We’ll talk to Norm. Maybe we can work out a trade with some other fellow.”

“They’re all alike. One job’s as bad as another—except one.”

“What’s that?”

“Jerking the motors off when they get to the end.”

“Cripes,” I said, “that job’s a man-killer. Nothing but hard manual labor.”

It was the only job on the line that had the engineers stumped—where brute force was still doing the work that should have been done by electricity. They had tried out different kinds of hoists but none of them worked. A big Russian had the job. He’d been doing the same thing for years. He wouldn’t have traded jobs with the superintendent himself. He worked a long iron lever attached by a chain to a truck that slid along a rail near the ceiling. He snapped a pair of iron hooks over each motor as it came to the end of the line and heaved it off the buggy and swung it onto another conveyor that ran into the motor test room. It took all of his two hundred and fifty pounds of beef at the end of the lever. But the visitors, coming along the steel bridge, watched him work. Here was a man, leather pads on his hands, sleeves cut off at the shoulders, doing something with a swing and a flourish—getting things done. They could appreciate his efforts, see the sweat on his shirt—watch his big muscles swell. The other jobs on the line were hard to understand. Not spectacular. Little jobs, for the most part—doing little things with the little muscles of the hands and the fingers. But the job the Russian had was different and he made the most of it, showing off for the visitors. He was the only man on the line that sometimes busted out in loud song. He had a pair of mustachios that stuck out like a pair of horns on a Texas steer. His shirts were never anything but silk, sleeves cut off at the shoulders, and on his ham-like arms, exposed to the admiring view of the school-teachers, were the tattooed figures of a wild boar and a wolfhound, which, held side by side, showed the animals to be in a fight when he made the muscles twitch in his arms.

“No soap,” I said. “I know that fellow. He won’t trade. He’s been on that job for years. He likes to show off for the visitors.”

About nine-thirty the line speeded up and the bosses started riding the buggies. About one buggy in every two hundred had a line boss on it instead of a motor, the boss standing up on the buggy and riding. You’d be working along and all of a sudden a couple of feet would show up and there would be a line boss standing over your head, drifting along, checking the operations to see if everybody was doing his work and not dragging over or bucking the line.

On a conveyor system like ours one man can ball up the whole works. If a man is slow on his operation he gets dragged over into the next fellow’s station, and the next fellow gets pushed over into the fellow’s station beyond him, and so down the line. In cases like that, a helper, or maybe two or three helpers, jump in and in a few minutes they get things straightened out—running smooth again.
