But there are other fellows harder to handle—harder to find. These are the fellows that make it a practice to buck the line. Most generally they never get behind on their own operations—they’re too smart for that—but they make it hard for the others, and especially for the niggers, by breaking down the morale, starting wild stories, putting on nuts that jam and won’t thread, using defective gaskets, and balling things up in general, so that there is always a kind of fight going on between the men and the bosses—the bosses trying to speed up the line, trying to get out production—the men holding back, doing things to make the line drag. Generally these fellows stay covered up, but once in a while one comes out in the open and when he does—he’s jerked out pretty darn quick! One of these, I’d always had a sneaking suspicion, was Jerry, the guy with the nutty ideas who worked next to Russ.

Lunchtime came and we lined up for soup. It was a long line—seventy or eighty fellows crowding each other, the stomach of one man rubbing the back of the fellow ahead. Behind Russ was a fat little Polack, crowding just like the others. Russ was pretty uncomfortable, shifting about, first to one side and then to the other. The little Polack, not wanting to lose his place in the line, shifted, too, and Russ got more and more nervous. He wasn’t used to that kind of thing. In the drop forge there were only a few of us and we never had to line up. In the lumber camps, I guess, he had the whole woods to himself. Anyway, he had funny ideas about not wanting to be touched, not wanting to be handled by others. I noticed it first when he jerked away from the doctor while taking his physical examination at the time he was hired.

Russ turned around and said to the little Polack:

“What you trying to do—run over me? Stand back a ways. Don’t stick your paunch into me. Don’t touch me.”

“‘Was’s a mat,” said the little Polack, “you goosey—maybe?”

“If you’re anxious to find out,” said Russ, “try it and see.”

The little Polack picked up an air hose and goosed him. Russ turned around, quick as a cat, and hit the Polack with his open hand alongside of the head. There was a stack of camshafts right near and the Polack grabbed one. But before he could raise it Russ let him have one that rolled him clean to the floor. There was a crowd right away and two H.P.’s, one big and one little, strong-armed their way up. Always, they seemed to bob up in pairs—always one was big and one little and the little one usually did all the talking while the big one stood around and looked serious.

“What’s the matter, here?” said the little one.

Russ started to explain. .

“This little Polack, here,” he said, “was crowding me from behind. I told him to lay off and he wouldn’t. I hit him.”

The little Polack was back on his feet and started jabbering.

“That’s not all,” I said. “The Polack goosed him with this air hose.”

The big one picked up the hose. Pressed the lever.

“Air in it,” he said, mighty solemn.

“Is that right?” said the other to Russ. “Did this fellow try to goose you with this hose?”

“That’s not what I knocked him down for,” said Russ. “He picked up an iron.”

“That’s not what I’m asking you. I want to know—did he try to goose you with this hose?”

“Aw, skip it,” said Russ. “He’s learned his lesson. Let the poor guy go.” Russ walked away.

But some fellow in the crowd I didn’t know, spoke up. Said he had seen the Polack pick up the hose. That was enough.

“Aw, right,” said the big fellow to the Polack; “turn in your tools; pull your card. You’re coming with us.”

The little H.P. climbed up on a box and began making a speech.

“Now I wanna tell you fellas something,” he said. “There’s gonna be no more horseplay in this shop with air hoses. There’s been too much of it. It’s gonna be stopped. I want you fellas to get that through your heads. If you fellas can’t read the safety rules, get someone else to read ’em for you. What’d you t’ink dese rules are for, anyhow? What’d you t’ink we put ’em up for all over dese buildings? Just for fun? Every year somebody gets killed with a busted gut just because some of you fellas never learn nottin’. Some of you fellas ain’t got brains enough to get along with hardly.” Etc., etc.

He went on that way for quite a while before he calmed down, got off the box and shut up. By that time the little Polack had got back with his card, looking worried and scared.

“Please, mister,” he said, “I don’t like me get fired. I got famlee—six. My wife seek. She’s no goot to vork. Pleese—mister—I no do nottin’ no more.”

“Come on,” said the big fellow. “We’re gonna learn you a lesson.”

And the little Polack, carrying his card and still begging for all he was worth not to be fired, was marched away.

## Chapter XV

In the afternoon Jerry started shooting off his mouth to Russ. Up till then he hadn’t said a word to Russ—hadn’t looked at him hardly. But he’d seen the fight and the way Russ handled himself and now he started taking Russ under his wing—getting pally with him.

“Why don’t you pull out of here,” he said, “before this thing gets you down. Once it gets you, and you get to the point where you don’t mind it, you’re sunk. Never be no good again for nothing. Lose your nerve—lose your self-respect—your manhood. Stay here a year and you might’s well be dead.”

Things like that he was telling Russ.

Russ was handling the job all by himself. But I stuck around pretty close, knowing that if he got behind and balled things up on the line, I’d get the blame. Besides, I wanted to catch some of the things Jerry was saying.

I knew Jerry. Knew him from the time I came to work in motor assembly. He was a Jew and full of crazy ideas. He wasn’t a communist, exactly, but he read books about Socialism and labor unions, and he went to Townsend meetings where he got up and made speeches. He used to go around telling people that he was Mr. Holt’s son—the real Raymond Holt, and that, by rights, he should be sitting with his feet on the big desk, running the yacht, living in the big house on Jefferson Avenue, and that Raymond should be doing production work on the line. When I first came to work in the factory he got chummy with me. He got me alone one day in the toilet.

“Listen,” he’d said, “if I let you in on a secret you’ll keep your mouth shut, won’t you?”

I promised and he pulled out a bunch of papers, worn out and dirty from being carried about a long time.

“These prove it,” he said. “I’ve got finger prints and everything. We was born the same day—same hospital. The nurse got us mixed.”

The fellows kidded him along. They said anyone could see just by looking at him that he was Mr. Holt’s son—same features—same little habits, like jingling coins in his pockets and pulling his watch chain when he talked. Norm tried to straighten him out, but you couldn’t argue with him. If you didn’t agree with what he said, he got mad.

And then one day he got all his papers together and sent them to Mr. Holt, along with a letter, explaining everything. The very same day a couple of H.P.’s popped up in his room; looked the place over, told him he was all wet and ordered him to lay off talking about Mr. Holt and his son. That had the effect of making him crawl into his shell and shut up tight as a clam, and he got the idea that the H.P.’s were all down on him and that they watched every move that he made day and night. Let a couple walk by strictly minding their own business, which they sometimes did, and he’d say to the fellow next to him:

“Notice the way they looked at me, then?”

“Too many books,” was the way Norman explained it, and I guess he was right. Once a fellow from the University at Ann Arbor made a study of Holt workers in Detroit and found that they spent on the average twenty cents each each year for books, not counting papers. Jerry spent twice that much and has a good education besides. And what does it get him? A lot of nutty ideas.

One idea that he had was that Holt workers should raise kids without brains to work on the automatics and assembly lines. Thinking, he said, was bad for a man that worked in Mr. Holt’s factory. Got him into all kinds of trouble. But a man without brains could work on the line, run his car hell-a-tootin’ up and down Woodward Avenue, drink beer with the Polacks, shoot pool with the niggers, listen to what Mr. Holt said and have a big time. He was serious about saying these things. It’s like Mr. Holt says in his book: “Too much education may be a very bad thing for a man.”

He was talking again, telling Russ about his plans.

“Some day,” he said, “I’m going to commit suicide right here in this plant. I’ve got it all figured out—the way I’m going to do it, but I’m not telling anybody. These Holt spotters around here watch every move I make. They’d put a jinx on my plans if they could.”

Russ didn’t join in the talk very much. He was too busy.

“All I’m telling anyhow now,” he continued, “is that I’ve got a way figured out that’s a honey. It’ll get my name in the headlines. And on the same day Mr. Holt will get a bunch of papers that will make him sit up and take notice—make him wish he’d done different. It’ll be too late then.”

“How are you going to commit suicide here in the plant?” said Russ. “Get yourself run over by a switch engine?”

“‘Na-a-a—” he said, in a way that insinuated that Russ’ ideas about such things were pretty old fashioned, “I’ve got a way figured out that beats that all hollow. But I can’t tell you. I’m not telling anyone. All I can say is that it’s going to shut the whole works down for three solid days. Everyone on production will get laid off and have a chance to go to my funeral. It’ll be the biggest funeral that’s ever been pulled off in Detroit. And it’s going to cost Mr. Holt plenty of money—plenty.”

Such talk as that gets around and is bad for the men. I had a good notion to speak to Norm and tell him that his talk was beginning to sound red.

## Chapter XVI

In the motor assembly we were cut back to five dollars a day, worked four days a week, and Russ, instead of laying away money, was spending more than he made. One reason was he’d take a day off and go job-hunting whenever he felt like it, or heard a rumor that some other company was hiring. On Fridays and Saturdays he’d ride the street cars, going from one plant to another, trying to get a better job. But none of them paid as much as five dollars; most of them worked even less than we did.

Coming home he’d have stories to tell—things the fellows around the employment gates had told him about new machines and new ways of doing things in the factories. He’d done some welding, once, and started out trying to get a job as a welder, but soon gave it up. He heard the men talk about spot welders, seam welders, butt welders, and flash welders, and found that these were the names of machines, and not, as he first thought, types and kinds of work done by skilled welders.

There was no more handwelding to be done in the factories except a little piddling and patching with oxyacetylene torches where the machines skipped. The machines had taken over the trade.

Some of the welding machines that he told about were sure slick. There were some that took in the parts of a car body and welded them together in a shower of fire, making a single, solid whole of the pieces—closing seams in one shot that were six feet long—doing the work in one flash that used to take three or four men a couple of hours. To hear him tell it the operators of the machines had no control over the work; the time, pressure, amount of wire, melting rate, and heat all being fixed by the engineers who set up the machines.

One company had a machine called the “Iron Man” that welded gas tanks. It was a spot welder—fully automatic—turned and tilted the tanks as it worked on them and ran a neat row of eleven spots to the inch around the edges like a sewing machine hemstitching a handkerchief, except that nobody put his hands on the work. Four of these did the work of two hundred and fifty hand welders.

Another company had a machine that checked and sorted piston pins for size, grading to limits of one ten-thousandths of an inch. The operator fed them into the machine with a shovel. New machines to combat the hard times by cutting down labor costs were being moved into the plants every day. Among these were the centerless grinders, where, because the work did not even have to be fastened in, the operator ran a whole battery of machines. There were drum-type milling machines, merry-go-rounds the men called them, that took the rough pieces around in a circuit and brought them back fully machined, eight or ten riding around at the same time; full automatic contour grinders for camshafts; and complicated machines that bored as many as ninety-six holes from five or six different angles in the same operation. In the power plants not a man put his hand to a slicing bar or a shovel, these tools being seen only in a glass cage in the boiler room where they were pointed out to the visitors as relics of an age that was past. In the steel mills of the plants there were a few men in the big, glass, air-conditioned cages, watching a row of tiny lights that went off and on—manipulating rows of steel levers that sent long, snakelike bars of red steel backwards and forwards between the giant rollers—flattened bar steel to thin strips the width and thickness of wallpaper—tossed ingots of steel back and forth with the strength of seven thousand horses; but down below on the floor there was not a man to be seen except the headers who stood with long-handled tongs next to the rollers and started the red snakes into the right grooves. After telling about these things Russ would be pretty discouraged.

“The engineers,” he would say, “are cutting us out of the picture. I can see why the men that have been around in the plants all want to be sweepers. It’s th last stand against the machine; it’s the last transfer, Picking up the dirt the machines make.”

He was always looking on the dark side of things.

After supper he’d haul the outboard from under the bed; spread a newspaper out on the table and begin taking something apart. Rita got so she hated the sight of the thing and would go into the bedroom and close the door. One night she came into my room.

“As long as he’s got that thing,” she said, “he’ll never settle down and take his responsibility. I wish there was some way I could get rid of it.”

“He’ll get tired of it,” I said. “Give him time. Soon’s he gets used to his new job—gets to like it—he’ll forget all about clamming.”

“You think so?” she said. “Do you think he’ll ever settle down and be steady like the other men that have families?”

“Sure,” I said, wanting to make her feel good. “After a month or two more the work will be a cinch for him. He’ll think he’s got the best job on the line. They all do, after they’ve been on the same job a while. Get so they do it easy—don’t have to think. What gets ’em is when they have to think about work they don’t like.”

“I don’t know,” she said. “He’s not like the others. I keep worrying. With the baby coming—I don’t feel like I did about things. I want things to be certain. It’s not myself I’m thinking about.”

“Of course not.”

“I used to think it was fun to talk about clamming—live out in the woods like Indians. But I didn’t think he was serious—not about that kind of thing. Going about from one place to another—not having a job—no pay checks coming in.”

“You’re like I am,” I said.

“Only—you’re not having a baby.”

“Well—not exactly.”

“I’d like to have money,” she said. “Enough to go to a hospital.”

“Mr. Holt’s would be a good place.”

“But it costs money,” she said. “And we’re not getting ahead. We spend more than we make. A lot goes for carfare. He lays off when he hears some other company is putting on men. And as long as that motor’s under the bed—as long as he tinkers around with it—I know what he’s thinking.”

“I know how you feel,” I said. “I won’t encourage him. I’ll try to make things go as smooth as I can in the factory. Get him to like things.”

I was mighty sorry for her. Sitting there on a box where I kept my writing things, she was sewing on a blanket that was hardly as big as a man’s handkerchief. That baby coming—being on the way—had sure changed her. It had changed her from a taxi-dancing flapper and was making a woman out of her. The trouble was—it left Russ the same.

One night, coming out of the factory after we’d been in motor assembly a little more than a month, we passed the bone-yard, and there, in with ten acres of other scrapped machinery, we saw the rig. One side was down in the mud; the boom was all screwy, and the big iron claw hung loose and open like an idiot’s mouth.

“There’s Old Betsy,” said Russ. “Let’s go in and see what they’ve done to her.”

I didn’t see the use, but I went, getting my feet wet and muddy. He went up to the rig and put his hand on the control box.

“Cold iron,” he said, serious. “No static, even. But if you was to turn the juice into her she’d soon come to warm life again.”

“She’ll come to warm life,” I said, “when they get the torches to work on her—cut her up and feed her to the open hearths. One of these days she’ll be coming over the line in the shape of cylinder blocks and you’ll be starting nuts on her.”

“You know, Bennie,” he said, sliding his hand back and forth along a drive-shaft, “there’s something almost funny about the way this steel feels to the touch. You get used to having a thing feel the same way, don’t you? And when it doesn’t—it’s almost as though something was wrong. Even during the night she never got really cold. In the morning there was always some heat left in the metal. It must be that way when you feel the hand of someone you’ve known a long time and —”

“Must be,” I said. “Let’s get going. If the H.P.s see us fooling around here you’ll have a hard time making ’em believe that you’re preaching a funeral sermon and not trying to get away with some brass bearings.”

“Don’t rush me,” he said. “What’s the difference if we get outa this place a few minutes later than usual? This is probably the last time we’ll see the old rig.”

“Last time you get sentimental about it, I hope.”

“To you,” he said, “this machine here is just a pile of old iron—junk. But to me it’s more than that, Bennie. To me—it’s Old Betsy—still. To you, all machines are alike. Pieces of steel and iron put together—all alike. But they’re not, Bennie. Machines do things to us—the men that work with them. Things the engineers never think about. Some machines build us up—help us to make the most of ourselves. Some tear us apart—grind us down. A machine geared to a man—is one thing. A man geared to a machine—is something else. One’s human, gives you a chance to be your best self. The other works on you like a gear-cutter—whittling you down, chiseling at you, cutting grooves in you, making you like all the other small gears that work on the line.”

He stood there fingering a winged nut on a brakerod, just the bare hint of a smile near the corner of his mouth, that smile that I never knew quite how to take.

“I don’t know what you’re thinking about,” I said, “but I guess it’s all right.”

“I’m not sentimental,” he said, in his quiet way. “At least, I don’t think I am. I see things for what they are worth—to me. When I was in the drop forge I had a man’s job and did a man’s work. It was fun. Remember how I used to stay after the bell rang and try to work out ideas for getting the work done faster, better? I had a chance to use the brains I was born with. Old Betsy, here, gave me a chance to get the most out of myself. When a job was done, I looked it over. Took some of the credit, if it was good. Took some of the blame, if it wasn’t. Tried to figure out how to do it better the next time. Old Betsy was geared to my brain—did what I wanted it to do. Gave its strength to my hand, gave its power to my will. I was the boss.

“But in the motor assembly, Bennie, it’s different. There the machine is the boss. The machine does the nice work—the hard work—the part that takes skill. The machine’s everything and gets credit for the work done. You’re nothing until you’ve learned to be a gear—a small part of the big machine—until there’s nothing left of you but a very small cog without any will. You start and stop when the machine’s ready—go slow or fast as the machine tells you. The machine counts—you don’t. And why should you? You don’t furnish the brains. You don’t furnish the skill. All you do is fasten a nut, put on a washer, stamp on a number. The machine does the real job. It’s the big boss standing over your head—grinding you down—wasting your strength—whittling away at your brain. Making you a small part of its dead, mechanical self.”

“Come on,” I said, “it’s late. Supper will be waiting for us. We haven’t cleaned up yet.”

“That’s right,” he said, picking his way through the mud, “we haven’t cleaned up. In the drop forge—we used to clean up every night before leaving. Used to change our clothes and wash the dirt from our faces. Walk out of the gate looking decent. Now we don’t—ever. Nobody does. When it’s time for the bell we don’t think about anything except getting out—getting out. Getting away from the machines. We lay our tools just so—roll our sleeves down—untie the knots in our aprons. We get set for the bell. We get ready to rush out when the bell rings, thinking we’ll get rid of the machines for a spell—the machines that eat into us like gear-cutters. But we don’t, Bennie. We don’t—ever. They stay with us, they get into our blood, leave their dirty imprint on our faces. It’s there when we hurry out—there on the river of dead faces streaming out through the gate. And in the morning, when we come back to the machines—it’s still there. We go home and use water and soap but we never clean up. The work of the machines that keep whittling and chiseling at us is too deep.”

## Chapter XVII

Gus was too old to work on production. He was too slow to keep up with the others. He was fifty.

“Gus,” his straw boss would say, “you’ve got to step on it a little. You ain’t gettin’ out the work. Colored fella on the graveyard shift is gettin’ out seventy blocks every hour—you’re doing fifty.”

“Ya-but—” he’d say. And then he’d get off a bunch of excuses. Always he could think of excuses. Always he’d start off the same way. “Ya-but—de oddas got hoists to lift de blocks op. I got to lift ’em myself. Allatime lift ’em myself,” or, “Ya-but—de blocks vas so hot I couldn’t stand putt my hands on ’em.”

It got so, after a while, half the fellows called him “Old Ya-but.” He was too old. He was too old for production but not too old for thinking up excuses for not doing the work faster and better. He had a one-speed brain and no gear-shift and once he made up his mind—no reverse. No wonder the bosses were always riding him—always right on his neck.

The machine that he worked on was a high-speed, five-way automatic drill that bored ninety-eight holes from five different directions into the cylinder block in the same operation. He put in a block and pushed the lever. Hydraulic clutches took hold; clusters of drills slid out and got to work on the iron-boring and drilling, cutting away the gray iron. Bright tungsten steel in hot clusters—cutting-speed seventy feet every minute—doing it all the day long, not complaining, not snapping off short under the heat and the strain; getting the holes where they belonged—not too deep, not too shallow—not too big, not too small, either. Getting them just right every time.

The operation done, the clusters of drills backed away; hydraulic clutches let go, and the machine pushed the block up and waited for Gus to come to, pull it off, and put on another. The machine did the work—the nice work—the hard work. Gus did the complaining.

“Ya-but,” he’d say, “if I had a wooden floor to stand on. My feets all-a-time get tired. Ya-but—de soda water. Het makes my hands hurt.” Always complaining. Always thinking up excuses for not being able to hold up his end of the job—not being able to keep up with the niggers.

Before coming to motor assembly Gus worked on the wrecking line. Old cars came into the plant from all over the country to be torn down and stripped and crushed and cut into slices and fed to the open hearth furnaces. Twenty-five dollars Mr. Holt paid for them. They came from Texas, Iowa, Indiana, Florida, Arizona—every state in the Union, every town in the country. On the trucks they came in—on the long double-deckers; on the lake boats; some under their own power—limping in through the gate, you’d see them—some with fenders that waggled, some with pieces of canvas tied over the tops, lamps busted. Not many ran on all cylinders.

On the wrecking line all the power tools ran in reverse—screwing nuts off instead of running them on. The wheels were removed; the engine heaved out; all glass saved; upholstery stripped—after which the conveyor dragged them onto the bed of a giant crusher. Here a piston came down and with one agonizing blow flattened the old cars into thin patches of scrap metal. And when the shears cut the patches into small strips, it was almost the end, except for the short ride on a belt to the furnaces.

In the wrecking department Gus had worked on the crusher. He had pulled the short lever that brought the huge piston down. One day, so they said, he had killed a man—the man who stripped out the upholstery. It had been just after lunch. The man had fallen asleep in the old car and had not awakened when the line started up. No one blamed Gus for the accident, though at the time, there had been an investigation, as a matter of course. Gus had been hauled up front. Still excited, he hadn’t been able to say much and what he did say was probably the wrong thing. But he needn’t have talked. It was probably one of those things that happen in factories. But since then he had got into the habit of thinking that he still had to clear himself of the blame to us. Kept thinking of all the things that he should have told the investigators at the time—things that he should have said and hadn’t—things he’d got balled up on and got wrong, maybe. Generally he was pretty gloomy and silent. With his big cow-like eyes he’d sit there eating his lunch—not saying much—chewing a dry sandwich—looking down, blank eyes to the floor. Not hearing a thing the other fellows were saying. Lots of times I’d wonder what he was thinking about.

But just let one of the fellows bring up the accident and he was ready to talk, ready to explain how it happened. That he hadn’t meant to do it, that it was none of his fault. And, I guess maybe it wasn’t, but the more he talked and the harder he argued the worse he made it look for himself.

We were having lunch one day—Russ, Amos, Jerry and me and one or two other fellows sitting together, when Amos, who worked below Jeff on the line, got him started again, just to have fun. Amos was always kidding somebody that wasn’t quite as smart as himself.

“Gus,” he said, “you never told Russ, here, how that accident happened on the wrecking line. How you killed that fellow—that Irishman—and how they tried to put the blame onto you. Of course, we all know you didn’t do it—didn’t do it on purpose, that is. But Russ, here—Russ’ never heard the story from you. Why don’t you tell it before somebody else does and gives him the wrong idea?”

“Sure,” said Russ, “let ’er come. I guess I can stand it if the rest can.”

Gus came out of a daydream and took notice.

“Vell, I can tell you vun t’ing,” he said. “I didn’t done it on purpose. So shure I sit hier, I didn’t know he vas in die old car. If I know he vas in dar, I vould not have killed heem.”

“Of course not,” said Amos. “You didn’t know he was in the old bus. But at the same time—I guess he had it coming to him, didn’t he, Gus? All the time running down the Hollanders and bragging up the Irish, wasn’t he?”

“Ja,” said Gus. “He vas all de time bodthering me. "Tis I-don’t-care-to-me a fellow bodthers me ven I’m not vorking. But ven I vork, I don’t like bodthering—all de time bodthering.”

“How did he bother you?” said Russ.

“Vell, you see, he vas de last man on de line—hexcept me. He pooled de stuffings out de old cars. All kinds tings he found in de old cars. Under de cussions—in de side-pockats—in de upholstrie. Some t’ings vas goot tings; some t’ings vas not goot. Some t’ings vas maybe in de old cars en long time. Stuck avay, maybe, by spooners. On a dak road at night. Somebody is coming, maybe. Some t’ings is—stuck avay in a hurrie. Alvays does t’ings he found. Goot t’ings—bad t’ings. Vomen’s pajamas, elastics, safety pins, lipsticks, old cigarettes, Sunday school papers, snooze boxes, bottle-openers, playing cads, old viskey bottles. Vun time a half stick dynamite in a side pockat.

“De line—he vas not so fas’—like dis vun. De Irisher talked to me. To de oddas.

“ “Hil? he say. ‘Look vat I found in dis car. ’Tis en car van en Dutsman—en Hollander’s car. De register card has on it a Duts name.’

“Den he vould hold op a voman’s chemise, maybe. And all de oddas vould laugh me out. Alvays, if he found somet’ing bad—it was a Duts car. But joost ven he found somet’ing goot—it was en Irisman’s card. Iris name on de card, or somet’ing.

“ ‘Look,’ he vould say, and hold op maybe a Bible. All de oddas vould look.

“ Ya,’ dey vould say, ‘de Iris is goot peoples—Iris is nice! Hokay de Iris!’ And gif me vunce more de laugh.

“First, I dun’t care. En den I get en little bit med. En den I get medder and medder. By’nby I’m so med I can get. But joost de same—I no kill heem.”

“Of course not,” said Amos. “I don’t see how you held yourself in. A man can stand just so much of that kind of thing.”

“All-a-time I’m de vun vats hokay. I’m lifing goot, going early to bed, standing up early, vorking hard every day. En de Irisher—he’s out every night, runnin’ round vit all kinds vomens. Vunce, vid his Sunday clothes on, he came in de factory. All de whool night he vas out—tomcettin’ around. En den vun day—on a Monday—it heppened. Maybe de whool night he vas out again. Noon time, de line vas shut down. I eat my lunsch. I dun’t see heem de whool time. I t’ink might-be he’s gone out, maybe. Might-be he’s telefunning. Same as alvays—de line stats again. En old car moves up on de block. I pool de lever. De big piston comes down. En ven it moves op again—I see bloed. I see all oever—bloed.”

“Flatter’n a pan-cake, eh, Gus?”

“Shure.”

“Too much runnin’ around Sunday night. Vent to sleep in the old bus, eh?”

“Hexactly. I couedn’t halp he vas in dar, in die old car. De boss say to me—‘Ven die old car he’s op on die block—pool die lever.’ I pool heem.”

“How’d they pick him up?” said Russ.

“Putty knifes and blow torches. A thousand tuns came don on heem.”

“Too bad,” said Amos, “have to go through something like that. Must-a been a pretty bad sehock—see all that blood.”

“Het vas awful,” said Gus. “Het vas joost awful. De whool afternoon—I couedn’t vork. En de next morning—I couedn’t. Ven I vas tryin’ to raise my arm to pool de lever—I couedn’t raise heem. My whool arm—he vas paraliced op to de schoulder. He vas stief.”

“So you got a transfer?” said Russ.

“Ya-but—my arm, he’s not rite. Sometimes he bodthers me yet. Ven production is fast en all time de boss he comes op to me en he say, ‘Vat’s de madther, Gus; can’t you stap on it? Can’t you keep op? You vant I should gif you a transfer to blue mass production?’ Times like dat—my arm he bodthers me yet. He gets stief. Sometimes I hardly can raise heem to poosh de lever on de macheen.”

“That’s hard to figure,” said Amos. “Sounds like your conscience was bothering you. You didn’t kill the guy—not on purpose. Did you?”

“Shure not. De boss say to me, ‘Ven die cars come op on die block—pool die lever.’ En now—sometimes ven I poosh die lever on de drilling macheen en de boss he’s all time make me go fast—I see bloed. De soda vater on de hot drills gets first pink—en den red. En I t’ink—vot ef it’s somebody in de macheen! Vot ef de drills go eento heem! Ento hes head—eyes—stomack—all over, eento heem. Vot ef it’s bloed—ef I kill! Den my arm he gets stief en I go to de toilet. Ven I come beck—de soda vater he’s vhite again. My arm—he’s hokay.”

“That proves you’re not guilty,” said Amos. “White stands for innocence.”

“Ya-but,” said Gus, “sometimes het’ s red.”

Jerry got up—mad.

“What are you trying to do?” he asked Amos. “Make the poor guy miserable? Trying to make him crack up?”

“Naw,” said Amos. “Just trying to kid him.”

Gus blinked a couple of times. Looked from one to the other. He was too dumb to catch on.

The bell rang and we all went to work.

## Chapter XVIII

One day when there was a heavy schedule and the bosses were riding the buggies, trying to get out production, Russ got into trouble. A couple of times in the morning he started dragging over into Jeff’s station, interfering with Jeff—still trying to get the nuts on when Jeff should have been running them down. This put Jeff behind on his operation and he crowded Amos, and Amos put up a loud squawk.

Amos didn’t like to be crowded. It interfered with a crazy notion he had.

The cotter pins that Amos inserted went in from any one of six different directions. The nuts were castellated with six points and the pins were inserted between the points and through the holes in the bolts. Nearly always one of the holes would be lined up with the points and it didn’t matter which hole was used, or from which direction the pins were inserted—just so they were in, just so the nut was locked on the bolt. And that’s the way he did his work on all the rods—all except number one. On number one rod the pins had to go in just so. They had to go in parallel to each other and heads to the front of the motor. Every motor. It was a crazy notion—a kind of game he played with himself. It made a lot of extra work—a lot of extra fiddling around to get the nuts right. The holes might be all lined up for the pins to go in crossways, or at an angle. It didn’t matter. He fooled around with the nuts until the pins would go in parallel. Getting those two in was more work than all six of the others. But it didn’t make any difference to him. He had to do it his own nutty way. If you asked him why, he looked foolish and said that he had to leave his mark on the motors. To skip one, to let one go by without leaving his mark, bothered him all day. It made him nervous. And when Jeff got behind because of Russ, didn’t get the nuts down in time, it crowded Amos. It didn’t leave him time enough for his foolishness and he would put up a squawk to Norman. I’d have to jump in and help Russ. Get everybody up where they belonged.

Twice in the morning it happened, and then again after lunch.

“Russ,” I said, “what’s the matter with you? You’re not snapping into it. You ain’t balling the jack today. What’s the matter?”

“Bennie,” he said, “I’ve been thinking. I’ve been thinking about what I’m gonna call that kid when he comes. I’m gonna call him ‘Bennie’—after you. What’d you think about that?”

“That’s swell,” I said. “That’s swell, Russ. But right now—we’re building motors. What’s the reason you’re gettin’ behind all the time?”

“And another thing, Bennie,” he said. “When do you suppose we’ll be pulling outa here?”

“When the bell rings, of course.”

“No—no—I mean, leave the factory. Start clamming. I’ve got a way figured out to make a cradle. Take some ropes—hang it under a tree. And when he gets bigger we can make a place for him up front in the boat. Take ; him out on the water. I’ve been thinking about it all morning.”

I had him up again.

“All right,” I said. “I’ve got you back in your station. Better think about the job for a while.”

I stepped back. Thought I’d watch him a while.

Everybody was snapping into it—keeping time with the beat of the machines. Blue smoke hung like a low fog over the hot automatics. The line was fast, all right, but not too fast for Russ if he put himself into it.

For a while he held his place fine. Then he started slipping again—losing ground. Every motor carried him back an inch or two and pretty soon he was over the line in Jeff’s station again. He wasn’t trying. He was taking it easy; going along in an absent-minded way—taking up a handful of nuts in loose fingers—starting them on with long, easy motions. Working as though the motor he was on would be the last to come over. I had to help him again.

“It’s the last time,” I said, stepping in. “After this—it’s up to you. Norman’s bellyaching to beat the band—me helping you all the time. Five or six fellows want relief.”

“That’s too bad,” he said. “But I’m glad you’re back. I wanted to ask you, how old does a kid have to be, generally, before he starts saying words? I don’t mean talking—I mean saying short words like ‘Daddy,’ for instance. There’s an awful lot I don’t know about kids. When a man knows he’s gonna be a father it sets him thinking. He thinks about a lot of things he’s never thought about before. What kind of songs, for instance, does a fellow sing when he’s putting a kid to sleep after supper? That’s one I’ll bet you never thought about yet.”

“No, by Judast,” I said, “I sure ain’t. And neither has nobody else when they’re working on the line—trying to get out production. You’re daydreaming, Russ. That’s what’s the matter with you.”

“What do you mean—daydreaming?” he said.

“You’re just not there. Your mind’s not on the job.”

“Doesn’t have to be. I don’t need my mind for the job. All I need is two fingers. I’ve got it down to four motions. I keep time with that belt up there. I do it this way—see: ONE—two—THREE—four. Two heavy beats. Two light ones. Do it over and over—without thinking.”

Overhead, attached to the ceiling, there was a ventilator fan driven by an electric motor and a long belt with four metal splices. The splices made a clicking sound as they passed over the steel pulley. Not loud—not as loud as the noise from the automatics. Not as fast either. But the sound was very distinct, once you noticed it.

I stood beside him—looked on. A helper meets up with some crazy ideas on the line, but this was the nuttiest I’d ever run into.

“Notice how I keep time,” he said. “Do it with my eyes shut, if I wanted to. ONE—I pick up the nut; two—I set it on the end of the bolt; THREE—I give it a twist; four—I move my hand back for another. Nothing to it. Once I get into the swing I don’t have to think about the job. ONE—two—THREE—four— Don’t have to give it any attention. Swing along with it.”

“You’d better start giving it a little attention,” I said. “Better start right now. Snap out of it, Russ. That motor’s not synchronized with the line.”

“If I don’t miss any beats,” he said, “do three hundred and twenty an hour. And I haven’t missed any all morning and yet you’re bellyaching about me holding up the line. Last week I missed some and there wasn’t a squawk outa nobody.”

“The line’s faster today. That motor up there runs the same speed all the time. Get the noise outa your system. Break away from it. Concentrate on the automatics.”

“On the automatics? That’s what I want to get away from. They interfere with my thinking.”

“You’re not getting paid here for thinking. You’re getting paid for getting the work out.”

“I’m not thinking about the work.”

“You’re not getting the work out, either. The others are laying into it, giving her all they got. Why can’t you?”

“I’m doing one man’s work—and a little more.”

“Listen, Russ,” I said. “It don’t make no difference to me how much you loaf on this job—it’s none of my business. I’m not the boss. Only thing is—I promised Rita I’d see you got along. I don’t want to see you get fired. But you’re bucking the line—that’s what you’re doing right now. And you can’t get away with it. Others have tried it and they only lasted about three days. Once the line bosses figure you’re not trying—you’re gone. It’s not like it was in the drop forge. They don’t have to send to Cleveland to get somebody to step into your job. There’s plenty fellows waiting, right out there by the gate. Ready to step into your place.”

“Q-keh,” he said. “Now you’ve got your little sermon off-a your chest—run along. Don’t worry.”

Just the same, I was worried. I didn’t want Norman to think we were buddies. I didn’t want Russ to get fired, either. I spoke to Jeff on the quiet.

“Jeff,” I said, “Russ ain’t feeling so hot today. If he gets behind give him a lift. Slip on a couple-a nuts for him once in a while, if you can.”

“Sure.”

Good old Jeff. The niggers never give us helpers no trouble.

Then, as I turned to go, I heard that bloody belt up there again, going: ONE—two—THREE—four— I heard it that time without listening for it. Heard it above all the noises. Russ would be hearing it, too. It would be getting into his system—drowning out the automatics—the fast time of the machines that were keeping step with the line. It would be there above his head all the time. Going slow. Going—ONE—two—THREE—four—Two heavy beats. Two light ones. I couldn’t get away from it.

All that afternoon the bosses were riding the buggies; all that afternoon Russ was dragging over, not quite holding his own. He wasn’t worried. The others were feeling the strain—laying into it. But not him. He was taking it easy. Something had to be done. I talked to Norman.

“Norm,” I said, “I guess you’d better take Russ for one of them little walks. Like you did me when I first came to work here. It made a Holt man outa me—maybe it’ll help him. He’s dragging over—getting behind all the time.”

“What’s the matter,” he said, “bucking the line, is he?”

“He’s not bucking it,” I said. “Just not hitting the ball, that’s all.”

“You ain’t tryin’ to kid me, are you?” he said. “You fellows ain’t buddies, are you?”

“Cripes, no,” I said. “If we was buddies I wouldn’t be giving you the low-down. I wouldn’t be telling on him, would I?”

“You ain’t telling me nothing,” he said. “What you think I use my eyes for? All day he’s been gettin’ behind. Half the time you’re over there chewing the rag, helping him. Only thing is—if he’s bucking the line—doing it a-purpose, I wanna know. I wancha to tell me. I’ll pull his card for him.”

“You know me, Norm,” I said. “I’ve always worked with you, ain’t I? He’ll snap outa it. He’s trying to save up. Wife’s gonna have a kid, so I hear.”

“No funny ideas—about unions? Ain’t red? Don’t belong to C.I.O.—nothing like that?”

“Never said nothing to me.”

“Q-keh,” said Norman. “Get back on the job.”

Nothing more happened that afternoon, but that night, walking home, I had another talk with Russ.

“Russ,” I said, “you either got to snap into it or else you might’s well start looking yourself up another job right now. Timing yourself with that belt was a dumb stunt. Once that one-two-three-four business gets ahold of you, you’ll never get away from it. You’re sunk. Might work all right when the line’s slow, but the minute it speeds up—you’re in trouble.”

“The line’s never slow,” he cut in. “Ever stop to think about that?”

“I know, but that’s a non-variable speed motor up there on the ceiling. It’s not timed with the job; it’s not synchronized with the line motors. It goes along up there all by itself—same speed all the time. The line speeds up—you don’t.”

“I could, Bennie,” he said. “I could put myself under a strain and speed up and get out four hundred motors an hour if I wanted to.”

“Why don’t you?”

“Why should I? I’m not getting paid for working that hard. I don’t want to work that hard for five dollars. I don’t want to be nervous and unstrung when I get home at night. I don’t want to feel mean and dragged out like I did first when I was under a strain learning the job. I don’t want to tear around, drink booze, and fight with my wife like most of the other fellows that work on the line. If the line’s too fast—let ’em cut down production, or give us more to get the work out. If I get behind it’s not because I’m laying down on the job. It’s their hard luck—not mine.”

“Yours—if you get fired.”

“Why should I get fired? They fire a man for not doing his work. I’m doing mine. I’m starting as many nuts as any two men you could put on that job tomorrow. On that job I’m an expert. It looks like I’m loafing. I’m not. I’ve got it down to a science. I make every move count.”

“Yes, but you’re holding back the others.”

“Forget about the others. That’s not what we’re talking about, is it? How fast should I go? How many motors an hour? Two-ninety? Three hundred? All right, say three hundred. That’s two thousand four hundred a day for five dollars. Well, I’m doing three hundred and twenty an hour when I’m going along with that belt. I know. I’ve counted. The others don’t know and the bosses are scared to death they’ll find out. Sometimes the line’s fast—sometimes it’s faster. That’s all the men know. If they knew any more—if they knew every time the superintendent screwed up the rheostat another notch when they were already breaking their necks to get out production, the whole system would break down.”

“And then we’d be out of jobs.”

“Out of Holt jobs. But there would be other automobiles. Other jobs. And most important for us, Bennie—other systems.”

“But you can’t buck the system.”

“I don’t want to,” he said. “All I want to do is to save up enough money to get away from the system.”

“Then you’ll have to hold that job for a while. And that means you’ll have to get away from that belt. It all comes to the same thing. Maybe we’d better see the Polack. See if he’ll trade you. Give him a little bonus if we have to.”

“Nix,” he said. “I’ll stay where I am. When we get back to working full weeks Ill be saving money again.”

“But that belt, Russ. It’s got you stymied. The noise gets into your system and you drift along with it and then you start daydreaming. I’ve seen others. When the line’s fast it’s the noise from the automatics that carries you along; that keeps you in step with the others.”

“On the job I got now,” he said, “Mr. Holt’s not paying me for the use of my brains. It’s not like it was in the drop forge. I’ll go along like I am. I’ll do Mr. Holt’s work—give him a little extra. And I’ll use my brains to do my own private thinking.”

He was a long ways yet from being a Holt worker.

## Chapter XIX

The next day the line was fast again. First thing in the morning Russ started dragging over, getting behind, crowding the others. I jumped in twice and got him back in his station. Jeff helped a little but it didn’t do no good. Just before lunch Norman jumped on him. I wasn’t there to hear what was said.

After lunch it was the same. Russ taking it easy, the others hitting the ball—laying into it.

Times like that there’s a real snap to the line. Makes you feel good just to be there—to be a part of it and know you’re doing your share of the work—making your share of the noise. Power wrenches pop and cut off quick; the pf-f-ft, pf-f-ft of air hoses is sharp; the short bursts of the pneumatic hammers stab through the noise like a racketeer’s gun. No leading up to it—no fumbling around to get started. Slam into it—get it done—grab another before it gets past.

Times like that, one man can hold up production in the whole department. He can slow down the work of two thousand others. One man hanging back is all that prevents the superintendent from screwing up the rheostat another notch—cutting off another two or three seconds—stepping up production five or six motors an hour.

Forty minutes before the quitting bell rang, Norman came up.

“Come with me,” he said. “I want you to take your chum’s place for a while.”

We went up to Russ. He was behind again—across the line in Jeff’s station. I jumped in.

“Didn’t do no good—me talking to you this morning, did it?” said Norman.

“No harm, either,” said Russ. “Seems to me I’m doing pretty good.”

“Not good enough,” said Norman. “Don’t look to me like you’re trying.”

“I’ve been doing three-twenty an hour—right steady. That’s better than I did last week.”

“How do you know you’re doing three-twenty an hour? You got time to stand there and count motors? Other fellows ain’t. Maybe that’s why you’re gettin’ behind all the time. Maybe that’s why you ain’t keeping up.”

“I don’t have to stop work to count them,” said Russ.

“How do you know then how fast they’re coming over? Other fellows don’t. I don’t even, myself.”

Russ didn’t answer. Didn’t say anything about the belt, though all the time they were arguing, his hands were keeping slow time with it. Norman didn’t catch on to the situation, though to me, it was as plain as a fellow keeping step with a drum.

“Come on,” said Norman, “me’n you’s gonna take a walk.” Russ dropped out and they left.

It was all I could do to hold the job down, even with Jeff’s help. Helpers never get as expert on an operation as the regular men. They don’t get to practice enough on one job. On some operations that are real tricky it takes two, three, and even four helpers to hold the job down while the regular man takes his three minutes off. Russ’ job was tricky enough to call for two, and here Norman was leaving me to do it alone, and the line extra fast. Besides, there was the belt going ONE—two—THREE—four— Two heavy beats—two light—trying to keep in time with my movements, trying to slow me down. I broke away from it. I thought about something else—about the time, four-five years ago, when I first came to work in motor assembly; when I got behind and Norm took me for a walk. It was his favorite way of making a man snap into it, making him appreciate his job.

I thought about Norman and Russ—about what they were doing. Now they’d be outside, walking past the blast furnaces, Norman not saying anything—a little ahead—Russ wondering what it was all about. But not asking. On these walks Norman always wanted it to be a surprise. Then across the railroad tracks and along the side of the glass factory—past the loading platforms and into final assembly where they put the blue cars together. “Blue mass” the men called it. Here, where they put on the fenders, Norman would stop. Here where they really work. Where they pinch fingers and cut hands on the sharp metal. Where they bend down and stoop and look under and half stand on their heads—where it’s a struggle all the time to get the bolt holes lined up. Where the holes are never just right and a perfect fit is something to wonder about and it’s a fight all the while against time—against the little seconds that slip by and never wait for you to catch up.

One fellow that worked there had lost both his legs in an accident. He shot over the floor in between the legs of the men on a scooter. He worked under the cars, starting nuts on fender bolts, and hooked his scooter to the chassis and rode along beneath the car while doing his work. The job done, he unhooked, shot back and hooked onto another. The visitors never failed to “Oh” and “Ah” at the fellow, because he was as clever as could be, and the guides used his case to show the visitors how well the Company took care of the men who got hurt in the factory; the scooter, with nickel plated wheels and ball-bearings, having been made especially for him. On the opposite side of the line was a man doing the same kind of work, but he was not nearly so fast, nor so expert, because his legs were just so much extra weight to push around on the scooter, and, besides, they were always in the way and getting tangled up in the gear such as light cords and air hoses.

“How would you like to work here?” Norman would say.

“Not so good.”

“Makes your job look pretty sweet, don’t it?”

Then they’d walk again, past the rolling mills and the foundry and into the power house. Up the long stairs. There they’d stand in front of the big plate glass and look out.

“Stand over here,” Norman would say.

There, below, would be the steel gate and the big guard, the ex-fighter, who filled the narrow lane with his shoulders. Back from the street, at the end of the narrow passage, the employment office—a little building all by itself. Fifty-sixty men would be standing there outside the high fence, some of them lined up, some with fingers hooked in the wires, faces against the iron fence—waiting to get in—eager to get jobs—hoping for a chance to earn their five bucks a day. Most of them would be mighty proud to have a Holt badge pinned to their shirts.

“See all those fellows out there?” Norman would say. “Plenty of good men out there. Every man there’s wait-ting for a chance to step into your shoes.”

Norman wasn’t much of a talker. He wouldn’t say very much. He wouldn’t have to. Most generally the fellows he took for these walks didn’t have any trouble at all getting the point. The whole story was there.

It was four-thirty when they got back to motor assembly. The bell rang and I met Russ at the clock and we went out and grabbed us a street car. For a wonder we didn’t have to stand up.

“Well,” I said, “what happened?”

He laughed a dry laugh and looked out of the window and said nothing until the aisle was packed and the car started. Then he began talking.

“He walked me all over the place,” he began. “Took me to see the fellows work on blue mass. And then to the power house and we climbed four flights of stairs—long ones. There he stood me in front of a window and had me look out. Down below was the gate to the employment office and a long string of men trying to get in.

“ ‘See all those fellows?’ he said. “They’re just waiting to step into your job.’

“‘That what you brought me all the way up here for?’ I asked him. ‘To see that?’

“‘Yeh,’ he said. ‘Kinda makes you think, don’t it?’

“Sure does,’ I said. ‘Makes me think plenty. For one thing—it makes me think I’ve been wanting to ask you for a raise. How about it?’

“For five minutes he stood there sputtering like a steam engine with a busted cylinder head full of cold water. Too bad he can’t talk.”

“Then what?”

“Then I lit into him.

“‘How do you expect me to take care of a job,’ I said, ‘me worrying all the time trying to figure out how to get along on my lousy wages? If you want me to work faster—give me a raise. See what happens. Don’t parade me all over the place—make me climb stairs to look at a bunch of poor bastards starving to death—worse off than I am. Do a thing like that in a lumber camp, I’d hate to think what would happen to you—or how quick.’

“All of a sudden he was in a hurry to get back.

“‘You’re gonna listen to me,” I told him. “You’re gonna listen to my side of the talk. I’ve got a family. I’ve got to pay rent and buy groceries. What I’m getting won’t do it—no matter how much I worry. I’m giving you value for the wages I’m getting. If you want me to work harder, give me more money, more time. Give me enough so I won’t have to dig into my savings to keep going. Enough so I won’t have to worry about what’s gonna happen when the little money I got saved up is gone, so I’ll be able to pay the doctor when the kid comes. Don’t take me up here and try to throw a scare into me by showing me a bunch of poor fellows starving to death. I’ve got worries enough. I don’t want any more. Understand?’

He stood there and whimpered like a sick pup.

“‘I can’t help it,’ he said, ‘if I have to step on you. I gotta get out production. They’re jumping on me all the time. If my section falls down—it’s my job. They’ll give me the gate.’”

I saw how it was. All of a sudden I was sorry for him. Mr. Holt jumps on the general manager; the general manager jumps on the superintendent; the superintendent jumps on Norman; Norman has to take it out on us. We’re the butt-end of the system. Norman is just a cog in the same gear-cutter that’s working on us. He’s not very important. Not very powerful. He’s taking the dirt from those above him—I saw it all. He couldn’t even get the raise because I’m getting the same as the others.

“‘Come on,’ I said to Norman, ‘I’ve got nothing against you—personally. I lost my temper. You and me—I guess we’re just a couple of dice in the same shaker.’

“On the way back we got to talking about things. Not business, nothing that had to do with the factory. Just this and that—where he lives and what he did before he came to Detroit. He’s got a kid about a year old. And what kind of songs do you suppose he sings when he puts him to sleep after supper?”

“I don’t know.”

“Cowboy songs, he told me. ‘Little Buckaroo’ is his favorite. I’m gonna learn it. He told me where I could get the words and music.”

## Chapter XX

In the morning there was a rumor in the factory that Mr. Holt was going to shut down. Men were ganged up in the aisles and around the time clocks and when the bell rang not more than half of them were in their places.

The line got off to a sloppy start and in less than two minutes shut down. The superintendent himself pulled the switch and when he saw how things were he rallied all the straw bosses around.

“Any man,” he hollered, “not in his place when the line starts will go home.”

Straw bosses began slinging out orders; us helpers stood in shoulder to shoulder with the men, and line bosses climbed up on the buggies. The line started again but there was no speed and no snap. The men bunched up to talk and swap gossip. Rumors were all over the shop.

The superintendent threw in more speed, thinking he could force the men to tend to their work and cut out the talk, but the whole bunch from one end of the line to the other started dragging over. Norm got us helpers together. He was pretty burned up and excited.

“Listen,” he said, “it’s getttin’ serious. If it gets any worse the boss is gonna send everybody home. I want you fellas to do what you can to help put it down. No relief, see? No going to toilets. Circulate around. Hear what they’re talking about. Find out who started this talk—I wanna know.”

“Cripes, Norm,” I said, “we can’t find out who started it. It got started on the graveyard—last night. It was going strong when we got here—it’s all through the shop.”

“All through the shop?” he said. “How do you know it’s all through the shop? You sound like you been helping to talk it up, too.”

“I ain’t been talking it up,” I said. “I been trying to help put it down.”

“How you know, then, it’s all through the shop?”

“Well,” I said, “starters ain’t been coming through like they oughta. Half the motors is coming off the line without starters—that’s what they tell me. That shows something is haywire in the starter department, don’t it?”

“Listen,” he said, “you ain’t gettin’ paid for checking up on the starter department. It’s listening to that kind of talk that gets things started like this. Get busy here. Do what I said—understand?”

He tore off. There was plenty to keep Norman busy.

When the line goes haywire and wild talk spreads all through the shop—times like that us helpers can do more to get things straightened out and running smoothly again than the bosses. The bosses—even the straw bosses—are on the other side of the fence, but the men figure that us helpers belong to the gang. A helper, if he uses sense and is careful not to let the men think he’s a stool, can find out what the men are thinking and talking about, but the minute a straw boss sticks his neck into a conversation the men shut up like a clam.

The men were bunched up in Jeff’s station. Jerry was dragging over; Amos was too far ahead and right under Jeff’s elbow. Russ, on the opposite side of the line, was getting along fine because production was only about half as fast as it should have been. I squeezed in.

“Come on, fellows,” I said, “let’s break it up now. The big boss is gonna send us all home if we don’t snap into it. There’s nothing to it—this talk about shutting down.”

“Oh yea-a-aah?” said Jerry. “How do you get that way, wise guy? I just heard they shut down the blast furnace.”

“Times like this,” I said, “you’re liable to hear anything. You know how it is.”

“Yeah, we sure do,” he said. “What you’re trying to give us is a lotta bull. I’d go easy with that kind of talk here if I was you.”

“If you don’t think we’re gonna shut down,” said Amos, “maybe you’d like to buy a used car cheap. Only three more payments against it. I’ll let you have it for half what I paid for it.”

“An’ I’s gwine lose my radio,” said Jeff. “I ain’t gwine make no payments no mo’. I’s gwine write them they radio ge’mens a letter. I’s gwine say, ‘Heah’s yo radio, ge’men. Come get it.’”

It was easy to see that I couldn’t get anywhere with Norm’s idea. It was too late. The talk about laying off was more than a rumor. The conversations turned into discussions about ways and means of getting by cheaply; of stalling landlords and hanging onto the last cent of cash. The young fellows schemed and cussed loudly but the older men who were married looked serious and listened and kept their mouths shut. They were still afraid of the bosses.

In the afternoon things got even worse. About two-thirty, generators stopped coming through and we heard that the men from the generator department had been sent home. Half the buggies in the line were empties and the men started dropping out, going to the toilets, without asking permission. In doing this one fellow did the work of the man next to him as well as his own. When the first man got back they changed places. This kept the line at less than half speed, and yet us helpers were swamped. The line bosses shouted and ran back and fourth across empty buggies but they had no control over the men. The quality of the work was something fierce. A third of the motors, it seemed, were rejected and on the “tear down” line where the motors that failed to pass final inspection were taken apart and the pieces put back on the conveyors and where all power tools ran in reverse, motors were stacked high on the floor and there was a lot of confusion.

I circulated around. The toilet opposite our section was jammed and all sorts of wild tales were going the rounds. Amos, who always managed to do more than his share to get rumors started, was telling everybody that Mr. Holt was going to fire every man in the shop and that he was going to hire back only the Arkansawyers and niggers. Russian Bill was burned up because someone had told him that the whole plant was being moved to Grand Rapids because Mr. Holt wished to get away from the labor agitators who were making things bad in Detroit. One fellow who claimed his uncle worked in the front office and got all the big dope said that Mr. Holt was getting ready to come out with a Diesel car and that the plant would be closed up tight for a year.

Jerry was standing on the edge of a toilet seat looking out of a window.

“They got her shut down,” he hollered. ““There’ll be no more cylinder blocks coming through.”

“Got what shut down?” someone yelled.

“The blast furnace,” said Jerry. “They’re running off the slag. There’s a couple of H.P.’s out there—looking on.”

Norm stuck his head through the door. He saw me.

“Aw, come on down off-a there,” I said to Jerry. “Why don’t you get on the job? Us helpers are swamped.”

Jerry got down and a few of the fellows went out and Norm pushed his way in. He had a pretty dark look on his face and I thought he was going to say something but he kept his mouth shut, which was good judgment, because some of the fellows were itching to get into an argument, not caring now whether they got fired or not.

Russian Bill, who had a wife and four kids, muscled his way up to Norm.

“How about it?” he said. “Do we get laid off—or not?”

“How do I know?” said Norm. “They ain’t told me nothing about it.”

“Yeah—and if you did know something about it,” said Russian Bill, “you wouldn’t have the nerve to tell us. I’ll bet you got the lay-off notices right there in your pocket.”

“I ain’t got the time to argue with you,” said Norm, and tore out. Russian Bill laughed.

Towards the end of the shift things got even more disorganized. Two thirds of the buggies were empties and fellows began visiting about on the line, or went around trying to get tools they had loaned out to someone else. These would have to be turned in. There were accidents up in the front end of the shop where the men worked at the high speed machines. Three or four times during the afternoon the high-wheeled hospital cart raced down the aisle. In fifteen or twenty minutes it would return, hauling a man stretched out on his back and a pair of oily shoes sticking out from beneath the end of the white covering. A short time later stories would begin circulating around about what had happened but you never knew from these whether the man had actually got killed or not.

When the bell rang the notices were up near the clocks. No one was the least bit surprised. There was no definite time set for coming back and we were told to get our money on Friday.

Russ and I checked out and left. He seemed not at all downhearted.

“Well,” he said, as we passed out of the gate, “that’s that, I’ll never go back—except to get the money Mr. Holt owes me. We should have got out before—but we didn’t have the nerve. Tomorrow we’ll step out and get us a couple of real jobs.”

“Lots of other fellows will be thinking the same thing,” I said. “Some of us that’s been through it before won’t be as optimistic as you are.”

“Any time I feel like working,” he said, “I work. I’ve always been able to get a job when I wanted one. All this talk you hear around the country about men wanting work and not being able to get it—most of that’s pure bunk.”

“Ever try to get a job,” I asked, “in a town where thirty-forty thousand fellows have been laid off in one crack?”

“No.”

“Well, tomorrow,” I said, “you’ll have a chance to see what it’s like. Mr. Holt’s shut-down is just the start. Tomorrow other outfits—stores and factories and barber shops and transportation companies will be laying off men left and right. They’ll figure every way to cut overhead and they’ll sit tight until Mr. Holt opens up again. I read once where the street-car company alone loses twenty-two thousand bucks a day when Mr. Holt closes down. Other outfits lose money, too. So they all draw up their belts and sit tight. That’s what we’ll do, if we’re smart.”

“That’s all wrong, Bennie,” he said. “We’ll use our heads. The other fellows will be resting for a couple of days before getting out. We’ll get out early tomorrow—make the rounds of the factories.”

“Sounds good, Russ, but I’ve been through it before. We’ll be off three or four months, and when we get on again, we’ll be back on the line—building motors.”

“Not me. I’ve had enough of that job. In fact, I’ve had a little too much. The only time I’ll ever go back there will be Friday when I draw my last pay.”

“When you draw it,” I said, “you’d better leave the five bucks with Mr. Holt and hang onto your badge for dear life. As long as you’ve got your badge—you’re on the pay roll.”

“As long as I’ve got the badge,” he said, “Mr. Holt’s got my five bucks. When I go back to get my dough, I get all of it.”

That was the way that he felt. But during the next few days things happened that made him take my advice.

## Chapter XXI

Early the next morning Russ and I were making the rounds of the factories. But it was like I told him—they were laying men off instead of hiring. With Mr. Holt closed down, the other companies were expecting a slump. The public would hold off buying cars until it had a chance to see what Mr. Holt was going to bring out. It was not unusual for Mr. Holt to hold up his new model until after the auto shows because that way he got all the publicity to himself. According to the talk that was going the rounds, he would stay closed until January. If you listened to the talk of the men you got a pretty good idea of how much you had to pull your belt in.

At one of the big factories we couldn’t even get close to the gate—and if we had it wouldn’t have done any good because it was locked. There wasn’t even a man at the gate to encourage the men in hanging around and yet the men filled the sidewalk and overflowed out into the street. They sat in small groups on the curbs. They were not badly behaved—only sort of dull and dragged out, with hands that looked unnaturally helpless and empty. Not one expected to get in or be hired and yet they hung around, waiting for some wonder to happen.

If the gates had opened to let one man in, the crowd would have turned into a mob. There would have been a riot. At Mr. Holt’s plant the crowds are never allowed to get large. If more than fifty collect at any one time the H.P.’s come out and herd the whole bunch down the street. Big crowds hanging around employment gates are dangerous.

Russ and I circulated around through the crowd. It was a new experience for him. As usual, there were a lot of hot arguments going on and the talk was about everything except automobiles—about everything except the one thing they knew something about.

They stood around in groups talking excitedly about what was wrong with the country. A big Swede with bushy eyebrows who could hardly talk English said the country would go straight to the dogs unless all the niggers were shipped back to Africa. An old man with oil dermatitis sores on his arms was in favor of retiring Roosevelt on a pension and electing Townsend for president. One guy said that the country needed a bigger navy; another said that the country would never get over the bad effects of Prohibition—three percent beer was all right but it’d come back too late to save the good ship from piling up on the rocks. The man waved his arms and threw a fine spray of spit over the heads of the men in the crowd, and when he paused a moment to get his breath someone stuck his head through the front ranks and shouted “Aw-w-w—nuts!” A little Italian with beady eyes and a black shirt said that what was needed was a dictator with nerve enough to lay down the law to Mr. Holt and run all the Russians out of town to the opposite side of Rouge River. Nobody agreed with anybody and everybody got into the arguments and worked themselves up into a sweat. Everyone, that is, except the niggers. They took it all in and grinned and kept their mouths shut and said nothing.

On the curb there was a crap game in progress between the niggers and whites and on the far edge of the crowd two fellows, one big and one little, were working a racket. The little fellow was wearing a straight jacket. He looked dumb and bewildered and said nothing. There was a rope tied around him and the big fellow, doing his best to collect dimes and nickels. He was the spieler and carried a bunch of ropes and chains with big padlocks on them.

“All right,” he said, “I’ve got one-forty. “Tain’t enough for this act. A few more nickels and dimes and I’ll let any man in the crowd here tie another rope on him. Tie him up tight—use any kind of knot—use any kind of language while you’re doing it. If my deaf and dumb friend can’t get loose—I’ll let him lay in the gutter. I’ll throw every cent back to the crowd.”

He got a few more dimes and nickels. A big nigger stepped out of the crowd, took one of the ropes and wrapped it around the dummy’s shoulders and arms. The dummy grunted and screwed up his face in make-believe pain.

The big fellow was not satisfied. He wanted more money.

“I got two dollars and five cents,” he hollered. “I want three dollars. Three dollars and I’ll let any man in the crowd tie up his legs. Tie him up tight—treat him rough—call him names if you wanna—see if I care. Yesterday he made me lose six dollars. Had to throw it all back to the crowd.”

Once more he passed round the hat. The crowd began to take an interest in the dummy. The ropes were cutting his body. This time a white man with a rigger’s belt round his side stepped out from the crowd.

“Gimme that rope,” he cried. “Ill tie the son-of-a-bitch up. I’ll tie him up so he’ll stay tied.”

He knew his stuff. He used knots as big as your fist. Wound the rope round the little fellow’s knees and between his legs. The last knot he pulled tight—tight as all heck. He put his foot up against the little guy’s hip and heaved with all of his might. When he finished the little fellow was all trussed up like an Egyptian mummy. His arms and legs were wrapped tight so that he could no longer walk.

And yet the big fellow was not satisfied. He was after more money. The men heckled the dummy and one or two who had paid out good money took pokes at him through the inner fringe of the crowd. It was good fun—exciting. The pot jumped to four dollars. Another rope was put on—it was the last. The crowd yelled for chains and the big fellow raised the pot to five-forty. Six fellows stepped out of the crowd. An Arkansawyer threw in a quarter and settled the argument.

“Lemme at him,” he said. “I’ll tie the bastard. Ah hepped to string up a nigger back in Missouri. They had to let him down with a hack-saw.”

He walked round and round the dummy, licking his lips, working himself up into a lather.

“I’ll get this yere chain on so tight it ain’t nevva gonna come off.”

His elbows stuck out and his legs were bowed like a bantam rooster’s. When he went to work the dummy squawked and bellered. He was better at making up faces than a professional wrestler. The Arkansawyer was rough, putting the loops of the chain where they hurt most—doing a good job—kicking the little fellow in the shins when he refused to stand still. It was as good as a sideshow at a circus.

At last all the ropes and chains were put on. The dummy’s legs, arms and hands were covered. Three men laid him down in the gutter.

“And now if he can’t free himself in two minutes,” said the big fellow, “I’ll throw all this chicken-feed into the crowd. If he gets out, I keep it. Get back! Give him room to work!”

The little fellow got busy. He grunted and struggled. He rolled and twisted his body around in the gutter. Pretty soon he had two fingers free. Then one hand and a wrist. Then the other hand came out from below. Knots melted. Chains slid over his head. The coils slipped off from his legs. He stood up. He was free and the time was less than two minutes. The big fellow picked up the gear and they slipped out of the crowd and left quickly.

Some of the fellows were still good and mad at the dummy. The Arkansawyer picked up a clod and fingered it sweetly. The nigger hollered an insult. Getting us mad at the dummy was a part of the scheme to get our nickels. It was a part of the racket.

As the two of them got to the corner of the big building, a block down the street, the little fellow turned and fanned his nose at us. “That goes for all of you,” he hollered back at us. He wasn’t a dummy. The rigger picked up a rock and then dropped it. It was too far.

## Chapter XXII

A week after the lay-off Russ and I were still looking for jobs. Not that we expected to have any luck, but there was nothing else for us to do. Besides, Mrs. Rettinelli, Rita’s mother, had suddenly got interested in the coming of little Bennie and when she busted in about nine in the morning it was like trying to put up with a whirlwind in the house. We had to move out.

There was plenty Russ didn’t know about having babies. He didn’t exactly think that babies were pulled out of cabbages but at the same time, he was plenty ignorant. His education in that line had been, you might say, neglected.

Rita had long had her mind made up to go to the Holt hospital. She had heard that things were pretty slick there because Mr. Holt had carried out a lot of ideas there that he’d had a chance to work out and experiment with in the factory. The doctors punched clocks and wore badges the same as us fellows. Prices were the same for everybody—everybody went through the same system.

Rita had gone there for an examination to see if everything was all right. She had passed down the line from one doctor to another. She had told us about it. Every doctor had his own tools that were charged out to him—little jabbers and lights. Every doctor had his own little job and he did that and nothing else. They got to be experts, like us fellows. One had a hammer and tapped knees; one stuck a lamp down your throat and made you say “A-a-a-a-ah”; another checked your heart with an instrument that must have been something like those the men on final inspection use to test motors.

Both Rita and I thought the system was pretty slick but Russ made fun of it.

“From what I can figure out,” he said, “Mr. Holt has got the system down so that it’s practically automatic. When it comes to having babies all the women probably go in at one end and come out the other like the motors in motor assembly. Every doctor has his own little job on the line and when the women get to the end it’s all over. There’s probably a mechanical scrubber to run the new babies through—and someone to stamp on the numbers.”

Lots of others had funny ideas about the Holt hospital. In the factory there was a story that once went the rounds. It was about a woman who had got on the maternity line by mistake. She wasn’t even in a family way but when she got to the end of the line she had a baby the same as the others. But it was a mechanical baby—had a duo-tone finish and a mohair covering—everything but a spare tire and bumpers, and these are not standard equipment.

Russ had no idea, for instance, that a woman who was going to have a baby got funny notions about things and lost her temper easily; that she couldn’t stand certain smells, like those that came from a gasoline motor under the bed. Mrs. Rettinelli could, having had seven babies herself, and so she put the motor under my bed and told Rita not to cook bacon for breakfast. You couldn’t argue with her because when she didn’t agree with what you were trying to say she couldn’t understand a word of English, but she could talk a blue streak in Italian against you when taking Rita’s side in an argument.

The preliminaries to getting that baby were complicated and strange. Mrs. Rettinelli warned Rita about taking long steps and told her never to hold her hands over her head. To drink black coffee was as bad as having a black cat around. Dogs were all right. There were dozens of things like being left alone when it thundered; frying liver; looking out of a window at lightning; watching a funeral go past, that left marks on babies. The sight of blood was especially bad and once when Russ cut his finger and Rita was tying it up the Old Lady came in and drove us both out of the house. She got the idea that whenever Russ pulled a boner, somehow I must have had a hand in it.

One morning when we were having breakfast, another argument got started. We had just sat down when Rita said to Russ:

“There’s thirty-two dollars and fifty cents gone from the box in the drawer already. If you don’t get a job pretty soon there’s not going to be enough left for the hospital.”

“Thirty-two dollars and fifty cents gone?” said Russ. “Where’d it all go to?”

At first it looked as if the money might have been stolen but after they figured a minute they both saw that it had been spent. Russ had paid out twelve and a half for rent; she had paid the water bill and bought groceries; Mrs. Rettinelli had taken a couple of dollars to buy baby clothes, and Russ had lost two dollars in a crap game with some niggers about which nothing was said. But it made them both sit up and take notice. Russ got downright excited.

“Cripes,” he said, “here I’ve only been out of work a little more than a week and over thirty dollars is gone. That only leaves us about a hundred and ten bucks.”

“And eighty of that,” she said, “is going to pay doctor and hospital bills. I’m going to take it out and hide it somewhere. We’re not going to touch it no matter what happens.”

“That leaves us thirty.”

“Until you get a job,” she said. “Or else—until we go on relief.”
