“Never,” he said. “There’s gotta be work for me somewhere. I’ll find it.”

“Maybe.”

He got up from the table.

“Thirty dollars,” he said, “is enough to get us out of Detroit, if we leave now. It’s nothing if we get stuck here and I can’t find a job.”

“Leave now?”

“Yeh, leave now,” he said, “before we’re dead broke.”

“Where would we go?”

“Up north. I know lots of places. We could get us a cabin for a couple dollars a month. Get our wood for nothing. I could line out a string of traps and cut fence posts for the farmers. We could save up some dough.”

“I’d die,” she said. “And so would the baby. Suppose I had to have a doctor in a big hurry?”

“There are doctors up there. People have babies—even out in the sticks.”

“Don’t talk about it,” she said. “It scares me—just to think about it.”

“We’ve got to talk about it,” he said. “Things are tough. Times are bad here in Detroit. Another month of laying around and our money will be gone. We’ll be sunk. We’ve got to do something—now.”

“Well—why don’t you? Get a job. Get a job on the W.P.A. A pick and shovel won’t hurt you.”

“I can’t. Don’t you see? Carrying stones in a tin bucket—throwing dirt for some other W.P.A. worker to throw back again. That’s putting in time—wasting money. I’m not built that way. I couldn’t do it.”

“Others do it.”

“Tet erm.”

He grabbed his hat and threw it down in a chair. And then stood looking out of the window. I tried to smooth things up.

“It’s this way,” I said to him. “This is not what you’d call a depression. It’s only what us old-timers call a ‘new model recession.’ We’ve been through them before. Every year or so, it’s the same thing. We get used to ’em. What I think we should do is to sit tight until Mr. Holt opens up. When Mr. Holt calls us back the old town will come to new life. We’re on the pay roll; we’ve got our badges.”

“And he’s got our five bucks.”

“That’s all right. Knowing you’ve got a job is worth five bucks, ain’t it?”

“Not when you can’t work at it.”

“It’s this way, Russ. If we pull out now—leave Detroit—we’re liable to be out of luck when the shop opens. We’re liable to be out in the sticks when the fellows get notified to come back to work. Some other fellows would get our jobs on the line and we’d be shut out of luck.”

“And so what?”

“And so we’ve got to be here. Got to stay close to the job.”

“And starve.”

“Not exactly. But we’ve got to tighten our belts. We’ve got to work things the way the others do. It’s this way: this month you keep track of what it costs you to live. Next month you cut that in half. And the next month you spend half what you did the month before.”

“And the month after that you live on nothing.”

“You never get to that. We’ll get called back to work. But what I was going to get to was this: I’m paying six dollars for board and room. Next month I won’t be able to pay but five.”

“That’s o-keh.”

“And after that, four, till we get work.”

“That’s o-keh, too.”

“But I’ll help rustle up things. Hunks of coal from along the tracks, and vegetables. When the cold weather comes we’ll be able to get vegetables that are frozen a little. You can get them for just about nothing. Once you realize what you can save by using your head, and getting along on just a few dollars a month—it’s fun. One thing—the rent’s got to be cut right now.”

“Fun, is it?” he said. “Fun to eat frozen vegetables. And it’ll be fun, I suppose, to feed frozen stuff to the kid? And I’m the guy who used to kick like a steer about the food we ate in the camps.”

He laughed.

“That’s all right,” he said. “I can take it. But the kid’s not going to eat frozen vegetables. If I’ve got to steal—he’s going to have the best there is. Come on. Get your hat. The last payment is due on the outboard. Let’s get it over with.”

“Russ,” she said, getting up from the table. “Are you crazy?”

“We gotta pay it,” he said, “or they’ll take it back.”

“But you can’t. We need every penny to live—for hospital bills. Let them have it.”

“Certainly not. It’s the last payment. It’s only five-forty.”

“Only five-forty? That’s enough to live on a week.”

“I’m gonna pay it,” he said.

She moved towards the door of the bedroom.

“Not if I can help it,” she said. “I’ll take every cent and hide it.”

“You’re too late. I’ve got the dough in my pocket. Bennie’s board money.”

“Hand it over,” she said, coming towards him. “Give it to me. I do the cooking—I make the beds. Give it here.”

“And lose the outboard —”

“Yes, lose it. Throw it away. Get rid of it. I’m sick and tired of having that thing in the house. I hate the sight of it; I hate the smell of it. I hate it—I hate it.”

“Pipe down,” he said. “What’re you getting excited about? Where would we be next summer—clamming—without it?”

“Clamming,” she said, walking around in circles, squeezing her apron. “Clamming—clamming—clamming—it’s all you think of. It’s all you talk about. I’m sick of it. Why don’t you think about your responsibilities?—your job?—your wife?—your baby that’s coming? I don’t want him to grow up a clammer.”

“And I don’t want him to grow up a Holt worker.”

“I’m not going. If you can’t settle down and be steady —”

“Steady! Man alive, how can I?”

“If you go clamming I’ll leave you—get a job. I’ve been to high school. I’ve got an education. I’ll sell lunches—work in the dance hall again—take care of the baby.”

“And make a Holt worker outa him.”

“Give me that money!”

“I’ll not.”

“Give it to me—it’s mine. Give it to me, Russ—or get out.”

“I’ll get out.”

“And stay out,” she screamed. “Do you hear me?”

“Sure, I hear you. So do the neighbors.”

She grabbed the bread knife and came towards him. He picked up his hat and backed towards the door.

“Give it to me,” she yelled, “or I’ll throw it. Hand it over, Russ.”

The door flew open and Mrs. Rettinelli busted in.

She rushed up to Rita, took the knife, and threw both arms around her.

“Oh, Oh,” she cried, “mia bambina—mia povera—mia povera bambina!”

She turned on Russ and me. Pointed at the door and sent us both out with a blast of strong-sounding Italian. We left—slunk out like a couple of tom-cats caught in the act of eating the family canary.

## Chapter XXIII

When we left the house and went down the street, Russ was pretty discouraged about things. The old town itself was not very cheerful. Fellows were hanging around in the barber shops and in the cheap eating joints reading, too broke to buy their own papers. One restaurant had ten or fifteen men hanging around and the waiter was sitting behind the counter on a high stool with nothing to do but look out of the window. Two street railway fellows were welding a rail and a whole gang of men with oily shoes stood watching.

“Let’s bust outa here,” said Russ. “Let’s get us an old car and pull out—what say?”

“Think it over,” I said. “I know how you feel now. But you’ll feel different tonight. I’ll put down the dough for the outboard this time and you can be owing me your half. When we get home give her the six bucks.”

“I’m getting mighty fed up—her laying it into me. I’d keep a job if I had it. I’d be steady. But how’s a man gonna be steady when he can’t find nothing to do?”

“Women are funny,” I said. “It’s not your being out of a job—that’s not what’s a-bothering her. It’s your attitude—your hating the factory. Any day, she figures, you’re liable to pull out and there’ll be no more pay checks. It’s like Mr. Holt says in his book —”

“Cripes, Bennie,” he busted out, “can’t you forget Mr. Holt’s book just this once. Why try to be funny? I’m serious.”

“So’m I. I’m trying to show you how your wife feels about things—how I feel—Mr. Holt.”

“I’m not interested in knowing how Mr. Holt feels.”

“I told her I was going to go clamming. We talked it all over before we got married. She was crazy about it, thought it was fine. ‘Cute’ idea, she called it.”

“That’s before she was in a family way. Now the kid’s coming. She’s not thinking any more about having fun—having a good time for herself. She’s already thinking about educating the kid. Sending him to Mr. Holt’s school—making a straw boss outa him.”

We came to a meat market and went in, thinking we might pick up an odd job. Russ talked to the manager who was behind the counter scraping a big chopping block.

“Many fellows asking for jobs?” Russ asked.

“Plenty.”

“Some fellows ask for odd jobs, don’t they?”

“Yeah—like sweeping the floors and stuff. But I can’t do nothing for ’em. I can’t even keep busy myself.”

“Fellows real hard up, with families, would even take jobs just for a piece of beef, wouldn’t they?” he asked.

“Sure they would. Little jobs, like delivering packages and stuff. But I got a kid.”

We turned and walked out.

“What’s the idea?” I said.

“The idea of what?”

“The idea of stringing out a lotta talk like that. Why didn’t you just say: ‘Mister, you got any kind of a job for us?’”

“I can’t do it,” he said. “I feel lower than a snake’s belly, now. If I get turned down once more I’d jump off a bridge. Doing it that way, you don’t get slapped in the face.”

“Kidding yourself.”

“Maybe. But at the same time you keep enough self-respect to be able to walk up to the next man. After all, he didn’t turn me down.”

“You didn’t ask for a job.”

“I didn’t have to. I knew it wouldn’t do no good.”

We went to the store where Russ bought the outboard and made the last payment with my dough. Then we still had nearly the whole day before us. I made the suggestion that we go to Jim’s Place and hang around, but Russ knew a place on a side-track where they were unloading some coal and thought we might be able to earn a couple of bucks swinging a shovel. But when we got there, there were twenty W.P.A. men on each car. They had hardly room enough to lean on their own shovels.

We walked up Woodward a ways to a place where they were putting in a big sewer. That, too, was a W.P.A. project. Russ was disgusted. The ditch was as big as a river and all the dirt was being handled by man power and shovels. A big excavator had been pulled off the job and stood idle.

“That excavator,” said Russ, “would do more work than that army.”

“You’re always bellyaching,” I said, “about machines putting men out of jobs. What you see there oughta make you happy.”

“It’s the automatics,” he said, “that are bad. The automatics that work on production. It’s not the excavators and manipulators that make things tough for us fellows. Nobody buys a sewer. Dies are used in the factory. But the automatics are always used on production. The things those fellows you see there would like to use but can’t buy.”

“How come?”

“Because the automatics are doing the job, getting the pay checks. Last year, according to the papers, the automatics made four million automobiles. The men helped, but not enough so they can buy new cars. The automatics draw wages, too—real wages. But they are not, used to buy cars. Some goes for taxes, some into Mr. , Holt’s bank—a lot goes to buy more automatics to put * more men out of work. And now the automatics are resting—waiting for us to use up the four million cars. They’ll have to wait a long time if we’re going to earn the money on W.P.A. projects.”

It was hard to follow his arguments.

We took a long walk across town to East Davison, to Jim’s Place. It was locked up, and in front of the joint young fellows were walking back and forth, milling around, carrying big banners and signs on their backs.

“What the hell,” said Russ. “It’s picketed.”

Sure enough. We read the signs. “More Cuspidors,” one said. “Fix Walls,” another. “Better Loafing Conditions,” “New Radio,” “Level Pool Tables,” “Air Condition,” “Kill Cockroaches.”

We eased up. There were twenty-thirty young fellows. A guy who went by the name of “Pop-eye,” was the president. They had a vice-president, too. A couple of cops stood off a block and looked on. I knew some of the fellows. They wanted us to join in, but I didn’t like the looks of the set-up. Not but what the place needed improvements, all right. Cockroaches had gray whiskers and were long as mice, but there were too many chances for arguments. Let some guy get excited and heave a rock through a window and the cops would pile over and gather the whole bunch in.

A big guy by the name of “Moon Mullins” stuck a sign on the door with the “Demands of the Committee” and Jim, the owner, stuck his head out of the window upstairs and told Moon, in strong language, to keep his hands off the joint. The fellows jeered back and Jim let out a yell and waved for the cops to come over.

“Come on,” said Russ, “let’s tear outa this mess. Things are gonna get hot.”

Going up the street, not more than two or three blocks away from the place, we saw the squad wagon tear down the boulevard, two cops in front and two hanging onto the railing behind.

We circulated around, trying to get odd jobs in stores, parking stations, beer joints and restaurants, but we had no luck and in the afternoon we found ourselves on Hastings Street, in the colored district.

“Always make me feel good,” said Russ, “to get into this neighborhood and just loaf awhile. There’s a different feeling—a different atmosphere here.”

“Different smell, too,” I said.

“Sometimes I wish I’d been born a few shades darker,” I said Russ. “Niggers always seem to have a good time. Give ’em a W.P.A. job and they’re happy. If they lose the job and go on relief, they have a good time just the same. White fellows worry. They lose their jobs and slink around like a bunch of hungry coyotes. They beat the landlords and the landlords try to beat them; they skin the grocer and the grocer tries to do them dirt. Among the whites it’s dog eat dog—all the time. I never seen nothing like it.”

The dirty street was swarming with people, mostly Negroes. Down below, near the river, the government had started a new housing project. Wreckers tore down the houses, engineers killed the rats and blew up the old foundations, and new buildings like barracks were put up for the niggers to live in. But the niggers packed their junk into two-wheeled carts and old cars and moved up the street, pushing the colored district a little farther out as they went. It looked like the engineers would never catch up.

The niggers, standing in little groups, sauntering along the broken sidewalks, looking at the displays of congealed stew, ham hocks, and boiled cabbages in the restaurant windows, were all happy as kids. There was a lot of laughing and knee-slapping at stories that didn’t amount to nothing, and plain loafing. Nobody seemed to have any business that needed attention. Across the street a couple of colored janes with a French poodle came out of a door that led up a narrow stairs. Anybody could see they were whores, but as they went down the street the colored fellows—friends and customers—touched their hats or made strictly decent remarks. They were treated like ladies.

The old stores and houses, close together, leaned against each other as though they were tired. In a window, right near us, there were piles of bacon rinds in rolls, a tin platter of tripe, oxtails—blue with age—herrings, and a string of wild rabbits, and gallon-jars of buttermilk that looked like soft cheese floating on the thinnish blue liquid. Twenty feet down the alley lay a dead dog with legs stiff and outstretched; and a gray cat, with sides like an empty purse, picked its way cautiously across the uneven street through six inches of garbage, old papers, tin cans, and ashes. Down the line a ways a dog fight got started. Heads popped out of windows; men flocked out into the street and traffic was tied up. Russ and I were moving along when a colored kid twelve-fourteen years old stopped us. He carried a box that hung by a strap from his shoulder.

“Yo-all wanna see what I got’s heah?” he said.

We looked. It was a rat—bigger than any I’d ever seen in the back streets of Detroit. He had yellow tusks like a boar and a snout like a wart-hog. He was scarred up something fierce. One ear and a part of the tail were clean gone.

“Judast!” said Russ.

“They’s gwine be a fight at fo-thutty.”

“A fight?”

“Yeah.”

“What kinda fight?”

“Rat and cat fight. Napolean, here, is gwine fight a big tom-cat.”

“How many rounds?”

“No rounds. Jist till one gets laid out.”

“You selling tickets?”

“No, sah. Mr. Felix, he sell ’em. He be along directly, I ’spect.”

The kid passed up the street, stopping now and then—showing the rat and explaining his fine points as a fighter. In a couple of minutes Mr. Felix came up. He looked prosperous in a black derby.

“Ah see you talking,” he said. “ ’Spects you-all’d like a couple of tickets.”

“How much?” said Russ.

“Fifteen-a-piece. Two fo quatah.”

“Where’s the cat?”

“Ovah theah.” He pointed diagonally across the street. There was another kid with a box slightly larger, selling tickets and working up bets on the cat.

The colored gentleman pulled out some small squares of cardboard. On them there was a pencilled address.

“Can’t spare the money,” said Russ.

“Ah’m taking bets, too.”

“How’s the odds?”

“They’s even. Yo-all ge’men see dat cat yet?”

“No.”

“Must be some cat. What chance has a rat got to clean off a cat?’

“Tha they rat’s a pow’ful mean rat.”

“He didn’t look mean. He looked scared.”

“Tha’s a way he’s got. He don’t look mean even when he’s fightin’. Ah wish yo-all ge’men could see the kind look on tha they rat’s face when he’s mad.”

“Get’s mad, eh?”

“Oh, sho. Get’s pow’ful mad. But he don’t let on how he’s mad. Let’s the cat tear into him, fust. He don’t fight back, or nothin—looks kindhearted and sad.”

“Then what?”

“Tha cat lays off and sets back. ‘Tha ain’t sich a bad rat,’ he thinks to hisself. ‘Ah’s gwine leave him be!’ And then the rat he done do’s his stuff. With the lovin’est look in his face he noses up to the cat and sorter nudges his way under till he most gets clean outer sight. Slow and easy he does it—nice and gentle like he’s trying to make friends. Then he flips hisself over and rips the cat’s belly wide open. Splits out the guts. Tha cat makes on-n-ne pow’ful leap and leaves half hisself on the floo’. Yo-all ge’men wanna put a buck on the rat?”

“We ain’t spending,” said Russ.

We loafed around some more. Went down the street a ways and met Jeff. He’d lost his radio but was getting by fine. Said he hadn’t got down yet to picking up snipes. To him the lay-off was just a chance to be more sociable and hang around more.

“How you fixed for bu’ning wood?” he asked Russ.

“Not so good. I’m about. Have to buy some, I guess.”

“With money?”

“Sure, what else?”

“Don’t nevva spend no money for bu’ning wood,” said Jeff. “They’s gwine be a house-wrecking next Friday. Evybody’s welcome to come.”

He started fumbling around in his pockets for an address.

“Can’t find it,” he said. “They’s colo’d people. They was buying till the man he got laid off. The holdin’ company give ’em notice to clear out.”

We had heard about those house-wrecking bees. It was a good way to get wood if somebody in the neighborhood didn’t call the cops. Sometimes only a part of the house was carried away by the men. Sometimes there was nothing left but a cellar hole full of old plaster.

It was late afternoon and it was a long ways to the house. We drifted along. Half-way home we came into another bunch of pickets. This time it was a large, fifty-family apartment. Men and women and kids were walking back and forth in front of the building, displaying big cards that read: “We demand lower rents,” “No Unjust Evictions,” “Human Rights First,” “We Are Striking for Low Rents, Landlord Unfair,” etc. A good-looking blonde wanted Russ and me to join the parade. She said the refreshments would be out pretty soon and tried to string a couple of signs the size of barn doors on us, but when we asked for details about the eats, she was vague about things.

Near the house we stopped in to talk to a man who ran a feed and fuel place. In his usual roundabout way Russ led up to the matter of work and found out that the man had just laid off three men. The fuel business, the man said, was so rotten it stunk.

“Maybe you can tell me,” Russ said, “what all these families around here do to keep warm.”

“Sure,” he said. “They’re not using their furnaces. They set up a tin stove and all live in one room.”

“But they’ve still got to have fuel.”

“Old boxes,” he said. “They go along through the alleys and pick up old boxes and stuff. I see ’em go by here with carts and old baby buggies piled high with junk, dragging it home. Some of ’em even burn old tires—makes a hell of a stink.”

Russ got an idea. He could hardly keep his mouth shut until we got in the street.

“Bennie,” he said, “there’s an idea for us. Instead of wearing out good shoe leather looking for odd jobs, we’ll cut down expenses. If we save a dime, it’s the same as making it. We’re gonna start getting in wood for the winter. It’ll be something new in lumbering for me.”

Before we reached home, he stopped in a hardware store and laid out two-seventy-five for an axe. It was decorated with red paint and nickel plate—had two cutting edges and was sharp as a razor. When we passed a shade tree near the sidewalk it was all he could do to restrain himself from going to work on it with the new axe.

## Chapter XXIV

The weeks dragged along and it was November and most of the eighty bucks that Rita had stuck away to pay hospital bills had been spent for groceries and rent, and Mrs. Rettinelli, who had a way of figuring out things, said that Rita’s time was near. Not being able to go to the hospital made Rita feel glum but it had the opposite effect on the Old Lady because she had the idea that anyone who went into a hospital—even Mr. Holt’s—was practically a goner. Besides, having the baby at the house gave her a chance to superintend the job to the end. She went about the house running things and Russ let her do as she pleased until one day she tried to convince Rita that Tony would be a better name for the kid than Bennie. Then Russ put his foot down and let her know that he was the boss.

Mr. Holt’s factory was still closed up tight as a drum. Once a rumor got started around that he was going to open, but when November was more than half gone and there was still no sign of work, the talk spread that we would go back in December. There was nothing to do but sit tight and wait. Russ and I had plenty of time on our hands and we used it, mostly, for figuring out ways to hold down expenses. We cut each other’s hair and washed our own clothes. Fancy packages never came into the house and instead of buying cereals and stuff in pretty boxes all done up in waxed paper and cellophane, Russ went to a feed yard and bought corn meal and cracked wheat for less than five cents a pound. Stale bread we got for half price and after being steamed in the oven you couldn’t hardly tell but what it was fresh. Meat we didn’t have very often but when we did lay hard cash on the counter for a pound of hamburger we spoke about having a big dog and asked the butcher to throw in a bone. This we used to make soup.

As the weather grew colder, getting wood kept us pretty busy. From the swamp in back of the house Russ salvaged a raft made of old doors that the neighborhood kids had left stranded in two feet of mud. It was too wet to burn good. Three or four times a week we searched the alleys behind stores for discarded boxes. These we dragged home and Russ broke them up with his axe. They burned like paper and on a cold day it was a two-man job to keep a fire going right—one man to haul in the stuff while the other fiddled around with the drafts and dampers. And even then the old stove was either red hot or stone cold—either going to beat the band, or clear out. The chimney had a habit of catching on fire and the first time it happened Russ, all excited, climbed up on the roof with a bucket of salt and smoked us out. Coming down he near busted a leg and after that when sparks started shooting out into the yard we let her roar.

Early on Thanksgiving Day, before daylight, Russ came into my room and woke me up.

“Get on your stuff,” he said, all excited, “call the Doc and get the Old Lady. It’s started.”

I was awake in a jiffy, grabbing my clothes.

“Hurry,” he said, “the kid’s liable to come before they get here. Never mind your shirt. Grab your shoes.”

In the other room I heard Rita. It was a sort of low moan.

“How long’s that been going on?” I asked, making a dive for my shoes.

“Just started,” he said. “Don’t stop to fasten your shoes. I’m liable to have a kid on my hands here, Bennie. What’ll I do?”

“Keep your shirt on. Be a couple of hours yet—maybe.”

“Cripes,” he said, “I don’t know.” He tore back into the room and popped out again as I grabbed my coat and made a pass for the door.

“Hurry, Bennie,” he hollered as I busted out of the house; “and come back—don’t leave me alone.”

There was a telephone in an eating joint two blocks away that stayed open all night. I got the number and hung onto the phone. After what seemed like an hour, Doc answered.

“Better get over here right quick,” I said. “Things are happening.”

He didn’t seem to get what I meant. Yawned a couple of times and asked questions. Wanted to know how long she had been having pains—how often they came.

“Judast, Doc,” I said. “I don’t know nothing about that. All I know is the lady is having a kid and the guy’s throwing a fit.”

He said something about nature taking its course. “Be hours yet,” he said. “You tell that big guy not to worry. Tell him to calm down—I’ve handled lots of baby cases and never yet lost a father.”

“Listen, Doc,” I said, “he’s going in circles. If I told him a crack like that—I don’t know what would happen to you or me either. Better jerk your pants on and get over.”

At Rettinelli’s I got more action. The Old Lady had her things packed and grabbed a street car. Then Retti-nelli got out and we made coffee and got us some breakfast. After that we sat around a while and just talked. I wasn’t in a hurry to get back. Thought I’d give the kid lots of time to get born because I didn’t want to get back there in the middle of things. With the Old Lady and Russ there at the same time and both excited, things would be going round and round.

About nine I left Rettinelli’s and went over to Jim’s Place.

There was only two or three guys there, hanging around. I couldn’t see that picketing the place had changed things none. One thing was sure—the same cockroaches were still there because I recognized an old boy who’d lost his whiskers one time in a fight. But there was a new sign on the wall —

Play Ball or Get Out.

I explained to Jim how things was at the house.

“Sure,” he said, “stick around all you wanna. Turn on de radio and stuff. It’s only for dose oddas I got-a sign up. For dose son-of-a beeches what tries to tell me how I shoulda run deesa place.” Jim was all right if you knew how to take him.

I hung around till noon and then went to a movie, after which, I figured it was about time to go home and see if it was a boy. But when I was still a half a block away from the house I knew I was early. The Doc’s coupe, with a blanket over the radiator, stood at the gate and I heard Rita. Not soft, gentle moans like in the morning, but loud. It was enough to make a man freeze in his tracks. I figured it was no time to turn back—they might be needing me bad.

In the house things were in a wild state of affairs. Mrs. Rettinelli was running back and forth between the stove and the bedroom. Russ was going around in circles kicking the furniture. The fire had gone out and the house was stone cold.

“What’s the matter?” I asked Russ.

“Cripes, I don’t know, Bennie. It’s been going on like this for hours.”

Doc came out into the room and left the door open. He had his sleeves up—no coat. Through the door I could see the edge of the bed. It had been moved a little away from the wall. The closet door stood open and there, up on a shelf, I could see the red box in which Rita kept her silver shoes and black dancing dress.

“Get some wood,” Doc told me. “Keep that fire going. See if you can make a job of it.”

Rita cried out again, four-five times in succession. She sounded desperate and mad. “Us Rettinelli’s are all fighters,” she had once told me. Her cries rose to a high, screaming pitch and then dropped to a low gurgling moan. Russ’ face twisted all out of shape. He spoke to Doc who was looking for something in a black bag that sat on a chair.

“It’s killing her, Doc. Can’t you do something?”

“She’s all right, I tell you.”

“It’s been going on for hours—getting worse all the time.”

“Big baby—she’s little.”

“But it’s killing her—she’s gonna die.”

“She’s not going to die, I tell you. First babies always come hard.”

“Give her something—kill the pain, can’t you? Get it over with.”

“She’s got to help. I can’t do anything alone.”

The screams came again. Throaty screams, from deep down. Through clinched teeth and set jaws—in a struggle to keep down the pain. Mrs. Rettinelli was with her—spoke soft sounding words in Italian.

Russ sent a chair spinning over the floor.

“I can’t stand it,” he said. “It’s enough to tear a man’s heart out, Doc. Give her something—give her something—stop it!”

He grabbed Doc by the arm.

“Can’t give her anything now,” said Doc. “She’s got to help—I tell you.” He jerked free. Looked at his arm. He had a syringe in his hand.

Russ came towards him again. Once, I saw a guy with D.T.’s.

“Doc, if she dies—it’ll come out of your hide. Understand what I’m telling you?”

“Listen, big boy,” said Doc, “I’m going to get hard in a minute. I’ve had enough out of you. Put on your hat and get out. Get out and don’t come back till I let you in. Get —”

“I can’t, Doc. I——”

“Get out!”

“I’ll keep my mouth shut!”

“Get out, or I’ll throw up the job. I don’t want you around. The way you been carrying on here—get out.”

Russ sobered up.

“You’re right, Doc,” he said. “I can’t take it.”

I got his hat, put it on him. Rita started again and he rushed into the bedroom.

“Oh, baby,” he kept saying over and over, “I wish I could help—I wish I could help.”

I went out to get an armful of wood and when I got back he was gone and Doc was trying to blockade the door with a chair.

There was a pan of water on the stove with some rubber gloves, needles and pieces of gut in it. Also, a white porcelain tank with a cover, that Doc must have brought with him.

I started a fire and then began peeling potatoes, not knowing why, except that I wanted things to be natural again—to have the place seem like a kitchen again and not like a hospital.

Doc came and stood at the stove and poked at the rubber gloves to make them stay down. He stood with his feet wide apart. He had small hands but big, hefty arms with hair on them—the kind of arms that would have done a good job changing a tire.

“Can’t do a thing with that fellow around here,” he said. “Comes back again I’m going to throw him out—he’s a nuisance.”

Rita started in again. The pains must have been something fierce, but he never let on to hear her. He was the rough sort—the kind that could take off a man’s leg and be telling a story about Mae West.

“Everything all right, Doc?” I asked.

“How do I know?” he said. “In a case like this, you never know till it’s over. And even then—you don’t know. She should be in a hospital where there’s something to work with. It’s a crime, trying to deliver a baby in a place like this.”

“Nothing to worry about—yet?”

“May have to use forceps. Don’t like to tear her up more’n I have to. Get that water boiling quick as you can.”

“Forceps dangerous?” I asked.

“Not necessarily dangerous,” he said. “Don’t like to use ’em if I don’t have to, that’s all. Only thing around here that’s dangerous is that big guy—trying to tell me what to do. If he comes back here— No fun for me either. My wife’s got a big turkey all cooked. I had plans made to go out into the country this afternoon—shoot rabbits.”

He lit a cigarette, looked at his arm.

“That guy’s strong,” he said.

“Plenty. If he comes back in,” I said, “you’d better let me handle him. He’ll generally listen to me.”

The cries from the bedroom grew louder, more desperate. They were almost continuous.

“Won’t be long, now,” he said. “I’ll give her something pretty soon to ease up the pain.”

He walked to the window and looked out.

“What’s this?” he said. “There’s somebody out there on the porch.”

It was old Rettinelli, standing there with his hat in his hand, too scared to knock or come in. I took the chair away and opened the door.

“Was ’s-a matta?” he asked. “Little To-nee—'ee’s-a not come yet?”

“Not yet,” said Doc.

“You t’ink ’ee’s come pretty soon—queek maybe?”

“I think so. Be along pretty soon, I guess.”

The old man stood there fiddling around with his hat. It looked like there was something more he wanted to say.

“Is already long time,” he said.

“Yeh, I know,” said Doc. “Can’t be helped, though. The girl—she’s all right. When it’s all over, we’ll let you know.”

Then I saw Russ coming back down the street with long steps. Mrs. Rettinelli came out on the porch and talked to the Old Man in Italian. Doc and I stepped back into the house. Russ barged in through the door.

“Has she been calling me, Doc?”

“I thought I told you—stay out.”

“I could hear her, Doc —”

Rita cried out again. He went into the room. Mrs. Rettinelli came back into the house and hurried into the bedroom. Her eyes were red. She looked pretty darn serious.

“Well—” I said to Doc, “he’s back again.”

“Tell me something,” he said, “I don’t know. He’s going out again—if I have to use force.”

Doc had the white tank on the table and was busy fishing out some big instruments with wide blades and strong handles. He put them carefully on a tray.

“Don’t touch them,” he said. “Keep that fire going all the time.”

Russ came back into the room.

“Doc,” he said, “she’s getting worse.”

“Listen,” said Doc, “if you’ll just let me alone—get out of here—stay out —”

“But she’s getting worse. She’s losing her nerve.”

“She’s all right, I tell you. If you’ll get out, give me a chance to do something, I’ll probably get you a big healthy baby.”

“Probably—” he said, “probably. Things are not going right. You know it. You’re keeping things from me.”

He saw the instruments on the table.

“What are these things?” he said.

“My instruments—what else?”

“You’re going to take it away from her. You’re —”

“Get away from that table.”

“You’re keeping things from me, Doc. You know it. I want another doctor in here—to take charge.”

Doc was beginning to get sore.

“I’m handling this case,” he said.

“Then do something,” said Russ. “Do something. She’s getting weak. Hear that? She’s not so loud—no fight. Do something—can’t you?”

“I will—if you’ll give me a chance.”

“Quick, then; before it’s too late. She’s losing her strength—getting weak.”

“She’s all right, I tell you.”

“She’s not all right, Doc. You lie ——”

“All right,” said Doc, starting to roll down his sleeve, “I’m through.”

Russ grabbed his arm.

“No—no—” he said, “don’t go. I don’t know what I’m talking about, Doc. I’m crazy.”

“Now, listen,” said Doc. “I want you to get out and stay out. Stay out till I’m through. Don’t come back till I let you in. Go now—or I leave.”

I got an idea. I took Russ by the arm.

“Listen,” I said, “Doc’s right. He can’t do nothing with you tearing around here. I never see you so rattled—you act worse than a kid. You gotta get out—give him a chance.”

“I gotta be near, Bennie. I can’t be out on the street—if she calls me.”

“You’ve gotta get out, stay out. Get your axe and work up that old willow in the back yard. We’ve gotta have wood.”

“I’d like to, Bennie,” he said. “But Rita ——”

“What good’s the old tree,” I said. “It’s half dead. We’ve gotta have wood. We’re clean out.”

The idea worked. He got the axe and went out the back door, and then wanted to come back for his coat.

“Never mind your coat,” I told him. “Work hard, you won’t get cold.”

“Stand at the window,” he said. “Anything goes wrong—I wanna know. I didn’t know it’d be like this, Bennie. I’m worried—I’m scared stiff.”

“Everything’s o-keh,” I said. “Doc told me. That cry- } ing don’t mean a thing—they all do when they’re having kids.”

I went back into the house. I fixed up the fire and then stood at the window and looked out.

In the bedroom things were going on. Doc was working—staying close to the job. Rita’s cries were not so sharp any more—not so loud either. They were dull groans but they still had some fight in them.

I heard Doc. “Now rest,” he would say. “Take it easy. Relax. Now get ahold of that sheet. Do your stuff. Grit your teeth. That-a girl. Fine.”

Then she’d rest. And then, in a minute or so, another spasm would hit her and she’d go through the same thing again. It was awful—just to be there—to hear it. Things didn’t seem real in the house. Things seemed like a bad dream that had no end to it. I got to wondering—got to thinking if that was the way I’d got born.

“Mrs. Rettinelli got more excited—but not nervous like Russ. She ran back and forth, put another pan of water to heat on the stove, hung some cloths on a chair set back to the fire. “Kip going,” she told me, meaning the fire.

Outside the sky got colder and darker. A fine drizzle began and there was not a bit of light in the sky where the sun was going down behind the row of gray shacks on the other side of the swamp.

I watched Russ. He had the old willow down. Standing over the black, knotty trunk he swung mighty strokes. Chips the size of a man’s hand flew off with each powerful stroke of his axe. In the dusk, lying there in the mud on the ground, the old tree looked like a dead dragon. Russ had his foot on the body, lopping off the legs and the claws, leaving white scars. Once more—he had control over things.

I got the smell of chloroform or ether. It was chloroform, I think. Mrs. Rettinelli got a pan of water from the stove and when she went back into the room she closed the door behind her. Doc’s voice again—I couldn’t hear what he said. Dull groans from Rita that turned to ear-splitting shrieks. I was afraid Russ would hear—come back again.

Then all was quiet. There were movements behind the closed door. Doc’s voice again—talking, this time, to the Old Lady. And then I heard a faint squeak. After all the noise—all the excitement—all the big pains—that little squeak sounded pretty darn small.

A minute or two went by and then Mrs. Rettinelli came out with something red and small in a cloth. She took a blanket from the chair and spread it out on the table.

“Ross-s-s,” she said. “You call—now.” Doc must have heard her.

“Don’t -call,” he yelled. “I don’t want him in yet.”

Russ had the tree nearly worked up. The rain drops began to make a noise on the roof. Yellow lights from the shacks across the swamp began to show up through the darkness.

Mrs. Rettinelli washed the baby—rubbed it with some sort of oil, and then wrapped it up. I got a look at its face. It was wrinkled like a turnip that’s been left in the cellar too long and is all puckered up.

Doc came out.

“All right,” he said. “I’ve got the china packed. Let in the bull if you want to.”

I went to the back door and let out a whistle. He came up on a run.

“We can call it Bennie,” I said. “It’s a boy.”

“Rita—” he said. “Is she all right?”

“They’re both fine and dandy,” I said.

Doc was packing his tools.

“May I see her?” asked Russ. Now that it was all over, he was meek enough. Mrs. Rettinelli was holding the baby but he never even went near it.

“Sure,” said Doc. “Don’t make it too long. I want her to sleep.”

Russ went into the bedroom and dropped down on his knees to the floor. He tried to cheer her. Mrs. Retti-nelli went in too, with the baby. Now they would be happy, I thought, now that it was all over and they three—Rita and Russ and the baby—were together. They would be happy, I thought, and wouldn’t need me and I would be left out. I felt terribly alone. I edged towards the door. Rita was talking to Russ.

“I’m tired,” she said. “I’m awfully tired, Russ. It was a long time.”

“Too long,” he said. “I’m sorry you had to do it alone—terribly sorry. I wanted to help—but I couldn’t.”

There were tears in his eyes. I was ashamed to be there, looking on. Yet I wanted to stay.

“Did I hear you chopping?” she asked.

“Yes,” he said. “Doc and Bennie wouldn’t let me stay in the house. I cut up the willow. We were all out of wood.”

“You cut it?” she said. “Cut it down?”

“Yes.”

“Oh Russ —”

Mrs. Rettinelli pushed forward. She felt something was wrong.

“Il bambino,” she said. “Qui il bambino, Rita.”

She put it down beside Rita.

“Oh Russ,” Rita said, “how could you do it? How could you?”

She turned her face to the wall. She didn’t even notice that the baby was there.

## Chapter XXV

By the middle of December things were tough. According to the papers forty-fifty thousand families were on relief and the mayor spent half his time going back and forth to Washington, trying to raise dough. I had fifty dollars left in the sock but Russ was getting down pretty low. He had bought a snow shovel and an ice spud and earned a few quarters cleaning sidewalks, but about the only real dough that he saw was the four dollars a week I paid in. And a good share of that went for some high-powered baby food that cost like the deuce.

Rita wanted Russ to go on the dole.

“Others do it,” she argued, “why don’t you?”

“Just the idea,” he said, “gives me the willies. Every day I see ’em, standing in rows at the fire houses, trying to hide their faces behind their coat collars—waiting out in the cold.”

“We gotta eat.”

“I suppose so,” he said. “But I don’t like to do it. It’s all right for fellows that are sick or can’t work, but I’m healthy—I’m strong. A big guy like me ought to be out bucking the cold and deep snow—swinging an axe out in the woods—earning his keep. I was brought up to think that if a man was willing and able to work he could get along without asking for hand-outs.”

She kept after him so much that one morning he pulled his hat down over his eyes and went out. He was gone the whole day and when he got back he had nothing—not even a hunk of good government bacon or a sack of number two spuds like the Rettinelli’s was getting.

“What’s the matter?” asked Rita. “We’ll have a pretty slim supper. I was figuring you’d bring something.” She was frying potatoes.

“My feet are frozen,” he said. “Been standing in line all day. They shoot you from one place to another. Ask all kinds of questions. Even wanted to know if we kept a boarder.”

He grabbed a chair and stuck his feet in the oven.

“How’s the kid been?” he asked.

“Fine, Russ. He’s in the bedroom—sleeping. But why wouldn’t they give you nothing?”

“I’d rather not talk about it. Rather forget about the whole mess.”

“But tell me —”

“They asked if we had any money.”

“And you told them—how stupid. They’re not going to search the house.”

“It’s bad enough to have to beg—without lying.”

“It wouldn’t be a very big lie,” she said. “Do you know how much we’ve got left?”

“No.”

“Six dollars.”

“Is that all?”

“That’s all. And when that’s gone you’ll have to do something—sell the outboard.”

“Never. First I’ll turn in my badge. Get the five bucks.”

“You would. And let someone else get your job when the factory opens.”

Sometimes Russ didn’t use his head. At other times you had to give him lots of credit. When he made soup, for instance. He would spend fifteen cents for a knuckle joint of beef, chop up a few carrots and onions, throw in a handful of barley, and make what he called lumber-jack soup. It was like nothing you ever got in a restaurant. Even the kid liked it.

And he was good about bringing home fruits and vegetables, after dark, mostly. Some of the stuff was frozen, some partly spoiled, but trimming and washing made it all good. After all, the main thing was to get by. I never asked where he got it and he never told me, either, until one night he came home with a whole case of frozen celery. He had a cut on the side of his mouth and one ear was puffed up like a turnip. He’d been fighting—I could see that in a minute.

“A couple of Polacks,” he said, “tried to take it away from me. Claimed they saw it first. We went round and round in the alley.”

About half the case was good. We had celery for two weeks.

He would have had more luck rustling up odd jobs if he hadn’t stayed home so much fooling around with the kid. Nothing gave him more fun than to get the kid out by the stove and change a diaper, or let him play with his finger.

“Look at that,” he’d say, kidding me, “notice the way he hangs on. The little bastard thinks he’s got ahold of a speed wrench. He’s gonna be a straw boss some day.”

Down in his heart he was scared to death the kid would be a straw boss in a factory. He had the idea that the kid would furnish the argument to get Rita to leave Detroit and move out into the country.

“Bennie,” he once said to me, when he was serious, “I can’t think that Rita would want him to grow up in the dirt and noise of these streets. What a kid needs when he’s growing up, is lots of fresh air; room to tear around, and a chance to learn something about animals and trees.”

And she had the idea somehow that the kid would make him settle down in the factory and be steady—that it would make him forget his wild dreams about clamming and roaming about in a trailer. She talked to me, confidential.

“He’ll see it the way I do,” she would say, “when the baby gets older. He’ll want him to have advantages—get an education like I did. Just think how awful it would be if he’d get sick—way out in the country. He might be dead before we could get to a doctor. I want him to go to the Holt School.”

They were so far apart in their plans for the kid they didn’t dare to talk to each other about them. They talked to me.

The auto shows came and went and some of the auto factories got into production but Mr. Holt stayed closed. The auto business was dead. There were too many used cars on the market, for one thing, and those who wanted new cars and had the money to pay for them, didn’t buy, because they wanted to see first what Mr. Holt’s new model was going to be like. The factories that had started up ran long enough to fill up the show-rooms and then they shut down again and Detroit was deader than ever.

And then one day, right after Christmas, we heard a rumor that Mr. Holt was going to call back twelve thousand men. The next morning at daylight, Russ and I were lined up at the gate. So were a big bunch of others, both niggers and whites.

It was seven before the big guard came into his booth. But he didn’t take the lock off the gate. I held Russ’ place while he went up and talked to the guard through the steel gate. They were not hiring, he said, just a rumor. He didn’t have any idea when the plant would open. Cards or telegrams, he said, would be sent when the company wanted us back.

Still he was there and that meant something—enough to keep the men lined up for a couple of hours. And then they left their places and the line broke up and the fellows started milling around in the slush and wet snow, trying to get near the gate, trying to talk to the guard, to ask questions. Some of them had papers showing they had good reputations and didn’t belong to no unions, or that they had bought a Holt car on time and were paying for it. Others had letters from the relief workers or politicians saying they needed work, and one fellow who crowded up and was loud in his talk and behavior had a letter three pages long telling all about his refinement and good character. Among other things it said that he never drank, used tobacco or swore, but when he pushed the letter through the bars of the gate and the guard, who was reading a newspaper, let it fall into the mud, he let out a string of cuss words what would have put a steel rigger to shame.

Russ began to get bored. Doing nothing never satisfied him long.

“Let’s beat it,” he said. “Nobody’s gonna get in. With this mob here the fellow would be crazy to open that gate.”

“Take it easy,” I said. “The mob won’t be here long—you see. Cops will be along pretty soon and herd the whole bunch down the street.”

“Think so? I’d like to get home, wring out my shoes.”

An old sedan with an Arkansas license plate and with four hill-billies in it drove up to the curb and came to a wheezing stop next to a fire plug. The rear windows were gone and a hill-billy with a woman’s shawl around his neck pulled back a strip of burlap and stuck out his head.

“Mister Holt been down yet this mo’ning?” he asked Russ.

“Not yet,” said Russ, acting serious. “We’re expecting him though, any minute. You fellas looking for work?”

“Yeah—bo. We-all’s just rarin’ to work.”

“What kind of car you got there?” asked Russ. “One of Mr. Holt’s?”

“No, sah.”

“Then you’d better get it outa here before Mr. Holt sees it,” he said. “I was you I’d park it way over there on the other side of them tracks. Mr. Holt see you driving a foreign-make car —”

The piece of burlap went down and the old car started out with a jerk. It showed a good bit of speed, considering its age and looks. Neither did it stop until it had got several blocks on the other side of the tracks. Then the hill-billies spilled out and walked back to the gate.

In the crowd we came across Jerry and Jeff. Jeff told us about a house-wrecking bee that was coming off in a couple of days. He said the house was all wood, the neighbors on both sides o-keh, and asked us to come. Our supply of old boxes was low and the wood from the old willow had all been used up. We said we’d be there although it would mean getting up pretty early.

Then Jerry took Russ and me aside, away from the crowd. He had something he wanted to tell us, something confidential, he said.

“I gotta get in there,” he said. “I been coming down here every day. Yesterday they let in a bunch.” Then he stopped. A couple of fellows were coming.

“That what you wanted to tell us?” said Russ.

“Wait till these fellows get past.” He was acting mighty mysterious and nervous. “Gotta be careful,” he said.

Inside the factory there was some activity. A little light smoke floated away from the tops of high stacks on the power plant. A switch engine pushed a string of cars loaded with machines into the plant. As it got closer we could see the machines were all streamlined automatics—gear-cutters, mostly. The coke ovens were running full blast though there was not a man in sight. A steel car moved and then stopped; an iron door raised up towards the sky and a glacier of white hot coke crumbled and fell ponderously into the car. The car moved past the long row of black ovens into the quenching towers and stopped; tons of water sprayed down and from the squat tower a cloud rose and drifted over the plant, raining a shower of tiny black cinders down on our heads.

“A man wouldn’t last long,” said Jerry, “if he was to jump into that car.”

“Get his feet dry,” said Russ. “What’s this you were going to tell us?” The two fellows had passed.

“Last week,” Jerry said, talking softly, “I got an invitation to join the Suicide Club of Detroit.”

“Swell,” said Russ. “How do they feed you?”

“Na-a-ah, that’s not the idea. They don’t feed you.”

“What good’s it then?”

“The idea is—you go there to read—meet fellows and talk.”

“You can do that in a pool hall.”

“It’s not the same thing.”

“Well,” said Russ, “did you join?”

“Na-a-ah, the place was full of screwballs. Every guy there’s got a wheel loose. The first night some fellow got me off in a corner. ‘Listen,’ he said, ‘I’ll tell you something, if you’ll keep you mouth shut, don’t blab it around.’

“ ‘Spill it,’ I said.

“He whipped out a card.

“‘[’'m Raymond Holt,’ he told me. ‘The real Raymond Holt. Some day when the Old Man dies, I get all the dough—all of it.’”

“The guy was trying to crab your stuff,” said Russ.

“No, he was serious. Got mad as the deuce when I laughed.”

“And I suppose you told him,” said Russ, “who you were—let him know that you were the real McCoy—the real Napoleon?”

“Na-a-ah, the guy was cracked. I figured to myself—what’s the use? I walked out.”

Russ and I shook him off and walked back towards the gang at the gate.

“The guy’s batty,” said Russ. “It gives me the willies, the way that fellow acts. He oughta be locked up.”

But I didn’t think so. He was all right in the shop if you didn’t try to argue with him—didn’t get him started talking about the H.P.’s or committing suicide.

Two police cars came down the street and pulled up at the gate. Eight or ten cops piled out and began herding the men down the street.

“Move along,” they ordered. “It’s against traffic rules to block up the street. Break it up. Move along, you fellows.”

The men grumbled and cussed. They didn’t move very fast, but the cops kept driving—kept crowding and swinging their clubs. The niggers, joking and laughing, moved off towards the main gate. The whites went in the opposite direction. Russ and I beat it across the street into the parking field and got out of sight among the cars.

“We’ll lay low,” he said; “see what happens. When the cops leave, the big fellow may open the gate. If he does—bust a leg getting over there. Don’t wait for nothing.” Russ was beginning to use the old head.

Two cops stayed near the gate. The other cops herded the whites along the sidewalk across the tracks away from the plant. Nobody bothered the niggers and when they reached the main gate they stopped and chewed the rag with the guard. Then they came back.

“What now?” said Russ. “They’re headed back. What do you make outa that?”

I didn’t know. The two cops were still hanging around and when the niggers reached the employment gate the cops didn’t chase them away. Instead, they got into the cars and drove off. And then, before we could figure out what was happening, the guard unlocked the gate and let in the niggers—didn’t even wait to have them line up.

“Come on!” said Russ, “he’s letting ’em in! Let’s get over there.”

We tore across the wide parking field, across the wide street, ignoring the traffic. But we were too late. When we got there the last nigger was in. The guard slammed the gate. Seven or eight other fellows who had been hiding behind a box-car had seen the niggers go in. They came running up.

Russ was so mad he lost control of himself. Didn’t know for a minute what he was doing. He flew at the gate.

“Come on, you big bruiser,” he yelled. “Let us in! Open up! We saw you let in those niggers. We want our jobs back! Give us a chance!”

“Get away from that!” the guard said. He had a short piece of heavy hose and clipped Russ across the bare knuckles.

Russ kicked at the gate.

“You nigger-loving bastard,” he said. “Let us in! We got our badges—we’re on the pay roll! We saw you let in those niggers!”

More fellows came running up. They bobbed up from all over—from out of old cars—from the parking field—from behind an old real estate office. They were eager to get into a scrap—eager to start taking something apart. But the steel gate was locked. It was strong. I grabbed Russ by the arm.

“Come on,” I said. “He can’t open up. What’s the matter with you? This gang would mob him!”

The guard stepped into his booth—grabbed the telephone. I knew what that meant—the cops would be back. The H.P.’s too. They were rough.

Everybody started calling insults—kicking and jerking at the steel fence and gate. It was a mess. You couldn’t hear yourself shout above the noise of the crowd.

The place wasn’t healthy no more, what with the crowd getting wild and the telephone call. Some of the guys were looking for rocks. I jerked Russ out of the crowd, gave him a kick on the shins. It wasn’t a minute too soon. Two cars of H.P.’s wheeled out of the main gate as we crossed the street. We cut through the parking field and went home through the side streets. Russ was pretty burned up.

“They don’t want white fellows in there,” he said. “They want niggers. I don’t blame them. The niggers can take it—we can’t. White fellows get their brains tied up into knots. They get screwy ideas like Gus and that crazy Jerry. I’d go nutty myself if I had to work in there all the time. But the niggers—nothing ever happens to them.”

I didn’t say nothing. I was feeling lower than a snake’s belly myself.

## Chapter XXVI

It was still dark when Russ and I got out of bed to go to the house-wrecking. And it was snowing and blowing a gale and the wind came up through the cracks in the floor and howled around the corner of the old house.

Rita stayed in bed and Russ told her that we were going out to rustle up wood, but he didn’t tell her that we were going to help tear down a house to get it. We got us a couple pieces of rope, a hammer, and an old wrecking bar, and left.

A fast walk of ten or twelve blocks got us warmed up and then we got cold again trying to find the right house.

“Gotta find it,” said Russ. “Might be a little embarrassing to start on the wrong one—have the owner stick his head out of a window about the time we were walking away with the front porch.”

“Sure would,” I said. “It’d be worse than going on relief and having a half-ton of coal delivered to your front door free-gratis. Sometimes I can’t figure out why I let myself get dragged into this kind of thing.”

“Me either,” he said, trying to be sarcastic. “When you get home you’ll have to look up ‘house-wrecking’ in Mr. Holt’s book—see what he says about it. He’s against the dole, we know that already.”

We found the house, a little two-story affair—all wood. It was next to a narrow alley, littered with ash-cans and garbage. Through the swirling snow we could see a line of old cars, two-wheeled carts and old trailers parked in the alley, pretty well back from the street. But not a man did we see until Jeff stuck his head out of a window and motioned for us to come in the back door.

It was beginning to get light outside, but in the old house it was still pretty dark. For a minute or so we couldn’t see a thing but the lights from two or three cigarettes and a lot of feet all over the floor. Then we noticed there was a meeting in progress. A short, heavyset nigger stood on what looked like a piece of linoleum at the end of the room and was talking. A big gang of niggers and three or four whites sat along the walls on the floor. The room was full and Russ and I stood next to Jeff near the door.

“Done voted a’ready,” said Jeff in a whisper.

“Yeah,” said Russ. “What for?”

“Officers. Ah’s a General.”

“What’s that make us?”

“Y’all boys kin be Captains,” said Jeff. “In my squad. Ah got two othas a’ready.”

“Dog-gone,” said Russ. “We shoulda been here early. We might-a been Majors—or something. Who’s that fellow shootin’ off there? What’s he holding things up for?”

“Tha’s Mista Williams,” said Jeff. “Stoodie, we calls him. He the Soo-preem High Commander. What he say—oes.”

It had got a little lighter. We could see things. The men were sprawled all over the floor. They had crowbars, hammers, bunches of ropes and hay-wire. One had a shovel and one was eating a fish from a piece of newspaper spread out on the floor. Stoodie was going on as though he was still good for a long time. He was taking himself pretty serious—screwed up his face and tried to look mad, like Mussolini.

“Ge’men,” he said, “this yere occasion remind me of the time my ole pappy usta tek me in the woodshed. ‘Boy,’ he say to me, ‘this yere’s a mighty solemn occasion—mighty. Ah’s gwine remember it a long time—caze it gwine hurt me more’n you, son.’

“‘Yeh—Ah knows that,’ Ah say to him, ‘but it ain’t gwine hurt you in the same place.’

“And tha’s how it gwine be yere. What we gwine do yere is gwine hurt us more’n what it’s gwine hurt the mortgage company what owns this yere place and put the people what lived yere and couldn’t pay they rent out in the old street. But it ain’t gwine hurt us in the same place. We doing this not so much for to get bu’ning wood, though that gwine help, as to discourage those otha landlords and holding companies who’s thinking right now about puttin’ us out in the cold, too. They is gwine read about this yere house-wrecking in they papers, they is, and they is gwine scratch they haids and say—‘Um-um-umuh, tha’s bad. Tha’s mighty bad. Ah ’spects Ah betta leave those poo’ people right whe’e they is till they gets jobs and kin pay they rents. Not move ’em out.’ Yas, sah—tha’s what they gwine say to theysefs—if they’s sma’t.”

Russ was eager to get into action. He hadn’t been around colored fellows enough to know how to take them. It was their party, not ours.

“How long’s this been going on?” he asked Jeff in a loud whisper. ““What’s the idea? Why don’t we get started?”

“We get sta’ted ’rectly,” said Jeff. “Ain’t gwine be long now.”

The speaker went on and on.

“Ah recollect one time,” he said, “Ah got e-viction papahs served on me. Ah most had to move out the house where Ah was. A ge’men opened the door and stuck some papahs at me. Then he looked sca’ed and got ready to run.

“Wait a minute, yere, boy,’ Ah say. ‘What this y’all give me?’

“‘E-viction papahs,’ he say. “They’s fum che sheriff’s office.’

“Way ’cross the front of the papah they was big letters—black as a niggah’s funeral.

“Greetings From The State of Michigan,’ it say.

“What’s this, though?’ I asked him. ‘It say yere, Greetings fum the state of Michigan. Y’all sho, boy, this yere papah ain’t congratulations or somethin’ from the Gov’nor?’

“No sah, boss,’ he say. ‘That they greetings and stuff—that they jist make it legal. That they papah’s a notice to e-vict.’

“‘F-vict?’ I say.

“‘Yes-sah, boss. It done mean—get out.’

“What house it say to e-vict fum?’

“‘Ah dunno,’ he say. “This house Ah ’spect.’

“That all yo brung?’ I ask him.

“Yes sah.’

“‘Pshaw,’ Ah say. “Y’all done brung me wrong papah, boy. What y’all do that fo’? This papah tell me which house to move out of. Ah knows that a’ready. What Ah wants is a papah which tell me what house to move in-to. Tha’s what Ah want.’

“P’s sorry, boss,’ he say. ‘I’s mighty sorry.’

“Yall tek this papah,’ I tell ’em. “Tek it back to that they no-count sheriff and dun’t come back yere till y’all gets me the right one. What ah wants is in-viction papahs—not e-viction. That mek it clear?’

“Yes sah,’ he say. ‘Yes sah, boss. I’s pow’ful sorry I mek you dis trouble fo nothin’.’”

Russ was getting mighty impatient. He took a step forward. -

“What are we here for?” he said. “If we’re gonna tear down this old joint—let’s get going. We’re killing time. What’s the idea?”

The gang stirred. The men were surprised but said nothing. Stoodie gave Russ a dark look.

“"N case they’s anybody yere don’t know,” said Stoodie, “Ah’m runnin’ this meetin’. Ah’m the Soo-preem High Commander. Ain’t gwine be no foolishment. We’s gwine to do it ’cording to Misto Hoyle. In the fustest place—anybody that wants to is welcome to make hisself scarce and get out. And in the secondest place—anybody that has got a job and is wukking, is obliged to lay off the wood. We’s gwine sta’t on the roof and wuk down. The Generals will now please preceed to round up they Captains and re-move the roof shingles.”

Russ stepped out into the middle of the floor.

“Na-a-a-ah,” he said, “that’s not the way to go at it. The idea is to do as much as we can from the inside—stay out of sight. The first thing to do is to get rid of the plaster—drop it into the cellar. Then tear out the windows and walls—collapse the old joint and do all our work on the ground. Get it over with—quick.”

There was a buzz of voices. Men talked. They could see that Russ knew his stuff.

“Ge’men,” said Stoodie, “does y’all want me—or no? If -—”

“Sure we want you,” said Russ. “You’re the Supreme High Commander, ain’t you? I’m the dictator—that’s all.”

“You is the dictator?” said Stoodie.

“Sure.”

“And me—Ah’m the Soo-preem High Commander?”

“That’s right.”

Stoodie called for order.

“Ge’men,” he said, “all who is in favor of having a dictator say ‘I.’ ”

The “I’s” were general.

“All who is in dis-favor of having a dictator say ‘no.’ ”

There wasn’t a sound.

“Thank you, gents,” said Russ, stepping up beside Stoodie. “I’m much obliged to you now and forever. And now to get at the job. We’ll do it the same as barn-raising, except that everything will be done in reverse. I want five men to go in the cellar and tear out the underpinning from the rear wall. Rear wall only—make it fast. Stoodie here, as the Supreme High Commander, will pick out the men.”

Every man rose to his feet, eager to start. Stoodie selected a General and four Captains. They went below.

“Six fellows,” said Russ, “will tear up a couple boards from the floor along the walls. I want ten others to get ready to rip off the plaster and drop it down into the cellar. The others take out all doors and windows. Make it snappy.”

Stoodie repeated the orders and selected the men for the jobs. Things started—moved fast. The underpinnings were knocked out in a hurry and as soon as the men came back up the plaster was peeled off and dropped through the slits in the floor. The dust and the noise was something fierce. Doors and windows were loaded into old trailers and hauled off. Braces and fire-stops were pulled out. Then came the ceilings and floors—last of all the partitions. Inside the old house was stripped bare.

“Everybody out,” hollered Russ. “Every man get himself a long board.”

Stoodie repeated the order—stuck close to Russ. He had a stick in his hand—waved it about like a sword. He screwed his face up so that he looked more and more like Mussolini.

Some of the fellows grabbed long pieces of lumber that had been ripped up from the floor. Others pulled long strips of siding from the outside of the old house. Russ got one, too.

“Have ’em do what I do,” he told Stoodie. “Get every man out in front.”

They followed Russ. He walked to the front—raised the end of his board high against the face of the old house. The men did the same, though none of them knew just what was going to take place.

“Take it easy,” said Russ. “Push when I say—heave. Let off when I say—ho-o-0-0-0. Stay together—keep it up. Get her weaving—heave—ho-o-o. Get her rocking—heave—ho-o-o-o-o. A little harder—get ’er swaying now men. All together —”

At first there was no give. The old house was firm. It looked silly—thirty-forty men pushing and swaying at the face of the old building with a bunch of long boards and thin, limber sticks. One or two fellows quit.

“Keep working,” said Russ. “Get her moving—heave—ho-o-o-o. Stay together.”

The old house started giving a little—just a little, at first. Not much, but enough to encourage the fellows. They worked harder. Then the whole thing began swaying—rocking back and forth with the sticks. Russ changed the rhythm—a little faster. It moved more and more—swayed farther each time the men pushed. Inside, things began loosening up—snapping and popping. Boards cracked.

“Keep her going,” said Russ. “We got her coming. Give ’er all you got—men—rock her over—this time ——”

Wood splintered and snapped. The old building lurched back—the rear crumbled and sagged—the whole front reared up and the house sat down on its haunches with its face to the sky.

Stoodie thought it was funny. He forgot all about trying to look dark and serious like Mussolini and bent double with laughter.

“Oh, Misto Russ,” he said, “look at dat now. Ole house ’e ti’ed. He’s gwine sit down on ’e’s hind end and rest. ‘Ah ain’t gwine git up no mo’,’ ’e say to ’esef.”

Russ made eight or nine fellows climb up on the face of the old house and the whole thing flattened out and collapsed. After that it was a short job to rip the shingles off with a shovel and tear things apart. The carts and trailers were quickly loaded with old boards and rafters. Some of the men carried away bundles of lumber and lath on their shoulders. I laid out a pile of stuff for Russ and me to take home. It was surprising how quickly the job was cleaned up.

“A neat job,” said Russ, “if I do say it myself. The cellar hole is filled—everything is cleaned up. I like to leave a job in nice shape.”

Besides Russ and I there was only Stoodie and a couple of colored fellows who had come late. They were gathering up the last scraps of wood from the snow. Stoodie, who had dropped all his high-faluting ways, was trying to salvage a toilet bowl from underneath a big pile of old plaster. Russ helped. The two colored fellows were arguing.

“Tha con’stable sho gonna be surprised,” said one, “when he get yere and try to move these people usta be yere outa this house.”

“Which house?” said the other.

“This house. This house we jist wrecked.”

“How he gonna tell people which ain’t yere to move outa house which ain’t yere neither. Tell me that—you crazy fool.”

“Tha what I jist say. He gonna be su’prised.”

“Su’prised at what?”

“Su’prised at they ain’t gonna be no house to move the people out.”

“Which people?”

“People usta be in this yere house.”

“But they ain’t gonna be no people yere.”

“Usta be.”

“Usta be ain’t is. Besides—how he gonna move people which ain’t is outa house that ain’t is neither? Tell me that.”

“That what I say—he gonna be su’prised. He gonna say—‘How I gonna move these people —’”

“Which people?” “

“People whose name it say on that papah.”

“Wait a minute, now. Wait a minute. You’s gettin’ way head of yousef. Which papah y’all talkin’ ’bout?”

“Papah at say for the people to move outa they house in forty-eight hours.”

“Which house?” :

“This house.”

“But they ain’t gonna be no house yere when he come, you crazy fool.”

And then they started all over again, going round and round like a couple of dogs trying to catch each other’s tails.

We got the toilet bowl out of the dirt. The two niggers tied their scraps and stood looking at my pile of wood.

“Go ahead—take it,” said Russ.

“Wait a minute—” I said.

“Let ’em have it,” said Russ. “We’ll get more.”

One of them took the belt from his pants—put it around the pile of boards, and went off with the load on his shoulders.

Stoodie was trying to figure out a way to carry the toilet bowl. He raised it up to the side of his hip.

“Pow’ful heavy,” he said. “Can’t do it that-a way—might slip. Put it ovah my shoulder, I guess. Ovah my haid.”

Russ and I helped. We lifted it up high and let it down, bottom side up, over his head with the weight on his shoulders and against the front of his derby. It worked.

“All Ah needs now,” he said, “is a silver sword and a red plume on this heah thing like the Elks wear when they pee-rades down the street on the Foth of July.”

He stuck out his hand. Once more, he was the Supreme High Commander.

“Misto Russ,” he said, and his voice had a strange booming sound, “Ah’m mighty proud of the way y’all hepped me get this job done. Ah thanks yo from the bottomest depths of my heart.” And then he, too, marched down the alley and Russ and I were alone.

“Yes,” I said, “you sure did a nice job, Russ. The lot’s all cleaned up—no mess. The outfit that owned the house ought to give you a vote of thanks. But what did you get out of it for yourself? Tell me that.”

“Satisfaction,” he said. “Knowing the job was done right.”

“I suppose that’s gonna help us keep warm? Pay for the chances we took? If the cops do catch up—you’ll be the first to get smacked into jail. You engineered the job.”

“’T ain’t right,” he said, “to burn up another fellow’s house.”

“But o-keh to help tear it down?”

“It will get into the papers,” he said. “It will discourage other outfits from throwing families without jobs out into the streets.”

“Aw—nuts,” I said. “Your arguments are lousy. Sometimes they’re so lousy they stink.”

We started home. The wind had died down and the sky was dark and the snow was coming down hard. The dirty streets and the ramshackle houses were fast getting covered with a clean layer of white. We were near the city limits, but actually in the town called Hamtramck. A half block down the street, across the main artery boulevard, hung a huge sign that read —

YOU ARE NOW ENTERING  
DYNAMIC DETROIT.  

Halfway home Russ stopped in at the feed and fuel place and laid out fifty cents for another bag of soft coal.

## Chapter XXVII

A couple of days after the wrecking bee Russ and I and Rita were sitting in the house after supper. The dishes and stuff had been cleared from the table and the kid was asleep and Rita was unraveling an old sweater. There was not even a newspaper to read, and as we sat there around the old stove I guess we were all thinking about the same thing—that is, when Mr. Holt would open up and let Russ and me go back to work in his factory.

Russ took two one-dollar bills from his pocket and spread them out on the table slow and carefully and then made a systematic search for small change and dug up two dimes and two pennies. These he laid in a neat pile on top of the two paper dollars.

“Two dollars and twenty-two cents,” he said. “It’s our all time low.”

“A dollar and a quarter of that,” said Rita, “will have to go for another bottle of baby food. That leaves ninety-seven cents to live on till you get a job or we go on the dole. If you should happen to go back to that relief office tomorrow, you can tell them you’re dead broke.”

“Might snow tonight,” said Russ. “If it does, I’ll have a couple of sidewalks to clean off in the morning.”

“And if it doesn’t snow,” she said, “and there’re no sidewalks to clean, you’ll have to get some potatoes somewhere. Maybe you’d like to borrow some from Dad.”

“Farmers pray for rain,” said Russ, “when there’s a drought. Let’s us pray for snow?”

“That ain’t gonna do no good,” I said. “Mr. Holt’s the only one can help us out of this fix. I’ve only got six bucks left myself. When that’s gone I’ll be in the same fix.”

“Well,” said Russ, trying to be sarcastic, “let’s have a little prayer meeting right now—just the three of us. Sing a couple of songs, read a chapter from Mr. Holt’s book, get down on the floor on our knees and ask Mr. Holt to open up and let us go back to work. Might do some good.”

“Might, at that,” I said. “I read in a Sunday paper how ideas from one mind can pass through the air and go into the mind of another. It showed pictures of two brains and static jumping from one to the other, like lightning. Mental telegraphy, I think is what the guy called it.”

“Not mental telegraphy,” said Rita, who knew all the big words in the dictionary. “Mental telepathy is what that is.”

“Yeh,” I said, “that’s it. Mental telepathy. Who knows but what that’s how it works when a guy prays. Who knows but what Mr. Holt would get the drift if we got down on our knees and sort of told him how tough things were getting for us and that there was a kid and that we’d like awful much to get back to work? Who knows?”

“Dog-gone, Bennie,” he said, “I believe you got something there. It’s a kind of mental radio system—your mind and his. But still, if it works, why does he need all those H.P.’s to tell him what us fellows talk and think about in the plant?”

“Nah,” I said, “you don’t get the idea. This mental telepathy stuff ain’t some kind of stool pigeon. It only works on certain levels—between certain minds. It has to be spiritual stuff. Bum thoughts sort of bog themselves down—at least that’s the way the guy explained it.”

“Oh,” said Russ. “No reception, you might say.”

“Something like that. A fellow has to be in the right mood. And if a fellow’s in the right mood, he ain’t gonna have no bum thoughts. Bum thoughts, the fellow explained in the paper, are like static. What do you think?” I asked Rita.

“I dén’t know anything about the principle of the thing,” she said, “but I think it’s one of those things that works if you think it works—at least for yourself. It’s like praying. If you don’t think it’s going to do any good—it’s a cinch that it won’t. But if a person is sure it’s going to work it puts him in the frame of mind where he’s liable to get good results just by being in the right frame of mind.”

“Well, Bennie, here,” said Russ, “is in the right frame of mind to send a little prayer to Mr. Holt. I’m not—my belly’s too empty. Go ahead, Bennie—tell him we haven’t had a square meal for a month. Tell him we want our jobs back—that we wanna go to work—now. And then, just by way of a postscript, you might add that if we don’t get a job pretty quick we’ll come down there and get that five bucks he’s holding back on our badges. You listen to his broadcasts—no reason why he shouldn’t listen to yours.”

“There you go,” said Rita. “It’s not what you’re saying that matters—only that it shows how you feel about things—about the man who can give you a job when no one else can. You’re all wet.”

“Me?” he said. “How do you figure?”

“When you were in the drop forge you were always getting into arguments and fights—laying off when you felt like it—trying to act smart. You’re the same now.”

“What of it?”

“Nothing of it. Except that we don’t have anything to eat—not enough coal or wood to keep warm, and the baby’s got to be fed on stuff from the drug store because I’ve no milk. That’s all.”

“What’s that got to do with what you just said? About being smart?”

“You lost your job in the drop forge. You got put on production—where the lay-offs hit hardest.”

“They threw out the machine I was on.”

“You were the only two that got transferred—you and Bennie. Bennie got it in the neck because he was your friend. The others stuck. If you hadn’t got transferred you’d be working now, getting out dies for the new models—earning money. I know. I’ve been listening to factory talk all my life.”

“She’s right, Russ,” I said. “Johnson hated our guts.”

“And I hated his.”

“That’s it,” said Rita, getting up from her chair. “You hated Johnson—you hate the factory—you hate your own job. If it wasn’t for Bennie here giving us board money, we’d starve. We’re three months behind with the rent. There’s nothing to eat—nothing for me and the baby. And you sit there and make wisecracks about the man whose name is on the pay checks.”

“Cripes,” he said. “I wanted to pull out of this mess when we could—when we had the dough. You wouldn’t.”

“I’m going to live in Detroit.”

“That’s what you’re doing.”

“With a fellow that’s no good for a husband.”

“Me?” He got up. They stood facing each other —across the stove. Her eyes snapped. His face was white as a sheet.

“You! Yes—you, Russ,” she said. “A fellow that can’t keep a good job when he’s got one—that tries to be smart when he’s broke—tries to be funny when there’s nothing to be funny about.”

“If I was serious,” he said, “I’d go nuts.”

“And if you don’t get serious—I’ll go nuts. We’re in a fix, Russ.”

“Don’t I know it? I don’t like it any better than you do.”

“Before I married you I had money—things to eat—things to wear.”

“So did I. And I didn’t have to listen to some woman tell me I was no good—I don’t now. If you can’t take it—pack up your stuff. Leave the kid and get out.”

“Listen,” I said, “what’s the matter with you two? What’s the —”

“I’ll get out,” she snapped. “I’ll be a taxi-dancer again—earn my own living. But if you think I’ll leave the baby, you got another think coming. I’ll get out tomorrow.”

“Get out now, if you want to. But I’m telling you—the kid stays.”

“You’re crazy.”

“The kid stays—Rita.”

“He comes with me—Russ. I’m the mother.”

“I’m the father.”

“He’s mine, Russ. I went through agony to get him. I’ll take him or else —”

“Hey—hey,” I said, “cut it out. What’s the idea—going on like this? Nobody’s going —”

“Stay out of it—Bennie,” she said. “We’re going to settle this—now. It’s been hanging fire a long time. It’s between me and him.”

Someone knocked at the door.

“Wait a minute,” I said. “Be quiet till I see who it is.”

It was a messenger boy from the telegraph office. He had two telegrams—one was for me and one was for Russ.
