“You have to sign,” I said to Russ. “One’s for you.”

They were from the Holt Motor Company—both alike.

“Report back to work at once,” they read.

Russ looked at his a couple of minutes and then let it drift to the table. I picked it up, gave it to Rita. She read it.

“Now let’s see,” I said, “what were you two arguing about?” .

They both stood there looking like a couple of spanked kids. Neither one said a word. I got my coat.

“We’re going to celebrate,” I said. “I’m gonna get a steak and some potatoes. Not a rib steak—a Porterhouse as big as a platter. And the potatoes are gonna be Florida prime—not number two U. S. spuds like they hand out to the reliefers. I’m giving you two thirty minutes to take back what you said to each other and make up. Hard times is over.” And then I left.

And when I got back all was peaceful and sweet in the house. Russ had on a clean shirt and was shaved; she had powdered her nose and had on a clean apron. Everything was all hunky-dorry. I made some talk, just to be saying something.

“Butcher asked me,” I said, “if I wanted a bone for the dog.”

“ ‘Nah,’ I said, ‘we got rid of the mutt. The way we had to shove bones into him was a crime. He ate as much as a cow. He wasn’t satisfied with just any old bone. There had to be meat on ’em or he’d sit up all night and howl.’”

I fried the steak. Russ cooked the potatoes and Rita put the stuff on the table, and to make it look like a party, she lighted a stub of a candle. Everything was just swell and we ate until we all liked to bust. And then Russ had to go and spoil it all by making a speech.

“Bennie,” he said, “you did noble. I’m ashamed of myself for acting like I did there a little while back—getting mean—taking it out on Rita, just because things were tough and I couldn’t see any way out.”

“That goes for me, too,” said Rita, going over and sitting on his lap. “I told Russ I was sorry—I lost my head. If it hadn’t been for you —”

“Aw-—forget it,” I said. “It’s all over now. If anybody gets any credit, it’s Mr. Holt—for calling us back. You can’t tell me it was an accident—those telegrams coming just when they did.”

“Maybe,” said Russ. “But dog-gone you, Bennie, I’ll bet you used a little of that mental telepathy on Mr. Holt. I’ll bet you broadcast a little message to him—let him know we were at the end of our rope.”

“Maybe,” I said. “I think I’ll just let you guess.”

Rita was fiddling around with his shirt collar—running her fingers through his hair.

“Well,” I said, getting up, “I guess I’ll turn in. Gotta get up early tomorrow, and besides, I got to write a letter yet to a man about a dog.”

## Chapter XXVIII

It was sure good, after the long lay-off, to get back in the factory and be working again. It was good to hear again the slam of steel against steel; the whir of the high-speed machines; the purring of the motors pulling the conveyors. It was good to see again the faces of the old men who worked on the line; the sweepers; the crib-men who handed out tools and always bellyached when you asked them to do something. It was good, almost, to smell again the stink of the coal-tar disinfectant used in the toilets; it was good to read again the old signs that said “No Running,” “No Smoking,” “No Spitting,” “No Sitting Down While Working.”

Russ and me got our jobs back on the line, and Norm and Gus and Jeff and Jerry and most of the other old fellows were there, and they were all feeling good and raring to go, and every once in a while the whole gang, to let off steam, would throw their heads back and let out a long howl like a bunch of soul-tortured coyotes.

The first day a lot of the buggies were empties and the line was slow and irregular because there were a lot of new men all through the departments, and even us old men were slow and out of practice. After a long lay-off it takes weeks to get the new fellows broke in and the old men up on their toes so that production is normal. There were not enough helpers to break in the new men and the line bosses stepped down off the buggies and worked shoulder to shoulder with us fellows.

The motors for the new models, as far as we could see, were exactly the same. Same kind of crankshaft; same gray iron pistons; same kind of heads on the cylinders. In fact, it was hard to see where they could be improved because they were just about perfect. But there were plenty of changes and improvements in the machines and in the factory. There were more air-conditioned cages up high off the floor with colored lights that went off and on that showed where the conveyors were dragging; more glass rooms where the temperatures were controlled. And the automatics in our department that worked on the cylinder blocks were more up to date. Streamlined, these were, and beautiful to look at. There were no cams and open gears to catch the men’s clothes; no belts or line-shafts to get out of whack and hold up the work. They were self-contained, each with its own group of motors, and all adjustments enclosed, so that the men were no longer able to change the cutting speeds or controls. They were beauties. Looking at them working there in tight groups or long rows, you had the feeling that there was something mysterious about them because they were so complicated and swift.

The broaching machines had a place for the rough motor blocks to go in; a place for the finished work to come out. What happened inside—you couldn’t see, but when the rough blocks came out they were broached.

The milling machines were like merry-go-rounds, set on edge to save space. The blocks took a ride, ten or twelve at a time—up on one side—down on the other. Cutting tools took off strips of hot metal. Rough and finish work in the same operation. When the blocks came to the end of the ride, they were milled.

Gus was back on the same boring machine. The ventilator fan was still up near the ceiling over Russ’s head, and Russian Bill was still heaving the motors off the end of the line with a steel lever. These things were the same. But the power tools on the line—the wrenches and drills and the hammers, had more power and speed. All of them were driven by high cycle, high frequency motors. There were no more pneumatics to fuss and sputter and get out of whack and cut down the speed—electricity had taken over the job.

During the lunch period Amos and me went over to final assembly and had a look at the new models. When we got back, Russ was still sitting there on an empty box eating his lunch. Amos started telling about the new jobs.

“They got the battery under the hood,” he said. “Every job’s got two tail-lights.”

“Big stuff,” said Russ, peeling back a banana and sticking a long leg out into the aisle. “Kids will be selling extras tonight. They’ll be hollering ‘All about the new models! Two tail-lights!’ You see if they don’t.”

“They’re more streamlined,” I said. “There’s a little cabinet in the instrument board to put your gloves in.”

“What’s that amount to?” he said. “The new models are right here in the shop—the automatics. They’re something to look at—something to talk about. They’re faster this year. This year they’ll flood the country with new cars quicker—we’ll all get laid off sooner and there’ll be a longer depression.”

There were a couple of new fellows, all eyes and ears, sitting off to one side, eating meat sandwiches out of a big paper bag. Hill-billies, I figured. Russ looked them over and I guess he figured the same as I did.

“They don’t want us to work steady,” said Russ. “They want to speed up production—get out a year’s work in three or four months—so they can close up the shops and kick us out in the streets. This yearly model stuff—this talk about the auto shows—is a lot of baloney. It gives em an excuse to shut down—lay us off and move in a lot of new machines to take our place and speed up things still more. It hurts us but at the same time it hurts them, too, but they’re too dumb to see it. They got the public educated into waiting for the auto shows to buy cars; they got the dealers educated to artificial depreciation so they’ll take two hundred dollars off on a last year’s model that’s never been off the floor; they’ve got the workers educated to take it on the chin without squawking. They speed up production—work to beat the band for a few months—and shut down. We get laid off. The men in the steel mills, tire factories, cotton fields, saw-mills, railroads and coal mines get laid off, too. No pay checks, or if they’re still working two or three days a week, they’re too broke to buy cars. And then Mr. Holt and the others sit back and bellyache about hard times. They lay off more men—make it impossible for more men to buy cars.”

“Yeh, but look at the wages we’re getting,” I said, “compared with the coal miners and fellows that work in the cotton mills.”

“Yeh, look at ’em,” said Russ. “Figure out the time you’re laid off and look at ’em at the end of the year, like any fellow does that’s in business. At the end of the year any man that works on production is broke.”

“How they gonna run steady,” said Amos, “when there’s no sales.”

“Make sales,” said Russ. “Not by high-pressure selling and by telling dealers to burn up old cars, but by getting things running steady. Cut out the yearly model stuff and give us workers so much a year. They could do it, if they made up their minds to it. But they’re scared. They’re scared they won’t get all the profits themselves, and so they move in more automatics to put more men out of work. Pretty soon they’ll have us all out—even the sweepers. Pretty soon all the factories will be perfect—all the machines automatic. And there’ll be no one left to buy cars, and some wise guy will come along and say, ‘Business is rotten. Let’s burn up the new cars, too. Let’s have a burn-up-a-new-car week. Put ads in the papers—everybody burn up a new car.’ There’ll be radio speeches. There’ll be pictures of Mr. Holt standing beside the mayor with a lighted torch in his hand ready to set the big pile a-blazing.”

Fellows gathered around to hear what he said. Niggers—hill-billies—men new in the shop. I got his ear.

“Man alive, Russ,” I said, “you’re in the shop now, pipe down.” He paid no attention.

“You’ll see what’ll happen,” he said. “From now on we’ll be getting into the swing. We’ll go a little faster—production will speed up a little more each day. New men will be crowded and transferred; old men will be pushed; little kinks ironed out. In about a month we’ll be getting out five days production in one. It’ll go along like that for a while till high-pressure selling breaks down and the dealers’ places are filled. Then we’ll get cut from five days to four. But still it’ll be ‘hurry-up, hurry-up’ till you’d think every man in the country was crazy anxious to buy a new car. And then we’ll get cut from four days to three. And that’s the time to pull out—not wait for things to get better, because they won’t. That’s the beginning of another new-model depression. I was pretty green when I first came here to work, I’d heard a lot of big talk about Mr. Holt—how swell he treated his men. Now I know more. The other time I wanted to work for a roll. This time—all I want is enough to pay what I owe and get out.”

Nice talk, I thought. Here we were back in the shop, earning good money. Everybody was happy to be back on the job—and Russ crabbing. The first day not hardly started yet—and already he was shooting off his big mouth, telling how he hated the shop.

The two-minute bell rang and the fellows started putting lunch boxes away and throwing papers into waste cans. Some of them hurried up to the crib and got tools, or paper cuspidors which cost a penny. Straw bosses hurried about giving orders. Russ got up from his box.

“It’s all right,” I said, “to shoot off your mouth when you’re outside, but for the love of Mike—use a little judgment. There’s guys here are liable to think you’re trying to stir up a big bunch of trouble.”

“Aw—nuts,” he said, “I’ll say what I want to.”

And then the bell rang for starting and the line began moving and there was a rising burst of sound like a storm, as the electric motors set all the machines and conveyors in motion.

## Chapter XXIX

That first day ended up with Russ getting me into another mess. Coming out of the factory we noticed a bunch of fellows on the opposite side of the street hanging around a new eating joint. We crossed over, thinking we might get a free hand-out, and to see what the joint was like. It was a new kind of dump, run by a couple of Greeks, and it was called, “The Merry-Go-Round.”

Through the big window we could see how it worked. Inside, there was a big circular oval around the outside of which the fellows sat. In front of the men there was an endless procession of trays that moved slowly around under glass covers. The fellows sitting around on the stools lifted the covers and took little dishes of stuff from the trays as they passed.

It was the grand opening. The front of the building was decorated with flags and a couple of yodeling cowboys were entertaining the crowd. A fat Greek went about handing out menus—“bill-o-ferries” he called them. He tried to get the fellows to go in.

“A’right, boys,” he kept saying, “you get cop-koffie for nuttin’. Stap einsy, now. Today you get cop-koffie for nuttin’.”

We took a menu. Meals were forty cents. Through the window we could figure out the whole system. You put down your money and got a plate with meat on it. Then you found a place in the circle and picked what you wanted off the trays as they came past your nose. Russ studied it carefully.

“Something wrong here, Bennie,” he said. “What it is—I don’t know. But something tells me these Greeks are using bad judgment.”

“Looks o-keh to me. Let’s go in—try their coffee.”

“Idea may be all right,” he said, “at a church sociable. But not here. They got the right idea in the wrong place. What’s to hinder that gang in there cleaning out the joint? What holds ’em back?”

“Decent manners,” I said. “Every time a man grabs something off that conveyor it leaves him with another empty dish. They got it fixed up so a man can’t make a pig of himself without advertising it. After he gets a couple of dozen empty dishes stacked up at his elbow he gets ashamed of himself and quits.”

“Maybe—” he said, “maybe. Maybe you know this gang better than I do. And then again—maybe you don’t. A man will do things in this dynamic Detroit that he wouldn’t do in a good Methodist neighborhood. The dynamite sorta gets under his skin.”

We went in to try out the coffee. The coffee was free, all right, but there was a catch. You had to put down the dough for a regular meal to get it.

It made Russ feel mean—I could see that—and I was for getting out. “Nothing doing,” he said, “this is too good a chance to pass up.”

After a man’s been on a starvation diet for a couple of months it takes him a long time to catch up on his eating. In those first days we could have eaten a dozen square meals every twenty-four hours.

We paid our dough, got our plates with the meat, and were looking for places to sit when we saw Gus and Jerry. There were two empty stools next to them which we took, Russ next to Gus and me on the outside. Among the fellows opposite us sat Russian Bill, the fellow who heaved the motors off the end of the line.

We started in. Russ grabbed a baked apple, two tapioca puddings and toast. I took a potato, a dish of squash and two Jellos. That gave each of us four extra dishes. We looked around. Some of the fellows had empty dishes stacked up like poker chips. They weren’t embarrassed. The only ones who were embarrassed of themselves were fellows like Russ and me who had just come in—got a late start. Instead of holding back, it was a kind of game—a game to see who could get the most empty dishes. Gus and Jerry weren’t doing so bad. Jerry had empty dishes stacked up on both sides of his plate. Gus not so many.

The fellows were all feeling good. Russian Bill was loud-mouthed—trying to be funny.

“Speech!” Someone hollered.

Russian Bill got up to his feet.

“Ladies and Gents,” he hollered, “I’m macking now a spitch. It is to tell you somethin’. It is to say—when Grik meets Grik dey stat a restaurant.”

He sat down to loud cheers.

“Yah,” someone hollered, “and hand out free coffee.”

Russ was beginning to act funny. He piled Gus’s empty dishes back on the conveyor.

“Listen, Gus,” he said, “I’m sending these back to the kitchen. You didn’t give Bennie and me a fair start—you got too many.”

A short Greek in a black coat came up. ——

“Waiter,” said Russ.

“We got no waites,” said the Greek. “I’m de manaje. You kut it out—what you doing. I call poleets-manos—you get fonny —”

“I wouldn’t,” said Russ. “There’ll be a riot in here if you do. You got a chance yet—to be a good sport.”

The Greek hurried up front to the telephone. Someone from the other side of the room threw a bun that just missed his ear. Fellows were getting louder—calling across to each other.

“Speech! Speech!” they hollered at Russ. He got up.

“Gents,” he said, waving a bunch of green onions in one hand, “I see fellows here that’re not doing their stuff—that’re trying to hold up the line. Not getting out production like they oughta. Gus, here’s, only got ten empty plates. That’s not enough—that’s laying down on the job. Vas’s de mather, Gus,” he said, laying a hand on his shoulder, “can’t you step on it en luddle? Can’t you kip opp? You vant I shood gif you a tranz-fere—dun’t you? You vant I shood poot you on Blue Mass?”

The fellows were getting wild. Laughed at the way Russ imitated Gus’s Dutch accent. The Greek hurried back and tried to get Russ to leave.

“You nice fellas,” he said, taking Russ by the arm. “You go now-—I give you ci .

“Nothing doing,” said Russ. “You called the cops. Let them put me out.”

A slice of toast sailed over our heads. The Greek gave Russ a jerk. He was showing bad judgment.

“Common now—” he said. “I gettin’ med—now. You sonababits, I ——”

Russ slapped him across the face with a handful of onions. The Greek beat it.

“As I was saying,” said Russ to the crowd —

I yanked his coat.

“Love of Mike, Russ,” I said, “let’s get the hell outa here. You’re starting a riot.”

“Sit down—you,” a guy hollered. “The big guy is talking.”

“As I was saying,” said Russ, “we’re not getting the jobs off the line. Take this model, for instance—” and he pointed to a dish of green Jello drifting by, “it’s got no wheels on it.”

He picked up a cookie and stuck it into the side of the Jello.

“En no head-lights,” said Gus, who stuck in a couple of olives.

“And no bumpers,” said Jerry, who added a piece of celery.

The idea caught like a house on fire and as the green Jello passed down the line, each man added something. A doughnut made a good looking spare tire, a piece of cake made a neat luggage trunk, a tooth pick was a radio antenna. By the time it reached the end of the line in the kitchen it was completely assembled. The fellows were clever. They built up sedans from baked potatoes; they used rolls to build coupes; dill pickles for long, sporty roadsters, whipped cream and catsup did nicely to touch up the trim.

The Greek was not long figuring something was hay-wire. He mobilized the help from the kitchen and came howling—headed for Russ. But he never made it. Some guy let out a yell—“Men do your stuff!”

The Greek and his crew were stopped by such a barrage of cream puffs, boiled potatoes, doughnuts, custards, dill pickles, and jelly rolls as you never saw in your life. They ducked through doors out of sight.

But the men didn’t stop. They had got into the spirit of the thing, were having fun. They began heaving stuff at each other—one side battling the other. One guy, not knowing which side he was on, pitched cottage cheese salads into a revolving fan.

I began kicking Russ on the shins—trying to get his mind off the rumpus.

“The cops, Russ!” I said. “They’ll be here in a minute—run us all in.”

I knocked a dish of pickled beets out of his hand to the floor. Gave him a push through a door. We were in the kitchen, but even then he was for going back for more. We heard the siren.

“The riot squad!” I said. “Now or never.” That got him started.

We ducked out of a back door and ran down the alleys. Then we stopped for breath behind an old shed to clean the mess off our clothes. Russ could hardly stand up for laughing.

“Judast, Bennie,” he said between breaths, “you got egg in your hair—mashed potato all down your neck. Where’s your hat?”

“You’re a swell guy,” I said, trying to dig a piece of hard-boiled egg out of my ear. “The very first day you try to act smart. Hadn’t been for me you’d be spending a few days in the cooler.”

“I wouldn’t have missed it,” he said. “Not for nothing. I got a sideache—from laughing. I’m weak.”

He had his coat off. He was digging the remains of a baked potato out of a pocket.

“That guy,” he said, “throwing stuff into that ’lectric fan—what a mess!”

“What’s so funny?” I said, getting more and more peeved.

“What’s so funny?” he said. “Everything. I got Russian Bill with a baked banana. He got you square in the neck with a big handful of mashed potato. That baked banana—it smeared all over his face—went smack. Didn’t you see?”

“No.”

“And the Greek—when he turned his head and we all let him have it—dog-gone, Bennie, I liked to died laughing. It was rich.”

He went off into a spasm again. My coat was a mess. I’d lost my hat. I couldn’t see anything funny to laugh at.

We began walking again, but hadn’t gone far when he stopped.

“Wait a minute,” he said. “You still got egg in your hair. I’ve heard about guys having egg on their vest, but you got it all over your hair.”

And that started him laughing again.

“Bennie,” he said, “you’re a mess.”

Once more we started. Once more he stopped me again.

“What’s this,” he said, “on your coat? Cottage cheese! I wonder who hit you with that? Not Russian Bill. Mashed potato—he had. Dog-gone—Bennie, why didn’t you duck? He got you square in the neck.”

Then he doubled up with laughter again.

I was mad.

“Blame it all, Russ,” I said, “I’m getting sick and tired of the crazy-fool way you act. I don’t see nothing to laugh at. I don’t think it’s funny to wreck a guy’s joint—getting hit with that stuff—losing my hat. If I’d-a known what you was figuring on doing, I wouldn’t-a went in. I’'d-a let the cops got you. I’m fed up on that kinda stuff.”

He was laughing so hard he was crying. I went across the street and walked home by myself.

## Chapter XXX

After a couple of weeks the engineers got things running smooth. A few machines were pulled out; a half dozen fellows were transferred, and some laid down their tools and walked out. And then the trouble-makers began to show up.

Gus, who ran a boring machine, got behind again and couldn’t keep up with the niggers, and Jerry, who held his job on the line very well, began getting chummy with Gus. There was nothing on them—Jerry was too smart for that, but they were both queer; and the way they hung around together, getting off by themselves during lunch, made it look bad.

Russ got along fine for a while until production went up to seventy-five hundred motors a day, but when everything was going smooth and it jumped to eight thousand one day and the bosses were riding the buggies, trying to make a new record, he got into trouble. It was the belt again. He was back in the same old rut—taking it easy, day dreaming, not snapping into it like the others, but letting the belt set his pace for him—three hundred and twenty an hour. Three or four times, before lunch, I jumped in and helped him get up. The last time I lit into him.

“You wanna get fired,” I said, “why don’t you just go up front and ask for your time? Why drag it out? Why get a reputation for not being able to hold a job on the line?”

“Aw, Bennie,” he said, “now you’ve got me balled up. I was trying to figure out a new drag —”

“Clamming!” I said. “Day dreaming! You got a wife and kid, Russ.”

“I know it.”

“Then why don’t you step on it—snap into it?”

“Do we have to go all over that again?” he said, good-natured and easy. “I thought I told you once, Bennie, how it was. If I had to think about this measly, no-account job—I’d go nuts. Suppose you tell the big boss to cut down a notch on the rheostat under his desk? Go up and have a talk with him.”

I was disgusted. I got him up in his station and then stood off near a fountain. The whole place was a-throb with the high beat of the machines. There was no joking—no talking—every man was bent down to his job. Except Russ. Smoke hung like a fog over the hot automatics. A cylinder boring machine had dull cutters and the whining cry of stellite against hot metal was like screeching brakes above the roar of night traffic. I watched Russ. He was outside of it all—cut off from the speed and sound of it all by the noise of the belt—the one-two-three-four of the slow beats of the thing over his head. Taking it easy—loafing, while good-natured Jeff, a nigger, had a fist full of nuts and was trying to help Russ keep up.

And then I saw Norm, standing near a pile of empty stock boxes, talking to one of the line bosses. Norm was watching Russ, too, and saw me and signalled for me to come over, and then the line boss left.

“I been watching your boy friend,” he said. “All day he’s been dragging over—bucking the line and stuff.”

“I know it,” I said, “I just been over there—talking to him.”

“Didn’t do much good, did it?”

“Well, no —”

“Listen,” he said, “I’m gonna ask you somethin’. If you was in my place, trying to get out production, what would you do about him? You wanna be a straw boss some day—let’s see you answer that question.”

He had a sarcastic grin on his face that I didn’t like.

“Gosh, Norm,” I said, “I don’t know. Russ is a good man—smart mechanic, and all that. Maybe we ain’t got the right job for him.” He laughed.

“We got the right job, all right,” he said. “Don’t worry about that. We got the right job. But we’ve got the wrong belt.”

“You mean —

“You know what I mean,” he snarled. “Or else, you’re dumber than I figured you are. Why don’t you tell me stuff instead of holding back on me? Instead of letting a dumb line boss show you up?”

“You know me, Norm,” I said. “I’ve always worked with you, ain’t I? Always told you things—even when it got the fellows sore at me.”

“Na-a-a-ah—you ain’t told me things,” he said. “What’re you tryin’ to give me? Tryin’ to tell me we ain’t got the right job for that fellow just because he’s got himself timed up with that belt. Get back on the job now. And tell your boy friend I’ll have a belt for ’im tomorrow that’s got a little more snap to it—that’ll help him keep up with the others.”

I didn’t go near Russ all the rest of that shift. Didn’t look at him even to see whether he was dragging over or not. And at night, when we went home, I didn’t say nothing. What’s the use, I figured. I didn’t want Russ to think I’d had a hand in it. Didn’t even want him to know what Norm had talked to me about it.

The next morning, when we came to work at the factory, there was another belt on the ventilator. Or maybe, it was the same old belt with more splices. The rhythm was changed—it was faster. Instead of four beats, there were at least a half dozen each time the belt went around over the pulleys. Instead of four definite beats, they were all strung together—too fast to follow. Norm and the machine flunkeys had done a good job, I thought. I had to admit that Norm’s idea was better than mine about getting Russ a transfer. It costs money to break a man in on a new job. I figured Russ would get straightened out now and be a regular.

Our shift went on and Russ took his place on the line. He noticed the change and looked up at the ventilator and his lips moved. If Norm saw the look on his face he must have had a good laugh.

The line was fast again, right from the start. I knew that it would be. Things were running smooth; all the little troubles that had been holding us back had been straightened out, and each day from now on the big bosses would be trying for a new record.

Figures would rise like the numbers on the chart of a boiler getting up steam. Eight thousand—eight thousand four hundred—eight thousand five hundred—eight thousand six hundred, maybe. Theoretically, production capacity of the line was nine thousand motors in twenty-four hours, but that figure had, as yet, never been reached. Maybe this time. For Mr. Holt and the engineers and the bosses it would be a big thing. Even us helpers would share a small part of the credit.

Russ got along swell. For once he was up on his toes, doing his share of the work, giving the job his attention. He had his feet wide apart, jaws set, and was facing the stream of motors coming down the line towards his station. He was the old Russ—Horatio on the bridge—doing his stuff—licking the tar out of a job that was tough.

I was beginning to think everything was going to work out nice, when along just before noon, he signalled me for relief. I went over and even before I stepped into his place, I could see he was mad enough to bite nails.

“Who squealed on me?” he said.

“You mean about changing the belt?”

“You know what I mean.”

“Gosh, Russ—not me.”

“Who then?”

“How should I know? Some line boss, I expect.”

“Any idea which one?”

“Why, no, Russ. There’s a dozen of ’em riding by here all day. They get paid, you know, for telling straw bosses things. Besides—how do you know it’s a scheme? Belts don’t last forever. Splices wear out—you know that.”

“Aw—nuts,” he said. “Don’t talk like a kid. You know why that belt’s been changed. And so do I. And I’d like to know the guy whose idea it was. I’d take one sweet crack at him—and get my time.”

And then he left and he was gone a lot longer than three minutes. And when he came back he stepped in and I worked alongside for a minute or so, even though I knew Norm would be eyeing us, because I wanted to talk to him and get him straightened out.

“You got a chance, now,” I said, “to get into this job right. Forget about that belt—let yourself go along with the hum of the shop and you won’t have no trouble. That one-two-three-four stuff pretty near got you fired. I don’t know who changed that belt, and I don’t care. But I’m glad of it because now you can straighten out and show Norm you can handle this job.”

He threw a handful of good nuts into the scrap, and they stung against the metal side of the barrel like shot.

“Get outa here,” he said. “You wear me down. Get outa here—before I step on you.”

## Chapter XXXI

Russ had no more trouble keeping up on the job but after the belt was changed he started getting mean and bad-tempered. Coming home from the factory at night he’d play with the kid for a while, but after supper when the kid had been put to bed, he would be gloomy and nervous and aching to get into an argument, or else he would tear off to some beer joint and drink more beer and spend more money than he should have. Sometimes he dragged me along but nearly always I came home first and alone. Later, when he’d come into the house, like as not, Rita would be awake and would want to know how much money he’d spent and there’d be an argument and in the morning, when I’d get up, I’d find him asleep on the old couch. It got so he had to spend all his spare time doing something foolish and wild. There was no sitting quiet in the house any more after supper reading the paper, or telling Rita about things that happened in the factory.

Before the landlord was paid up he spent a whole pay check for an old car that wouldn’t run, and when the guy towed it up we pushed it into the old shed in the back of the house and in three days he had it all apart. Rita wanted a coupe and she said:

“Why a touring car, Russ?”

“Haul more gear in it,” he said.

“What gear?”

“The outboard and stuff.”

A couple of days later he started hammering a trailer hitch out of some rough pieces of iron and she knew he still had his mind set on clamming. Sometimes I tried to figure out how it would end.

He was full of unrest—hated his job more than ever, and got into the habit of taking time off for job-hunting whenever he felt like it, or heard a rumor that some other factory was hiring. Always they asked him where he’d worked last, and always, if he told them the truth, a telephone call to the Holt Motor Company put an end to his prospects. It was foolish because the Holt Motor Company has a reputation for paying good wages and giving steadier work than the others. It made Rita mad. The time wasted running around cut big holes in the pay checks.

And then, one day, when the February snows had all melted away and there was a balmy breeze off the lakes, he laid off a whole day for no reason whatever. Late in the afternoon we got home the same time. He came up to the house with river mud on his shoes and carried a bundle of green stuff that looked like spinach. Water cress—he called it. Rita knew he hadn’t been at the factory and after sitting around awhile he said to her:

“How about supper?”

“I’m laying off today,” she said. “I’m not working.”

“That’s too bad,” he said. “Bennie and I will have to eat out.”

We left the house and got supper in a restaurant and afterwards we walked until we found ourselves on a high bridge over the ship canal in the rear of the Holt plant. The night air was soft and damp, and there was a hint of rain in the air; and the whole vast plant with its hundreds of stacks and tremendously long buildings bathed in their own light lay there on the flat land against the black Michigan night. The whole sound was like the roar of a mighty river. Small blue lightning from the electric welding machines in the steel body plant flashed sharply; the sputtering glare from the Bessemers turned the sky a deep, rosy red. We could see plainly the towering shape of the blast furnace; the long darkish lines of the rolling mill; the hundreds of lighted windows in the final assembly plant. Nearest to us was the foundry and the glass plant and the rear axle building, and farthest away the coke ovens and the giant power house with its cluster of high silver stacks carrying the words—Holt Motor Company—in glowing letters fifty feet high. Piles of iron ore and coal stood out against the heavy darkness like mountains, and rolling billows of steam from the quenching tower drifted over them like masses of clouds.

The bigness of it all, seeing it all there, made me feel like a poet. I thought of a line from Mr. Holt’s book.

“‘With machinery,’ I said, ‘for its Messiah-America is safe.’”

“Sometimes,” said Russ, “you make me very sad, Bennie. To me this is all very depressing. You think of the new automobiles being made in there tonight; I think of the thousands of men that will be pouring out of that place pretty soon with strained looks and dead faces—looking as though they had been released from a torture chamber. They’ll go home with the sound and fury of the machines hot in their blood. I can be calm about it tonight; I got some of the strain out of my system today—watching a hell-diver. But other nights—I know what it is. I know what it is to lay awake nights with the noise in my head—machines pounding—not being able to get away from the thing. I see motors—hundreds of them—pouring out of the dark. The other night I had a dream. I was all alone on the line. I had my axe and the motors were pouring—coming at me, and I was smashing them up with my axe. Some night I’m liable to have a nightmare and wreck all the furniture.”

“But work, Russ,” I said, “work is basic. What would a man do if he didn’t work?”

“Ah, Bennie,” he said, “right from the book again, ain’t it. You talk about work, but you don’t know what it is. You think you do, but you don’t, because what goes on there—on the assembly lines—at the automatics—is all that you ever learned. You’re a part of it—you’ve been brought up on it—never been away from it—don’t know any better. But it’s not work, Bennie.”

“Russ,” I said, “sometimes I think you’re plain lazy. Of course it’s work. What else is it? Work is something you don’t like to do—else you wouldn’t get paid for it. I don’t like it, myself, but you—you hate it. You’d rather hang around in a swamp, wasting your time—watching a hell-diver—getting your wife mad at you.”

“I don’t hate work, Bennie,” he said, leaning his arms on the rail and talking calm. “I like work—real work. Real work is good for a fellow—it brings out the best there is in you. I’ve done real work in the woods. I was a faller. The boss would say—‘Now this tree, Russ—I want down. Don’t wreck the small stuff around it. Don’t shatter the trunk—it’s got two-three hundred dollars worth of clear bridge timber in it. Lay it so’s the loggers can get it out of the brush.’ He didn’t have to put out a straw boss ro watch me. I didn’t have to keep up with a motor. I gave him the best I had in me. And at night I could let down. Read, or sit around the old stove and relax. Didn’t have to keep going. Didn’t have to tear around—go to beer joints to get the crazy noise out of my system.

“Another time I ran a steam threshing engine. A big brute it was. After working all day the boss would come up to me and say—“Tomorrow we’re threshing for Bill Jones, three sections north. Move the rig over tonight so we can get started at the regular ume in the morning.’

“It sounds simple, Bennie—but it wasn’t. It was work—a man’s work and I liked it. There was nothing in it for me—no overtime—no extra pay.

“Behind the engine there’d be a string of stuff a block long. Water tank, separator, coal tender, cook shack and tool wagon. I’d get strung out and start down the road, headlight showing the way. There’d be bridges to cross. Not many bridges in that country are built to hold up under thirty-forty ton loads. I’d have to stop, grab a lantern and examine the stringers—use my own judgment. That’s hard to do when you’re anxious to get there and the timbers are not rotted all the way through and its a long way through rough stubble fields if you go around. Times like that it’s easier to take a chance than not. But if you’re a good man, you don’t. You let down the fence and pull off the road through the fields, hoping you don’t drop the engine into a coulee level full of brown tumble weeds. You bog down in soft dirt; uncouple; string out a long piece of cable and get the engine up on hard ground. Plenty of things to worry about. There’s the danger of setting the stubble on fire; a leaky boiler flue getting worse; a balky injector that’s probably got a duck feather in it; alkali water pulls over and shoots out of the stack. The water glass reads ‘full’; but when you look again you see it’s a bubble and you don’t know whether your crownsheet over the top of the fire is wet or clean bare and ready to pop. All night it’s a struggle; all night it’s a fight against odds. But in the morning, when the blue light is breaking, you pull up to the stacks and you’re through. That’s work, Bennie. The kind that leaves a fellow’s manhood intact. Doesn’t cramp his ego—doesn’t screw him down and leave him feeling weak and mean and little at the end of the day.”

“Why didn’t you stick to your steam engine?” I asked.

“Why didn’t I stick to the manipulator?” he said. “There are no more steam threshers. It’s another case of where the automatics have taken a job over. Combines do the work faster. Get a job on one of those and you have to keep up with a gas motor—sewing sacks. The days of steam threshers, like the days of manipulators, are past. When I look at that bunch on the line—bell bottom pants, slick hair, twisted brains, and weak faces—I think to myself, this is the age of automatics and small men.”

A lake freighter, leaving the plant, came slowly down the canal and boomed a signal for clearance. The bell rang and we left the bridge.

“You’re hard to figure—” I said, “You and your ideas about machines. You love them—you love them not.”

“I like machines,” he said, “that I run. I don’t like those that run me.”

That night he was calm and strong—the old Russ. On the way home he stopped in at a chop suey joint and bought some chow mein for Rita.

“She likes it,” he said. “I shouldn’t have gone off and left her.”

But when we got home she was in bed and refused to get up when Russ asked her.

## Chapter XXXII

Production stepped up, and Gus, who ran one of the boring machines, got into trouble. The niggers doing the same kind of work raised their figures to seventy, seventy-five—and then eighty—and got along fine, but Gus couldn’t raise his production above sixty-five an hour. That made it look bad.

“What’s the matter, Gus?” his straw boss would say. “Can’t you step on it a little? Can’t you keep up with the niggers? You want me to get you a transfer—send you to final assembly to work on blue mass production?”

Gus was always able to think of plenty of excuses. He tried, but he was too old, and the more the straw boss jumped on him, the oftener he had to take time off and go to the toilet.

At the end of the shift the niggers would punch out and start joking and kidding. They’d leave behind the strain of the job and their worries, but Gus would nurse all his troubles carefully and take them all home, and in the morning, when he’d come back to the factory, oxeyed and worried, he’d bring them all carefully back. He was little and bent. It seemed like his troubles were always pressing him down to the floor.

A bunch of us fellows were sitting around one day having lunch when Gus started to talk.

“I hed’n droom las’ night,” he said.

“Dream, you mean,” said Amos. Amos was always pestering Gus. Not to be mean, but just to make us think that he was smarter than he really was.

“Ya—das vat I mean—droom,” said Gus. “I hed’n droom dat I died en vent opp to heaven. En ven I got by de ghate—derr vas en long line en I vas on de tail end. En joost as I vas going to go in ——”

Amos interrupted again.

“A couple of H.P.’s stepped up and said ‘Where’s your badge?’”

“No—no—” said Gus. “I saw de Irisman.”

“That was the Holt dream you had,” said Amos. “That’s a warning. We know the rest—how it goes. All the toilets were on conveyors and a big Russian heaved you off when your three minutes were up. The angels had composition wings, like the stuff steering wheels are made of, and they played “Turkey In The Straw,’ and you danced with a paper cuspidor on your head. You didn’t go to heaven, Gus. You had the Holt dream—it’s a warning. You better put in for a transfer—get a job sweeping the floor.”

“I saw de Irisman,” said Gus, “en he —”

“Wait a minute,” said Amos. “I thought you killed him—flattened him out in that big machine?”

“Lay off him!” said Jerry, getting up to his feet. “Let him be! We all know you’re smart.”

“Nuts to you,” said Amos.

Gus went on with his story.

“En I saw de Irisman,” he said, “en he saw me at de same time.

“‘Dar’s a Dutsman in hier!’ he hollowed ‘Dar’s a Dutsman in hier—poot him oudt!’

“In vun handt he vas vaiving somet’ing—a chemise. De peoples came running opp—stood all around me.

“‘I didn’t kill heem,’ I said. ‘I didn’t know he vas in die oldt car. De boss say to me—“Pool die lever.” I pool. I didn’t know he vas in die oldt car—I didn’t know.’ But dey vouldn’t belief me.”

Nobody said a word for a second, and then it was Amos again.

“And then the H.P.’s came running up, eh Gus? And they hauled you away.”

“No—” said Gus. “No—I voke opp. But I tell you—so shurr-rr-re I sit hier—I did not do it.”

“Did—or didn’t not?” said Amos.

“I didn’t not—I tell you.”

“Hear that,” said Amos. “He says ‘I didn’t not do it.’ That means that he did. Stands there and tells us he did. Good gosh—fellows—that’s—that’s awful!”

Gus threw a dumb look at Jerry. Jerry jumped to his feet and faced Amos. There would have been a fight, I think, but just then one of the timekeeps came up. He had a slip of paper for Jerry. We gathered round him to read it. It was an order, signed by one of the H.P.’s in the front office, and it told him to report at the plant hospital at twelve the next day for an examination.

Jerry forgot about Amos. He was excited. He doubled the paper up and stuffed it into his pocket. Then pulled it out; unscrambled it, and read it again. His hand trembled.

“What do they want to examine me for?” he said. “I’m all right. There’s never been nothing the matter with me, fellows. What’s the idea? I’m all right.”

“Yeh,” said Amos, “you’re all right, Jerry. Nothing the matter with you—except you got a slight case of bats in the belfry. They’ll probably have a look at your head.”

The two-minute bell rang and the bunch scattered.

A lot of funny thoughts were mixed up in my head. All afternoon I was nervous.

## Chapter XXXIII

The next morning, when Russ and I came into the shop, we saw right quick that there was something hay-wire. Fellows were ganged up around the clock, there was running, and small bunches of men stood around talking and acting excited. Jeff told us the news.

“Jerry’s done kilt hissef,” he said, hurrying past. “Jumped into the blast furnace—dog-gone.”

That much seemed true. For the rest, it was hard to tell what was true and what wasn’t. Some said he’d ridden up the long incline in a skip car full of iron ore and coke. Others said he’d climbed the long ladder. One guy, who worked on the graveyard shift and said he’d got the dope straight from a foreman who had charge of tapping the furnace, told us Jerry had climbed up the highline. From there he’d made his way to the super-structure of the furnace where he’d hidden away among the steel girders and pipes all through the first part of the shift. At midnight, when the men were tapping the furnace and the whole sky was red, they’d heard a shout and had looked up. And they’d seen him there, on the very peak of the tower, with his arms stretched out and the red light on his shirt. For a short minute he’d balanced himself against the darkness while the men below were too surprised to even let out a yell. And then he’d raised his arms high over his head and dropped in.

They’d been pouring, then, and it would have been inconvenient to stop, but after the clay plug had been tamped in and the run was over, the foreman had called a couple of H.P.’s and they’d climbed up. They’d found his coat and in it a letter with directions that it be delivered personally to Mr. Holt.

Amos came up all excited.

“The crazy Jew—” he said. “Can you imagine a guy doing that? I always said he was cracked. Here he’s been going around for years bragging about how he was going to shut down the plant. We were all going to lay off. It was going to cost Mr. Holt eight hundred tons of good iron. And they didn’t even stop pouring.”

The two-minute bell rang and the gangs sort of broke —, up. Russ and I walked towards the line. Amos followed.

“Imagine,” he said, “a guy crazy like that—writing a letter to Mr. Holt. Mr. Holt won’t even hear about it. They’re tapping off another batch of hot iron right now. They’re running old Jerry outa the spout. In another hour they’ll be pouring him into a cylinder block mold. About two-thirty he’ll be coming over the line. And here he thought he was going to lay us all off—shut down the shop so we’d have to take a cut on our pay checks. Instead of that, he’ll be coming over the line this afternoon. We’ll be running nuts on him—I’ll be sucking cotter pins in the motor. Too bad he can’t know about it. He’d sure be burned up.” He laughed at his own joke.

“Lay off,” said Russ. “Your jokes give me the shivers. I’m not in the mood for listening to your talk.”

Amos and Russ went to their stations.

Norm came shagging up with a brainful of worries.

“Take Jerry’s place,” he said to me, as though Jerry were just laying off for the day. “I hate to spare you,” he said. “It’s gonna be crazy-wild today and I’m gonna need more helpers. Do what you can to keep down the gang-talk. We’re gonna boost the line—break it up if we can.”

“There’ll be a lot of bum motors,” I said.

“Don’t I know it?” he said. “We got a heavy schedule. We gotta get out eighty-four hundred today. Why did that crazy Jew have to kill himself for right at this time?”

The bell rang and the shifts changed. It seemed funny, standing in his place—doing the very thing that he’d been doing a few hours before. He’d been doing this same little job for eight years—putting four bearing caps on each up-side-down motor that slid past. That was more than twelve hundred an hour—ten thousand each shift—three million each year—twenty-four million during his time in the factory. Twenty-four million caps on six million motors—that’s what it figured up to in the long end. That, and two holes that his feet had worn in the hard concrete floor. I tell you—times like that, a man gets to thinking about things. He gets to wondering what life is all about—whether Mr. Holt’s ideas about working hard may not be all wrong, and whether Jerry might not have been just as happy, if instead of doing twenty-four million caps, he had done only twenty million, or even only ten. But my thinking was not very clear. The line had started off with a bang and production was fast. Line bosses were riding the buggies and I heard one let out a yell at the top of his voice at a fellow that was standing on a box trying to look out of a window at the blast furnace.

That made me forget what I was trying to figure out. Maybe Jerry, too, had tried to figure out things for himself. Maybe, too, the same things had interfered with his thinking.

The line was confused. Men had their heads together, trying to talk and do their work at the same time. It was easy to guess what they were talking about.

Across from me, and a little below, was Russ. Opposite him was Jeff and next to Jeff, Amos. Unlike most of the others, Russ was giving himself to his job, but he acted gloomy and queer.

Even before lunch, rejected motors began streaming back from the run-in department. When the bell rang the tear-down line was swamped, and defective motors were stacked three high on the floor.

After lunch things got definitely worse. One motor came down the line without pistons; another had pistons but no valves. And still the bosses were afraid to slow down the line, because that would give the fellows more time to gang up and talk. Instead of dying down, the excitement got worse. A rumor would start near the front of the building, sweep down the line, and in twenty minutes it would be all through the department. One rumor had it that Jerry was really Mr. Holt’s son. According to another, Mr. Holt was actually going to shut down and make us all go to a big funeral that he was going to pay for himself. Still another rumor had it that the chemists had found the motor block with Jerry’s remains in it.

In a big gang like ours, where the men are all jammed up close, feelings and emotions are more catching than a bad case of measles, and two or three fellows like Amos can start a wild rumor going and demoralize the whole crew.

Fellows began taking time off—ganging up in the toilets, without asking for relief. I knew what that meant. Line bosses began losing control, shouting orders at men slipping away behind pillars and boxes. Two times I saw Gus with a dumb look on his face, leave his machine and sneak off. The third time his boss grabbed him by the collar and spun him around in his tracks—sent him back.

At two o’clock it looked like the bosses were not going to be able to hold things together until the end of the shift. The line was sull fast, but half the buggies were empties. That meant that the blocks were not coming off the machines up in front; that the gangs on the automatics where Gus worked, were breaking down. Line bosses were hurriedly transferred like shock troops in a battle. Orders flew thick and fast. Straw bosses tried to round up the men—tried to get every man back on the job.

And then a rumor came down the line that set every man’s nerves on the jump. It was a rumor that the cylinder block, containing all that was left of Jerry, had arrived from the foundry and was being machined up in front. Tension built up in the men like a high voltage current. Conversation grew shrill and excited. Word spread to the toilets and the men hurried back to their stations, not wanting to miss out on a thing. Standing shoulder to shoulder with the other men on the line you could feel the tension build up.

Word came that the block was going through the broaching machine. Amos laid out eight nickel-plated cotter pins that he’d dug up from some-where. Jeff said they’d probably stick an American flag on the block like —they did when the twentieth million motor came over the line, only this time the flag would be at half-mast.

A little later we heard that the block was being milled on one of the merry-go-rounds—it was getting nearer. The fellows were getting more excited—the tension more strained.

A little later still the cylinders were being bored out. And then honed. A minute later—they’d found a porcelain tooth in the gray iron.

Men forgot what they were supposed to be doing. They craned their necks and raised up on their toes. One motor came by without a crankshaft—another with a speed wrench stuck upright on a main bearing nut.

One more machine operation to go—boring the holes. Maybe Gus would do it—Jerry’s one friend. Then it would be clamped on a buggy and start down the line. Men’s faces were white. There was no talk. The whole gang on the line surged forward.

There was a yell. Once—and then again and again. It rose high above the noise of the shop—like a man hurt—badly. It was Gus. Every man dropped his job and ran up towards the front of the shop. They jumped over tools and machines—around pillars and boxes. It was a stampede—a riot. And there was Gus—we saw him. Crazy-eyed, he was, standing away from his machine, pointing at the base near the floor.

“He’s in dar!” he hollered. “He’s in dar—I couedn’t halp it. Jerry’s in dar—I tell you! I kilt heem!”

Four or five of us fellows held him—tried to make him shut up. The others grabbed steel bars, power drills, and hammers, and tore down the machine. In no time it was a wreck—pieces scattered wide over the floor.

The automatics were down. The assembly line, of course, stopped. Someone had turned in an emergency call and two orderlies in white clothes came running up with a high-wheeled hospital cart. It took a half dozen men to strap him down, and when they wheeled him away, going swiftly down the long aisle, he was bucking the straps, trying to sit up, and hollering —

“Och Herre—Herre—I couedn’t halp it. I couedn’t halp it. I kilt heem!”

Motor assembly was shut down for the day. All of us fellows went out of the factory and slunk home like a bunch of whipped pups.

## Chapter XXXIV

That night, after supper, we sat around in the house. It was raining outside and we had a fire in the stove and Russ was down in the dumps and quiet, and I was telling Rita about the day in the factory—sort of stringing things out in slow talk; telling about Jerry committing suicide and Gus cracking up, but leaving out the part about wrecking the machine, because I thought it would get on Russ’ nerves. When I finished she said:

“Those two guys—you’ve told me about them before. They were screwy. I don’t see why Mr. Holt lets fellows like that work in the plant.”

“That’s what makes them screwy,” Russ shouted. “They’re all screwy. All the fellows that work on the line—except the niggers—all the whites on the automatics are queer. Go ahead,” he told me, “go ahead—tell her what happened this afternoon. Tell her how you helped wreck the machine.”

“You were in it, too —”

“We were all in it,” he shouted. “All except Jerry. Jerry’s the only one that had sense. He made an end to it—a hot breath and it was all over for him. He’s out of it. You and I—we’ve gotta go back in the morning.”

“What else?” she said. “Men are supposed to grow up. After all—you can’t play hookey all your life. But tell me about wrecking the machine.”

“You tell her,” said Russ. “I’m trying to forget it.”

So then I told her, making it seem as logical as possible. But without giving her an idea how the excitement built up throughout the whole day, it was hard to make the story seem real, so that I don’t know whether she believed it or not. Telling it that way, to her, it all sounded so crazy.

“How funny,” she said, when I finished. “Why didn’t they get a light and look in the machine?”

“They wouldn’t-a had to,” I said, “if they’d only stopped to figure a minute—used their heads. In the first place, there wasn’t room for a man to get in the machine, and in the second place—why would any man want to?”

“Why try to explain it?” Russ said, acting as though he was sore. “Why try to make it sound as though it was something we tried to figure out? We were all wild-eyed crazy. The next time— We would have torn up a man just as quick. It gives me the jitters.”

But Rita wouldn’t take it serious. She got up, walked around the stove, and sat down on his lap.

“Poor old Russ,” she said. “The blue debbils got him, tonight. He’s got the jitters and he’s awful discouraged.”

“Don’t bother me,” he said. “Get up.”

“I don’t want to,” she said. “I’m going to sit on your lap. I guess I’ve got a right—if I feel like it.”

She fooled around with his shirt collar. Unfastened the button and then tried to fasten it again.

“Come to bed, Russ,” she said. “Forget about the factory—tonight.”

“Don’t,” he said. “You’re pinching.”

“But I want to,” she said. “I want to fuss around with you.”

I got up.

“Well—” I said, “I guess I’ll get me a drink and go into my room. I’ve gotta write a letter to a man about a dog.”

I went to the sink, let the water run for a minute. She said something more and then he. His words were sharp. She came back. He got up quick—gave her a push, and she half rolled and half fell to the floor. She got up and struck him a stinging blow along side the face. Came at him again but he grabbed her by the wrist and spun her away. She was mad clean through. She grabbed the poker—cut the air—missed—and then threw it, hitting him in the face. She tried to get it again but he caught her. Held her arm like a vice.

“Now do you understand?” he said. “I don’t want to go to bed. See?” There was a little trickle of blood from his lip.

“You’re no good,” she screamed at him, “you’re a fake. You’re impotent! impotent! impotent! Do you understand that? That means you’re no good, Russ—no good.”

For a second they stood looking hard at each other—their faces white. Her eyes blazing. Then he spun her half-way across the floor, got his coat, and went out the door. She flopped down on a chair and threw her head on her arms on the table—she was crying.

I went up to the table. She raised her head and dabbed at her eyes with her apron.

“He hurt me,” she said. “Look at that.”

There was a red circle around her arm where he’d held her.

“Why you little hell-cat,” I said, “he shoulda busted it. He shoulda took you over his knee and paddled you good.”

She looked up at me. Her eyes wide and the tears cold on her cheeks.

“Get out!” she said, standing up and stamping her foot. “Get out!”

“I’m gonna,” I said, getting into my coat. “I’m gonna get out fast as I can and follow your husband—see he don’t kill himself. He’s nervous tonight—got the jitters. You heard what he said about Jerry being out of it all. He’s liable to do the same thing if I don’t stop him. I told you some of what happened today in the factory. But not all. I didn’t tell you how the guys hollered—how they looked—what a job we had to strap Gus down on the cart. I was telling it easy—trying to make Russ think it wasn’t so bad, trying not to stir up his feelings. You had to go and spoil it all.”

I went out. Russ was a long way down the street, walking fast. I hurried after, trying to catch up. Then I decided to follow—keep out of sight. No use getting excited, I thought, if he’s only going to the beer joint—only going to get drunk.

Where there were stores I kept close to the buildings. Where there were street lights I kept in the dark. He neared a corner where I knew he would turn either to the left or the right. To the right was the beer joint; in the other direction was the factory. He turned left.

I walked fast. Near the corner I ran, not wanting to lose him. From the corner I looked down the street. For a minute I didn’t see him. There were other men walking, fellows going to the factory, who worked the graveyard. Then I saw him again, coming into the light. He had no hat. I followed again. A bunch of fellows got on a street car, but he continued to walk. No use spending good money for car fare, I thought, when there’s no hurry—when all you’re going to do is jump into a blast furnace, maybe. I remembered again what he’d said “A hot breath and it’s all over.” Simple as that, in his mind—the way he was feeling. Think of a woman trying to make love to a man when he’s feeling like that.

I remembered a piece I’d read in the Sunday paper about favorite suicide places. One was a volcano in Japan. People had to climb a high mountain to reach it. Another was a high bridge near Los Angeles. “Suicide Bridge” it was called and there’d been a picture. More than a hundred people had jumped off into the canyon beneath it and they had to put up a high fence over the railing. Maybe they’d have to do something about the blast furnace, I thought.

There were more men on the streets. I walked faster. Near the gate, in the crowd, I’d be able to walk right behind him. There was no danger of losing him now; no danger of his seeing me in the crowd. Blindfolded, I’d still know the way.

I decided not to call in the H.P.’s unless I had to. I’d wait till he got near the furnace. Then I’d talk to him. Step out of the dark and put my hand on his shoulder.

“You got a job, Russ,” I’d say. “Think of all the poor bastards in the streets of Detroit looking for work. You’ve got a wife and kid—they need you.”

He went in through number two gate. Besides the guard, there were a couple of H.P.’s. They noticed our badges, but they were interested mostly in the stream of men going out, noticing pockets—coats that bulged out too much in the wrong places. It was easy to stick a Bendix gear under your hat—a roll of soldering wire inside your shirt. In Detroit, these things are like cash.

Through the wide alley between the high buildings I followed. Past the power house; the glass plant; the spring and upset building. Then he turned left—unexpected. I couldn’t figure it out. He was going directly away from the blast furnace—over the railroad tracks—past final assembly—away from the noise and the lights.

Ahead there was nothing but dark acres of corded steel; the boneyard; the field full of old cars waiting their turn for the crusher. Beyond that, a high fence. I had to walk faster to keep him in sight. Now the roar of the plant was behind us; ahead, the boneyard under a few open lights that swung crazily in a blustery sky. He went in among the old machines. The manipulator was still there in the mud. He went up to it; pried open the rusty tool-box; took out a rag and began swiping.

I leaned weakly against the dark side of an old welding machine. Weakly, I said to myself, over and over again “Well I’ll be—I’ll be a son-of-a-bitch.”

I watched him a while just to see what he would do. His big hands curved over the brasses; his fingers touching and feeling; the tips of his fingers sensing the feel of the metal that was cold and dead. Along the smooth shafts his hands slid, gentle and yet strong in their motions; firm and gentle; flowing along the cold steel, except when they rested a moment, cupped over a brass journal, like a man’s hand on a young woman’s breast. Continually in motion, along the easy curves of gear housings; on bare iron; along naked steel—exploring, as if trying to draw strength from the cold metal. He climbed to the controls and let his hands flow over the dead levers; levers that his hands had once known so well; touching each one—sliding his hands along the smooth metal—feeling them—trying to understand them with the tips of his bare fingers. And then down again—swiping away 2 bit of rust—reaching a hand into a nest of cold gears; leaning the while against the base of the machine—one hand exploring and touching, the other pressed against the bare, unpainted steel.

It came to me, all of a sudden, that what he was doing was sort of saying farewell to the machine—getting up nerve to pull off some desperate act. But I was wrong again.

I stepped up into the light and he saw me. He came out of it, then, like someone waking up from a dream.

“You here?” he said.

“‘What’s the idea?” I asked. ““What’s the idea, coming out here like this?”

He stood there in the mud to his ankles, twisting the rag.

“Why don’t you leave me be?” he said. “Why did you have to follow? I had to get away by myself. Old Betsy here—to see her again—I thought it would do me good. I was mixed up—feeling low.”

“You feel better now—since swiping around on that thing?”

“I don’t know.”

“Judast, Russ,” I said. “What’s the matter with you? I never saw you acting like that. When I think of the man you was in the drop forge and then look at you now—slobbering—standing there in the mud —”

“That’s why I came out here,” he said. “Old Betsy here—I thought I would get some of my old strength back. But it’s no good, Bennie. No good.”

“Come on,” I said. “All you’ll get out here in this mud is wet feet and a dose of pneumonia.”

I grabbed his arm. Made him walk fast. Neither one of us said a word till we got outside the plant. Then I talked to him again. Tried to get him straightened out.

“You gotta snap out of it,” I said. “You gotta get ahold of yourself. The way you been acting lately is something fierce. Today was bad in the factory, but tomorrow everything will be running along smooth again.”

“I gotta get out of it,” he said.

“Get out of it? How you gonna get out of it now? You still owe me thirty bucks. You ain’t got no money saved up. All you got to get out with is a half-interest in the outboard and an old jaloppie that won’t run.”

“Ain’t you gonna pull out with me, Bennie?”

“I’m used to Detroit. I’m not saying I’m gonna pull out—I’m not saying I won’t. I don’t want to talk about it now.”

“I don’t wanna pull out alone. Not alone—Bennie.”

“Alone? What about your wife and the kid?”

“We’d make a place for ’em,” he said. “Then get ’em to come. Build a cabin—not wait till we get enough money saved up for a trailer. I know a place near a river where the grass comes down to the water—tall trees behind for the wind to play in. We’d make a low fence to keep the kid in—paint it white. There’d be wild morning-glory vines by the windows and a bunch of hollyhocks near the gate. Do you see how it’d look, Bennie? She’d like it. There’d be no more arguments—no fights.”

“And use what for money? Blame it all, Russ, I don’t know why I bother about you. I should have stayed in the house. The way you talked—about ending it all—I thought you was going to jump in the blast furnace.”

“No, Bennie—no. Not that.”

“You ain’t got the nerve—have you, Russ?”

“Not even to think about it,” he said. “Think what would happen! Think what happened to Jerry!”

“I wish now,” I said, “I’d-a let you gone out by yourself. Instead of that I got in an argument with your wife and got put out of the house. Dog-gone you—you’re gonna apologize for us both. And then you’re gonna make a new start. Cut out the beer joints and stuff—work steady—be decent to Rita.”

“I don’t want to go back to the factory,” he said. “I don’t want to ever put another nut on a bolt.”

“Well, you’re gonna,” I said. “You’re gonna go back and like it. You’re not gonna let a little thing like what happened today get you down. You’re gonna get ahold of yourself—make a new start. And the way to do it is not to run away from it. The way to do it is to stick out your chest and raise up your chin and lick the tar out of any man that says you can’t hold down a-job on the line.”

When we reached the house it was dark. We went in and Russ turned on the light. We hung our coats up on the wall behind the stove. Russ went into the bedroom and came out.

“She’s gone,” he said in a whisper. “The kid too. It’s my fault, Bennie. What’ll I do?” He had a scared look on his face.

“Do nothing,” I said. “Go to bed.”

“I can’t. I’ve got to know where they are.”

“I’ll tell you,” I said. “They’re at the Old Man’s house.”

“I’ve got to get them,” he said, making a grab for his coat. But I stopped him.

“No you don’t,” I said. “Try to talk to her now and there’d be another argument. Let her alone. for tonight. It’ll give you a chance to figure out what a sap you’ve been. It’ll give her a chance to cool off her temper. In the morning you can tell her you’re sorry—ask her to come back.”

I took his coat and hung it back on the nail. Then I tarned off the light and we went into our rooms. I heard one of his shoes drop to the floor, and after waiting till I almost fell off to sleep, I heard the other.

## Chapter XXXV

In the morning we left the house early and stopped at the Rettinellis’ on the way to the factory. The Old Man had already left for work but Mrs. Rettinelli was there and came out before we had a chance to knock. We should have throwed our hats in through the door even before going up on the porch to find out how she felt, because she was on the war-path. Her elbows stuck out and she cut loose on us with some high-tension Italian so fast that we couldn’t understand a word that she said. But even so, it was easy enough to figure out, in a general way, what she meant.

Rita was nowhere in sight, but her kid brother was, and he poked his nose past the Old Lady’s elbow and when she ran out of breath he made things additionally clear.

“Sis is mad at youse guys,” he said. “And Mom says to get off our porch and we changed the baby’s name back to Tony—like Pa’s. Bennie’s too sissy.”

“Well—that’s that,” I said to Russ. “Let’s go.”

We retreated as far as the sidewalk.

“Wait a minute,” said Russ. “Maybe we oughta do something more.”

“That’s right,” I said. “What we oughta do is slap that kid’s ears down for him.”

That started them both off again and then there was nothing for us to do but clear out, because the neighbors were beginning to take notice of the argument and it was easy to see where they stood. Russ didn’t know what to do. He couldn’t make up his mind about nothing.

“If I could see her,” he said.

“But you can’t. Didn’t you hear what the Old Lady said?”

“If I could talk to her —”

“You can’t do that either—until she gets over being mad at you.”

“Maybe I oughta stay home? Maybe she’ll come after her stuff—and I’ll see her.”

The kid kept a sharp eye on us from the porch. The Old Lady watched us through a window.

“Don’t take it so serious,” I said. “Give her time to get over it; let her alone for a couple of days. After the Old Man pays for a can of that high-powered baby food he’ll be glad to ship them both back.”

We went to the factory. While going through the gate he was still trying to make up his mind whether we should have taken the street car or walked; whether he should have come to the factory or stayed home; whether to buy his lunch outside the gate or in the shop. I was getting more disgusted with him all the time.

We were two minutes late. It was the first time that year I’d had red ink on my card. Russ took his place on the line; I stepped in beside a new fellow who was taking Jerry’s place and showed him what to do. The fellow took a hold good, and being a nigger, I knew he’d get onto the job without tying himself up into a knot.

The line was slow and quite a few of the old fellows were absent. There was still some talk about what had happened the day before but there were no more rumors and the men were no longer excited.

About ten in the morning a bunch of H.P.’s with polished shoes came down from the office and stood around the bare spot on the floor where Gus’ machine had been, and looked wise. They talked to Norm and then pulled some of the fellows off the line to ask questions. They didn’t bother Russ or me and I was glad when they left.

And then, shortly after, Russ started dragging over and getting into trouble. I hurried over to see what was the matter. He was fighting the job—not working quiet and smooth like the others—and although the line was not fast, he looked tired and worried.

“Listen, Bennie,” he said, when I stepped in beside him, “do you hear what it says? What that belt says—up there?”

“No. What?” At first I didn’t take him serious.

“It keeps pounding at me,” he said. “It keeps pounding—IM-PO-TENT—IM-PO-TENT—IM-PO-TENT—over and over. It’s been going like that all morning. All morning I’ve been trying to shut it outa my ears. But I can’t. Don’t you get it?”

“Aw-w—nuts, Russ. Lay off.”

“But I’m telling you—Bennie—it’s so. There it is again. Hear it?”

“No.”

“It’s louder now. There it goes—IM-PO-TENT—IM-PO-TENT—IM-PO-TENT—. One heavy beat—two slow—over and over. And it’s so, Bennie—I’m no good—I’m impotent! She was right—when she said it. I’ve been no good ever since they changed the belt on that motor.”

“Love of Mike, Russ—you want the fellows to know all about your wife troubles? Pipe down.”

He’d been talking loud—holding a handful of nuts—letting me do the job. Amos was edging up close. He had a nose for anything that made talk. Already he sensed something funny. He had a grin on his face and his ears fairly stuck out.

I had Russ back where he belonged.

“Now stay up,” I said. “Tend to your job. If I see you getting behind again I’m going to tell Norm. Cut out that crazy talk.”

“I can’t stay up,” he said, “not with that belt, Bennie. I’ve got the willies. That belt’s the cause of it all. I was all right till they changed it.”

“That belt stuff,” I said, “is the bunk. It’s giving me a bad pain in the neck. I don’t wanna hear no more about it.”

“See Norm,” he said. “Talk to him. Tell him to take it off and put on the other. I’ll be all right with the other. I’ll promise not to drag over. I’ll keep up on the job, Bennie.”

“No—by Judast!”

“You’ve got to, Bennie. This one is driving me nutty. I’ve got the willies. It’s been the cause of all my trouble with you—with Norm—and with Rita. I’d be all right with the other.”

I had to step in again and do three or four motors. He didn’t have his mind on the job. And talking to him, I could see, wasn’t going to do any good.

I stepped out and left him. I figured I’d have to do something quick—get him transferred, if possible. I decided to have a talk with Norm right away. If a line boss came to Norm first—Russ would go out through the door. I got a hold of Norm just as the bell rang for lunch.

“Norm,” I said, “Russ has got himself tied up with that belt again.”

“That crazy lumber-jack,” he said. “Get me his timecard. I’ll fire him.”

“But listen —”

“Listen, nothing,” he said. “I’m gonna fire him. After what happened yesterday I ain’t taking no chances.”

“But I’m telling you, Norm—that guy’s a whizz. He can run anything that’s got wheels on.”

“Who’d want him?” he said. “I can’t give him no recommend.”

“He was a good man in drop forge. He’d be good again—around big stuff, where there’s hard work to do. The tougher a job is the better he likes it. That’s the kind of guy he is.”

“Yeh? Don’t make me laugh. It’s the old buddy stuff again—ain’t it, Bennie? All right—get me a transfer slip from the desk. Make it snappy.”

I got the slip and Norm made it out.

“Give it to him,” he said. “And tell him to punch out—now. We’ll get along without him this afternoon.”

I started away when he called.

“Better go with him,” he said. “If they got no place for him—don’t bring him back.”

Russ was lined up behind a big nigger at the ptomaine wagon, waiting his turn to buy a carton of beans. I pulled him out.

“Come on,” I said. “We gotta get you a transfer. You’re all through in motor assembly.”

I was expecting an argument, but he said nothing.

We went to the clock and punched out his card and took it up to the desk. Then we went to the transfer department, and all the way through the plant, instead of walking up beside me, he trailed behind like a tired pup.

In the transfer department he acted as though he didn’t know quite what it was all about. I talked to the clerk.

“What you got for this man?” I asked.

“Final assembly,” he said, “blue mass.”

“That all? Nothing else?”

“Rolling mill.”

“What kind of work would he be doing there?”

“How would I know? I don’t own the place. I only work here.”

“You wouldn’t kid me, would you?” I asked.

“Maybe you think it ain’t work,” he said, “standing here listening to you guys all day asking them kind of questions.”

“Maybe we oughta come back tomorrow,” said Russ. “Maybe they’d have something better.”

“What kind of job you looking for, Buddy?” asked the clerk, turning his high-powered attention to Russ.

“I’d kinda like to get a job sweeping,” he said.

“We got more sweepers now’n we know what to do with. Come back tomorrow, there might not be anything at all. Your card says you worked in the drop forge. I’m going to send you to the rolling mill,” he said, starting to fill out the slip. “Big fellow like you oughta get along good there. Report four-thirty, this afternoon. You’re on the night shift.”

He gave Russ the card and we left. Before separating, near the motor assembly building, I gave him another talk, telling him things that I thought would do him some good.

“You were lucky,” I said, “getting that job in the rolling mill. There’s big machines there—the kind of stuff that you like. You shoulda jumped at the chance. Instead of that you stand there and tell the guy you’d kinda like to be a sweeper. Kinda—. What’s the matter with you, Russ? I never see you act that way before.”

“I don’t know,” he said, acting sort of nervous and queer. “I don’t know. I keep thinking all the ume I’m no good. I keep hearing that belr—what it said. I keep hearing it. You and Norm shouldn’t have changed it, Bennie.”

“Me and Norm? Where do you get that me and Norm stuff?” I said.

“If I fall down on this job—hear that noise in my ears ——”

“Aw-w-w for the love of Mike, Russ, cut it out. Forget about that belt—can’t you? Try to act like a man. You got a new job. You’re starting all over now—see? You’ll be around big machines—hot steel. It’ll be like it was in the drop forge—big jobs going on all the time. And there’s no belts.”

“No belts?”

“No belts.”

I heard the two-minute bell ring and remembered I’d had no lunch yet. Men were hurrying past where we stood.

“All right now,” I said. “Go home—get some sleep. Get going.”

I put my hand on his shoulder and gave him a push and then left him.

From the wide door of the building I watched him. It was a minute or two before he started moving off towards the gate.

## Chapter XXXVI

When I got home that night the house was cold and dark. Russ had already gone to the factory and the dirty breakfast dishes still littered the table and the alarm clock had stopped and behind the stove was the outboard motor in a wooden box. Russ must have packed it that afternoon with pieces of used lumber that he’d probably got from the hardware store. There were chips and pieces of wood on the floor and across the top of the box in mutilated letters was stenciled: “F. O. B. Detroit.”

I looked into their bedroom and saw in a minute that all of Rita’s things were gone. Even the red box in which she had always kept her black velvet dress and silver dancing shoes was no longer up on the shelf in the closet and I happened to think that not once in all the time she’d been married to Russ had she worn them.
