One of the Rettinellis, the kid brother I figured, had probably come for her things in the morning.

I cooked me some hamburger and potatoes and ate, and then washed the dishes, and the fire went out, and the tea kettle puttered a couple of times and then stopped, and the excitement dropped to an all-time low in that house.

I thought some about going to Rettinelli’s to try to have a talk with Rita and then remembered that the Old Man would be there and that he’d probably be drunk and pretty sore about things in general, and I decided that when Russ braced up again he would be able to handle things better than I. And then I thought about Russ and wondered what kind of job he’d got in the rolling mill and right then I got up, put on my coat, and left the house for the factory to see how he was making out.

The rolling mill was at the rear of the plant about a mile away from the main gate. Outsiders and those not working there were not allowed on the floor because the work that went on there was big stuff and dangerous. But there was a small door at the side of the building through which in the day time the guides led the long strings of visitors. From this door a long stairway led to a steel walk high up above the ground near the ceiling and from this point I saw the whole vast floor spread out below.

There were three mills in the building and two were running, rolling out long red snakes of steel, and beside each set of rollers stood a man on a platform with a pair of long tongs to seize the heads of the red snakes to guide them into the grooves. On the whole wide floor there were only these two and one of these two was Russ.

Often before I’d stood up on the high bridge watching the work, but never at night. Now the vast interior of the building lay in semi-darkness except where a giant crane lifted glowing ingots of steel from the “soaking pits”; where the red snakes flowed, and where the high spot-lights stabbed through the dark at the two men below.

In spite of his six feet two and magnificent shoulders the distance and the size of the machine at which he stood made Russ look small and insignificant and puny. He was like a puppet in a floor show. Here the machines were all giants—electric motors with ladders for men to climb; drive shafts of polished steel as big as a man’s waist; rollers so large that the bearings alone weighed tons. Here the whole process of rolling steel was so nearly automatic that you had to look for the operators—the men in glass cages, suspended between the floor and the ceiling, who played with rows of small levers that controlled the motion and speed of motors whose horsepower was rated in thousands.

The whole business of the plant was to change short, heavy pieces of steel into light ones; short, fat snakes into long, thin ones. They came out of the darkness on long tables, heads bobbing and weaving. They came with incredible speed—slowed up a little as they came up to Russ standing on a steel platform beside the big rollers.

There were different grooves in the rollers—big ones and little. Russ seized the heads with his tongs—got the snakes started into the right grooves. Then the rollers got in their powerful work. A fat snake went in—a slim, angry snake squirted out on the opposite side, its head bouncing away into the darkness.

. Water sprayed over the hot rollers and for a minute or two Russ would be lost to sight in the steam.

The snake having passed through, the big rollers would come to a sudden stop and reverse. The snake would stop, too, and return. Russ had to be quick, because what had been the snake’s tail was now its head and came bouncing back to be seized again and once more passed through the rollers.

If you looked close you saw what it was that made the snakes stop and dart back and forth. In the beds of the long tables there were hundreds of spinning feed rolls. These were controlled by the operators in the steel and glass cages.

I watched Russ awhile. I thought he was getting along all right. He was not yet as expert in the job as the other fellow—more excited. The other fellow refused to let himself be hurried. When the red snakes came too fast he shot a hard-boiled look up at the cage over his head. Once he leaned on his tongs and forced the operator to bring the spinning feed rolls to a complete stop.

Being on a new job Russ was naturally excited. He swung the tongs about more than was necessary, and each time one of the long snakes darted towards him he flinched back as though fearing that it might not slow up. Then it shot through the rollers and was coming back at him again almost, it seemed, before he had recovered his poise.

I could see it was not exactly the kind of job that he would have liked. “I like machines,” he had said, “‘chat I run—not those that run me.” Well, he wasn’t exactly running this one, but at the same time, he was doing his part on a big job.

Working there, a tiny black shape on the floor, he had his back towards me and not once had he looked up. I had a notion to let out a yell and shout down 2 word of encouragement. And then I decided it wouldn’t be a wise thing to do.

ing a job like that a man has to keep his wits about him. A sudden turn—or jerk—and he might easily slip off the shiny steel platform. Those big motors could stop and reverse quick—but not quick enough, if a man should happen to lose his footing and fall down in front of the rollers.

I went down the long stairs and walked home, thinking that Russ was all straightened out and that he was going to get along swell.

I went home and to bed and it seemed like I’d only just got to sleep when there was a loud knock on the door. I jerked on my pants and went out.

There on the porch stood an H.P. with a flash light —in his hand and he wanted to know if he’d come to the house where Russ lived and asked if Rita was home. He’d left his car running and lights on, and the way he stood there and the way his heavy words came out of the dark—I knew what he’d come to say. If he’d hollered out at me at the top of his voice, “There’s been an accident—a bad one!” he couldn’t have made it any plainer to me.

“Did it kill him?” I asked.

“They had to take his legs off,” he said.

I went into the house and while putting on the rest of my clothes I thought those dry words would never stop floating before me.

I came out again and explained how it was that Rita was not at the house.

“We’ll have to find her,” I said.

We got into the car and I told him which way to go and after a bit my brain started working again and I asked questions. The H.P. didn’t know all the details, but somehow, he told me, Russ had got into the machine. Before the operator could stop the rollers, both legs had been crushed. They’d amputated above the knees.

At Rettinelli’s the house was dark but after pounding the door the Old Man stuck his head out of a window. Rita was not at home, he said. She was at the dance hall. He grumbled something more in Italian and then pulled his head back into the house and slammed down the window as if he was burned up about something, and if Rita was at the taxi-dance place, I guess maybe he was.

It didn’t take us long to reach the dance hall and while the H.P. waited I went upstairs to get Rita.

About fifty couples were dancing to swing music and I saw Rita with a tall sailor who was trying to do the Big Apple, and a guy dressed like a waiter tried to stop me from going on the floor. The music and everything seemed far away and all I could think of was what the H.P. had told me and when I came up to Rita and she stopped dancing, I blurted out—“They had to take his legs off—Russ.”

She looked at me hard with scared eyes and grabbed my shoulders and said “Bennie! What do you mean, Bennie?”

“Russ,” I said. “He’s had an accident.”

The sailor jerked her arm.

“It’s a gag,” he said. “Come on, baby—let’s dance.”

“Don’t,” she said. “It’s my husband.” And to me, “Where is he?”

“In the hospital,” I said. “There’s a car waiting to take us. I thought maybe —”

“Of course. Quick, Bennie—let’s go.”

She got her wrap and threw it over her shoulders and we hurried down and got into the back of the car. While driving, the H.P. repeated what he’d already told me, which wasn’t a great deal, and then I told her about Russ getting transferred to the rolling mill and how I’d watched him at work in his new job that very night.

aes?

“It’s my fault,” she moaned, “it’s my fault, Bennie. I shouldn’t have acted the way I did. Oh, why didn’t I talk to him this morning? Why didn’t I talk to him, Bennie?”

“Why didn’t you?” I said.

“I don’t know, Bennie. I wanted to—but I was still mad. I wanted to teach him a lesson. I wanted him to see how it felt to be left alone. Always there was his talk about clamming—about going away. And I couldn’t—not after we had the baby. His talk frightened me—I loved him so. Always—I’d put my hand out in the morning before waking—I’d feel him, and wake with a prayer. ‘Thank God,’ I’d say, ‘he’s still there.’ I wanted him to come for me tonight—to the dance hall. I wanted him to dance with me again—see me again in this dress. Oh, Bennie—why did it have to turn out like this? Why can’t we turn time back like a clock? Why can’t we live one little day over again to correct our mistakes?”

She broke down and cried and I sort of put my arm around her shoulder.

“It’s not your fault,” I said. “Lately he’s been acting nervous and sort of funny. There was a belt over his head. He blamed it on that. And then, during the last couple of days—he was worse. That’s why we had to get him a transfer. I thought being around big machines—working hard—doing tough jobs, he’d come out of it all right.”

She was silent, except for her sobbing. There was a red light and the car rolled to a stop.

“Ain’t that the way it is?” said the H.P. “When you’re trying to make time every signal gives you the laugh.”

I didn’t answer. I guess he was only saying something to make talk. The idling motor turned over lightly; a lake boat sent a booming note out of the night fog. The lights changed and we started. I drew my arm back.

“Better get a hold of yourself now,” I said. “We’re getting near the plant.”

She braced up and dabbed at her face with a small handkerchief. I could see she was trying to steel herself for something that would take every bit of nerve that she had.

“Was he running the machine—the machine that hurt him?” she asked.

“Well—no. Things are pretty automatic in there. There were a couple of fellows in a glass cage pulling levers, but you couldn’t tell which one was doing what.”

“I thought so,” she said. “He used to tell me ‘I like machines that I run—I hate those that run me.’”

“He could run anything that had wheels on it,” I said. “He was a good mechanic.”

“Good at anything,” she said, “where he had a chance. He would have been a good clammer, perhaps. You were friends—you and he were always together. But there were things about him you didn’t understand—didn’t know. I didn’t either—but I do now. And now it’s too late.”

“Not too late,” I said. “You’re talking as if he were dead. There are lots of good jobs in the factory that a man without —”

“Don’t say it,” she screamed. “There are no more good jobs for him. You don’t understand yet, Bennie. He needed the wind in his hair—the rain in his face. Don’t you see—we were all three against him. You and I and the factory. On the other side were the woods—the rivers—the blackbirds building their nests in the swamps. We won, Bennie. Don’t you understand? I see it all, now—why didn’t I before?”

To tell the truth about it I didn’t see. But I didn’t argue with her because she was letting herself get worked up again. Besides, we had reached the factory.

The H.P. waved to the guard and we drove in through the gate. In a minute we’d be at the hospital. Too soon, I figured, because Rita was shaking.

“We want to walk,” I called to the H.P. “Let us out here. A little walk before we go in will be good for us.”

“Sure,” he said. “I know how it is. It’ll give you a chance to pull yourselves together a little.”

He pulled up alongside the front of the final assembly building and stopped and opened the door for us like a taxi-cab driver.

“I’ll go with you,” he said. “It’s my orders, you know. If we go through the building, here, we won’t have to go over the tracks. Tracks are dangerous at night.”

He led the way. I took Rita’s arm and we went into the building. The burst of noise as we stepped through the door was like an ocean wave that rolled over everything and went on and on. I liked the smells and the sights and the speed of things. The Cooper-Hewitts shot their bright lights against the glistening sides of new cars; pneumatic hoists purred as bodies swung down to meet chassis at just the right moment; electric wrenches whirred continuously; there was the energetic beat of air hammers on steel. It was too noisy to talk.

We walked in the wide traffic aisle that ran through the length of the building. Tractor drivers pulled their loads to one side and stopped; swampers and stockmen moved boxes of stuff out of the way and stood respectfully to one side while we passed. It reminded me of the times I’d seen an English actress come through the plant with two special guides.

Rita tried to hold her dress from the floor as she hurried along. I held her arm and watched where we walked. She carried a beaded bag and had some kind of sparkling ornament on her head that shimmered and danced as she walked, and the smell of lilacs seemed to come from her hair. She wore earrings that looked like diamonds and the skin of her neck was snow-white against the edge of her black velvet dress.

Hundreds of fellows stopped their work for a second or two and stared as we passed, and I was glad that I had thought to put on my good suit in the hurry and excitement of leaving the house.

It must have been an unusual sight for the Hungarians, the Polacks, the Italians, the Russians, the Hollanders, the niggers, and all the others working there on the graveyard shift in the loneliest time of the whole night—to see a beautiful young girl in a floating wrap and evening dress come through the shop.

Many of them had probably never seen a woman dressed like that in all their ordinary lives. Some of them must have thought she was a movie star and that she was my wife. Not many would have guessed that she was only a taxi-dancer hurrying to the side of her husband who lay crushed and mangled in the Holt hospital and who until that very night had been a Holt worker like themselves; not many would have guessed that her earrings were glass and that there was probably nothing in the beaded purse but a couple of dimes.

There are lots of good jobs in a place like the Holt factory for a man without legs. That’s what I was going to tell Rita when she stopped me.

Russ would be a Holt worker again. The company would find a place for him. I’d help.

That was one of the advantages of working for a big outfit like the Holt Motor Company—they took care of their men. They didn’t have to kick out their cripples like the smaller outfits that didn’t have the jobs nor the money to carry them.

I thought of the salvage department on the top floor of a high building where the factory visitors never came—a whole department where every man lacked a leg or an arm or a nose or an eye; where the floor sweepings were dumped on a long belt, and the men sorted out bits of brass, cotter pins, bolts, lock washers, small pieces of copper, etc. The bosses had things fixed up for them. The men without hands used steel hooks instead of fingers; those without feet used chairs with wheels on them. Some of the old men with good feet were even allowed to sit down at their jobs.

But I couldn’t see Russ working up there. He’d get bored with the jobs up there—they’d be too easy—too simple.

And then something happened that gave me an idea all of a sudden. We came to the place on the line where they put on the fenders and I saw the man without legs who shot across the floor and under the cars on a scooter and put in the bolts. Rita saw him, too, and she stopped and put up a hand as though she had suddenly seen a ghost there in the factory.

“What’s the matter?” I said.

She made a sound and said something but I couldn’t hear what it was and we started walking again.

She was excited and you couldn’t blame her. All she saw there was a man without legs.

What she didn’t see was that the man was clever; that he was doing his work twice as well as the man on the opposite side of the car whose legs, as long as he was on the job, were only a nuisance. She didn’t know that the engineers had rigged up a special chromium-plated scooter for him with ball bearings, and that the straw boss was not allowed to fire him without an o-keh from the safety department. She didn’t stop to figure that the man was pulling down the same dough as the rest; that he was hanging onto his job for dear life, knowing that if he was fired for being absent without cause, he’d never be able to get back in the shop.

The man was probably saving his money—not buying outboard motors and old cars—not running around to beer joints at night. She had said “There are no more good jobs for him.”

She didn’t know that when the man without legs had worked on the day shift that the crowds of visitors coming through the factory had watched him in openmouthed wonder.

Somewhere in his book Mr. Holt says “If there’s one thing the American worker lacks—it’s discipline.” Russ never had any.

Thinking about all those things—letting them run through my mind—I got to thinking about what I’d tell that fellow up in the transfer department when the time came.

We reached the hospital. It was a squarish, two story building in the heart of the plant.

On the ground floor were the first aid doctors who took care of small injuries.

Above, was the operating room and two rows of small rooms with high beds in them. Here, in a long hall with dim lights, a doctor in a sleeveless white gown came up and the H.P. went back down the stairs. The doctor spoke to me first.

“You Bennie?” he said, poking a finger in the direction of my stomach.

“Yeh,” I said. “And this is his wife. Tell us about him.”

He looked at Rita in a kind, gentle way. He was about the same age as her father.

“I guess you know we had to amputate,” he said.

“Both legs?” she asked.

He nodded.

“Above the knees,” he said. She turned her head as though trying not to look at his bare arms and hands.

“We never like to do those things,” he said, “but sometimes we have to. And when they have to be done—the quicker we do them the better. We tried to call you.”

Opposite us a door opened and a nurse came out into the hall. When the door closed again I had the feeling that he was behind that door—in that room.

“Is there danger?” I asked.

“Not much,” he said. “Some loss of blood, of course. But they had him up here in no time and he has a physique like a bull.”

“Let me see him,” said Rita. “Please—now. Let me go in.”

“In a minute,” he said, putting his hand on her arm. “But I want you to promise—no emotional stuff while you’re in there.”

“I promise—if I have to bite off my tongue.”

“And about the amputation—he doesn’t know.”

“Doesn’t know?”

“Not yet. He’s had enough shock for one night.”

“How could he help but—how could you keep it from him?”

“He’s had an anaesthetic. And a couple of shots. No feeling—you know. Not yet.”

The nurse came back down the hall. He spoke to her.

“Let these people see the patient,” he said. And to us “Go in now—make it short.”

The nurse opened the door—the same door—and we saw Russ. His face was white and the old fighting spirit was gone. Knowing him—it was a shock just to see him lying there flat and helpless on a hospital bed. I had expected to see the bottom part of the bed smooth and empty, but they had arranged some material under the lower part of the sheets to take the place of his legs that were not there.

Rita threw herself forward and covered his face and his hair. She held his head in her hands and kissed him over and over. Tight little sobs came from her throat, and there were tears, I guess, but no wailing and moaning.

“I’m so glad—I’m so glad,” she repeated.

“You went away,” he said, looking at her with troubled eyes as though trying to recall something he couldn’t quite remember. She brushed at her eyes.

“But I’m back now, Russ,” she said, trying to smile.

“Why did you go away?” he asked. “It’s such a long time —”

“It doesn’t matter—now,” she said, kissing his mouth and his cheeks. “All that matters now is that I’m back—that I’ll never leave you again—that I’ll take care of you, Russ. Take care of you always.”

“What are you glad about? You said you were glad.”

“That I’m here, Russ. That I still have you—to love and take care of.”

“Oh.”

Then he was quiet a minute while she stroked his hair and whispered something that I couldn’t hear. The nurse remained near the door and I stood at the foot of the bed and I couldn’t keep from looking at the long bump under the sheets that were supposed to be his two legs. It pulled at my eyes like a magnet. Russ put out his hand and touched Rita’s dress.

“You were dancing,” he said.

“I was hoping you’d come,” she answered low. “I was hoping you’d come and dance with me and take me home—like you used to.”

“But I didn’t.”

“You were on the night shift,” she said. “And I didn’t know.”

“I was on the night shift,” he repeated. “In the rolling mill.”

And then for the first time, it seemed, he noticed me. He tried to raise himself up on his elbow and his eyes opened wide as though he were coming out of a sleep.

“Bennie,” he said, “where was you when I called? Why didn’t you come? I was in the mill—now I’m here.”

I put my hand on his arm. The nurse stepped forward.

“You’re all right, Russ,” I said. “Take it easy, old boy.”

“I’m not all right,” he shouted. “Something’s happened. There’s been an accident—my legs are all numb. I was in the mill, Bennie—fighting long pieces of steel—trying to make them go in. They were like snakes. They kept coming at me. They were like the motors—there was no end—I couldn’t stop them. And then something happened. I called you, Bennie—I yelled. Why didn’t you come?”

Rita and the nurse tried to quiet him but he pushed them away with his arms.

“They came at me,” he shouted. “Out of the dark—bobbing their heads. They never stopped—never gave me a chance —”

“Calm down,” I said.

He relaxed. His head dropped down on the pillow.

“Russ—” said Rita, in a voice that was husky with pain, “don’t carry on so. Please don’t —”

The doctor hurried back into the room. He motioned for us to get out quickly. I took my hat from the bed. Russ saw me turning to leave when he spoke.

“Wait!” he said. “Don’t go!” He sounded like the old Russ again; like the old Russ who had not been afraid to tell Johnson where to head in; who had turned the tables on Norm when Norm had taken him for a walk; whose word had been law among the niggers at the house-wrecking. What there was in the sound of his voice or the way he looked—I don’t know. All I know is that while he talked not one of us in that room moved a muscle or spoke.

“Bennie,” he said, “it’s spring now. I can feel the air from the lake. In a few days now the wild geese will be flying. It’s time to pull out. I know a place where there’s a bend in the river—where old trees—soft maples and elms—hang over the water. We’ll build us a cabin there with windows that swing open like doors—make a path winding up to a spring. When the first dandelions come we’ll be all ready to move in. We’ll get her and the kid—Little Bennie. They’ll come. We’ve got to go now—not wait for another depression—not wait for summer. I’ve got the outboard all packed. They can’t keep me here in this dump—on my back. I’ll be outa here —”

He stopped short and looked at our faces.

“What’s the matter?” he said. “You all look so dumb.”

Rita hurried out of the room sobbing loudly. The doctor followed and the nurse motioned for me to go, too. Russ made an effort to raise his head from the pillow.

“Bennie,” he said, “are you going with me? I want to know—now!”

“Sure, Russ,” I said. “You know me. I’ve always stuck with you—ain’t I?”

Then I went out, too, and the nurse closed the door.

THE END
